# PsyForge v1.0 — Volume I: Strategic Foundation

```
Document: PsyForge v1.0 — Volume I: Strategic Foundation
Volume: I
Specification Version: 1.0-draft
Status: Living Specification
Last Updated: August 7, 2026
Governing Law: WPAI Constitution; accepted canonical source frozen per AGENTS.md
Conformance: MUST, SHOULD, MAY (RFC 2119)
```

---

## Volume I Scope

Volume I establishes the strategic foundation of the PsyForge platform. It defines the vision, mission, philosophy, terminology, behavioral model, and ethics framework that govern all subsequent volumes. No other volume may contradict the principles, definitions, or requirements established here.

**Parts:**
- **I.1** Vision and Mission
- **I.2** Philosophy and Core Principles
- **I.3** Terminology and Glossary
- **I.4** Behavioral Model
- **I.5** Ethics Framework

**Normative Requirements in This Volume:** REQ-VIS-001 through REQ-VIS-029, REQ-PHI-001 through REQ-PHI-025, REQ-GLOS-001 through REQ-GLOS-007 (plus domain-specific requirement IDs in glossary entries), REQ-BEH-001 through REQ-BEH-090, REQ-ETH-001 through REQ-ETH-105.

**Cross-Volume References:** This volume is referenced by and references Volumes II through X and Appendices A through L. Forward references indicate dependencies that are specified in detail in later volumes.

## Part I.1 — Vision and Mission

**Status:** Normative (except where explicitly marked Informative)
**Governing Law:** [WPAI Constitution](https://github.com/MrWizard94-Compile/Agents-Constitution), frozen per AGENTS.md (CONST-COMPLETE-001, DOC-WHY-001, RND-DOC-001, IP-INVENTION-001)
**Scope:** This Part defines the vision, mission, problem, solution, and strategic differentiators of the PsyForge v1.0 platform. It is the originating anchor of the entire specification; every requirement, architectural decision, and acceptance criterion in Volumes II–X and the Appendices MUST trace back to a statement made here. See [Appendix G, Section G.1] for the Vision-to-Requirements traceability matrix and [Front Matter, Section FM.0.10.4] for the traceability chain.

---

### I.1.1 Vision Statement

#### I.1.1.1 Core Vision

PsyForge exists to close the behavioral intelligence gap in digital experience design. The core vision is a **behavioral reasoning engine that transforms raw human interaction into explainable models, predictive simulations, and evidence-backed design recommendations across digital experiences.** This is not a slogan; it is a normative statement of intent that constrains every downstream architectural choice. Where a conventional analytics product asks "what happened," and a conventional AI product asks "what will happen," PsyForge asks the third and harder question: "what should we change, why, and what will happen to whom if we do." Answering that question requires the four capabilities named in the vision statement — behavioral models, simulations, recommendations, and explainability — and the platform MUST NOT be scoped, marketed, or architected in a way that drops any one of them.

The vision is deliberately framed around *behavioral* reasoning rather than *behavioral analytics* or *behavioral AI*. Analytics describes the past; AI predicts the future; reasoning prescribes action under uncertainty with stated evidence. The distinction is load-bearing: it is the reason PsyForge MUST carry a causal inference framework (See [Volume III, Section III.7.2]), a multi-agent reasoning architecture (See [Volume V]), and an explainability layer that adapts to audience (See [Volume III, Section III.7.3]). A system that predicts without explaining, or that explains without citing evidence, fails the core vision regardless of its accuracy.

The phrase "across digital experiences" is the second load-bearing commitment. It positions PsyForge as domain-agnostic by construction: a single reasoning engine with a swappable psychology corpus (See [Volume III, Section III.2.13]) rather than a portfolio of vertical products. The initial deployment target is game studios, because games produce the richest, highest-frequency, most emotionally loaded behavioral telemetry of any digital medium and because the companion demonstration product, *Echoes of the Frontier*, gives PsyForge a self-controlled proving ground. But the engine MUST NOT embed game-specific assumptions into its core. Any capability that cannot be expressed without the word "game" belongs in a corpus domain, not in the engine.

**REQ-VIS-001:** The platform MUST deliver all four vision capabilities — behavioral modeling, predictive simulation, evidence-backed recommendation, and explainability — as first-class subsystems. A release that omits any one MUST NOT be designated a PsyForge release.

**REQ-VIS-002:** Core engine components (Feature Engine, Knowledge Graph, Reasoning Engine, Simulation Engine, Recommendation Engine, Ethics Layer) MUST NOT contain domain-specific logic; all domain knowledge MUST be expressed through the swappable corpus interface. See [Volume III, Section III.2.1].

The vision is grounded in engineering reality by three commitments stated once here and enforced throughout the specification. First, *nothing writes directly to production* — every system output flows through the recommendation gate and human review (See [Volume II, Section II.2.6]). Second, *ethics by architecture, not bolt-on* — manipulation risk is scored on every recommendation before it can be shown to a human, and no human override can bypass the audit trail (See [Volume I, Section I.5] and [Volume V, Section V.11]). Third, *local-first* — the platform MUST run on a single developer machine, including local model inference, because data residency and analytical self-sufficiency are product features, not deployment options (See [Volume II, Section II.5.5]). These three commitments are what make the vision defensible rather than aspirational: they convert "explainable behavioral reasoning" from a marketing claim into an enforceable architectural invariant.

#### I.1.1.2 Long-Term Aspiration (2032 Framing)

The core vision is realized against a 2032 horizon. By 2032, PsyForge aspires to be **a behavioral reasoning engine that transforms raw human interaction into explainable models, predictive simulations, and evidence-backed design recommendations across digital experiences** — operating at a scale and maturity that the v1.0 platform only sketches. The 2032 framing is not a roadmap date; it is a forcing function. It forces every present-day design decision to be evaluated against the question: "does this choice survive the next six years of growth in data volume, corpus diversity, regulatory pressure, and model capability, or does it become a refactor liability?" A design that is cheap to build now but structurally incompatible with the 2032 aspiration MUST be rejected in favor of one that costs more today but compounds.

Concretely, the 2032 aspiration decomposes into five measurable states. First, **scale**: the simulation engine MUST sustain 10 million concurrent synthetic personas running continuously through candidate design changes before any production patch ships, with 500 or more candidate updates evaluated per patch decision (See [Volume IV, Section IV.9.1]). This is the platform's competitive moat and the single hardest engineering target in the specification. Second, **breadth**: the swappable corpus MUST span at minimum game psychology, consumer marketing, education, workplace/HR, healthcare engagement, and financial behavior (See [Volume III, Section III.2.13.4]), with the engine unchanged across swaps. Third, **explanation depth**: every recommendation MUST ship with a causal-chain explanation, an evidence citation, and a confidence interval, not a black-box score (See [Volume III, Section III.7.3]). Fourth, **ethical enforceability**: the Ethics Layer MUST score every recommendation on seven dimensions — Retention Impact, Revenue Impact, Player Satisfaction, Fairness, Manipulation Risk, Long-Term Trust, Regulatory Risk — and no recommendation MAY proceed to human review without those scores attached (See [Volume I, Section I.5.2]). Fifth, **deployment sovereignty**: local-first, cloud, hybrid, and air-gapped deployments MUST all be first-class, because customers in regulated or sovereignty-sensitive industries will not accept a cloud-only behavioral reasoning platform (See [Volume II, Section II.5.1]).

The 2032 framing also defines what PsyForge aspires *not* to become. It MUST NOT become a general-purpose LLM, a generic BI tool, or an A/B testing framework sold standalone. Each of those is a component or a consumer of PsyForge, not the product. Conflating the engine with any one component would collapse the vision back into an analytics tool or an AI model, losing the reasoning-layer value that justifies the platform's existence.

**REQ-VIS-003:** Architecture decisions MUST be evaluated against 2032 scalability targets (10M-persona simulation, six-corpus breadth, causal explanation depth, seven-dimension ethics scoring, four deployment models). A decision that blocks any 2032 target MUST be documented as a known limit with a migration path, not silently accepted. See [Volume X, Section X.1.1].

The aspiration is reviewed against real outcomes on the cadence defined in I.1.1.3. It is normatively treated as a contract with the future, not a vision slide.

#### I.1.1.3 Vision Evolution and Reassessment Cadence

A vision that never changes is either already obsolete or never enforced. PsyForge treats the vision as a versioned artifact subject to scheduled reassessment and bounded change. The reassessment cadence is annual, synchronized with the strategic planning cycle, and produces a Vision Diff document — a structured record of what was proposed to change in the vision, what was accepted, what was rejected, and why. The Vision Diff is itself a normative artifact: it MUST be archived, MUST be traceable to the requirements it adds or retires, and MUST NOT be applied retroactively to invalidate past decisions without a formal deprecation record (See [Front Matter, Section FM.1.2]).

The cadence is layered. **Annual reassessment** re-examines the 2032 framing, market positioning, and corpus roadmap against observed adoption, competitive movement, and regulatory change. This is the floor; it MUST happen. **Biennial structural review** re-examines the four-capability definition (modeling, simulation, recommendation, explainability) and the three commitments (no-direct-production-write, ethics-by-architecture, local-first) for whether they remain jointly sufficient to deliver the vision. The structural review MAY propose retiring or adding a capability, but such a proposal MUST pass the change criteria in [Volume I, Section I.2.4.1] and survive a red-team critique before acceptance. **Event-triggered reassessment** fires on defined triggers — a material regulatory shift (e.g., a new AI Act implementing act), a corpus-market failure (a launched corpus that does not reach adoption threshold within 18 months), or a moat-erosion event (a competitor demonstrably replicating the simulation-at-scale capability). Event-triggered reassessment MUST complete within 60 days of trigger and MUST produce either an accepted Vision Diff or a documented decision not to change with reasons.

Change is bounded to prevent vision drift from becoming vision erosion. Three guardrails apply. First, the four capabilities are *foundational* and may be extended but not removed without a two-cycle deprecation — removing a capability that downstream architecture depends on requires the migration path to be specified, costed, and resourced before the deprecation is accepted. Second, the no-direct-production-write doctrine and ethics-by-architecture commitment are *invariant*: they MAY be strengthened but MUST NOT be weakened, and any Vision Diff that proposes weakening either MUST be escalated to the Ethics Review Board (See [Volume I, Section I.5.3]) and MUST require unanimous acceptance with a published rationale. Third, the corpus set is *expansive*: new corpora MAY be added under the standard corpus contribution process (See [Volume III, Section III.2.1.4]); existing corpora MAY be deprecated only after a defined sunset period and a data-portability guarantee for affected customers.

**REQ-VIS-004:** A Vision Diff MUST be produced at least annually and within 60 days of any event-triggered reassessment. Each diff entry MUST classify the change as Extension, Strengthening, Deprecation, or Invariant-Revision, and Invariant-Revision entries MUST carry an Ethics Review Board disposition.

This cadence exists because behavioral science, AI capability, and digital-experience regulation are all moving targets. A vision frozen in 2026 would be wrong by 2032 by construction. The cadence converts that risk into a managed process.

#### I.1.1.4 Vision Communication Strategy (Internal, External, Investor)

A vision that lives only in this specification has failed. The vision MUST be communicated in three distinct registers — internal, external, and investor — each with its own fidelity, constraints, and review path. These are not three paraphrases of the same sentence; they are three artifacts tuned to three audiences with three different decision rights over the platform.

**Internal communication** targets engineers, researchers, and product staff. Fidelity is highest here: the internal register MUST include the full vision statement, the 2032 aspiration, the four capabilities, the three commitments, and the normative requirements. The internal artifact is this specification itself plus a living one-page "Vision at a Glance" maintained alongside the Vision Diff (See [I.1.1.5]). The constraint is consistency: any internal slide, all-hands narrative, or onboarding document MUST NOT contradict the normative vision, and contradiction is treated as a documentation defect under DOC-SYNC-001. Internal communication SHOULD use engineering language (capabilities, invariants, requirements) rather than aspirational language, because the internal audience must be able to derive what they should and should not build from the vision.

**External communication** targets customers, prospects, and the broader practitioner community. Fidelity is selectively reduced: external materials MUST convey the four capabilities and the three commitments but MAY omit requirement IDs, internal numbering, and roadmap specifics. The constraint is *no overclaim*. External claims about prediction accuracy, simulation scale, or ethics enforcement MUST be backed by published benchmarks (See [Volume X, Section X.6]) and MUST use hedged language ("the platform scores every recommendation on seven dimensions" is acceptable; "the platform guarantees ethical outcomes" is not and MUST NOT be used). External communication MUST pass a review that checks every quantitative claim against the benchmark registry; a claim without a backing benchmark MUST NOT be published. The demonstration product *Echoes of the Frontier* serves as the primary external proof point: it is a real game shipping real telemetry through PsyForge, and case-study material derived from it is the preferred external artifact (See [Volume VIII, Section VIII.6.1.4]).

**Investor communication** targets current and prospective financial stakeholders. Fidelity is strategic: investor materials MUST convey the market thesis (behavioral intelligence gap), the moat (simulation at scale plus causal knowledge graph plus ethics-by-architecture), and the expansion path (game studios → adjacent corpora). The constraint is *verifiability*: any market-sizing, adoption, or moat claim made to investors MUST be reproducible from internal data, and the underlying assumptions MUST be documented in a Investor Claim Register maintained by the business architecture function (See [Volume VIII]). Investor materials MUST NOT imply capabilities beyond the 2032 aspiration's measurable states, because doing so creates a liability under emerging AI transparency regulation (See [Volume IX, Section IX.5.4]).

**REQ-VIS-005:** Every external and investor quantitative claim about PsyForge capabilities MUST cite a published benchmark entry. Claims without backing benchmarks MUST NOT be published.

The three registers share one invariant: none of them MAY describe PsyForge as a system that writes directly to production or as a system that optimizes without human oversight. Misrepresenting the human-in-the-loop and recommendation-gate commitments to any audience is a vision violation and MUST be corrected via the errata process (See [Front Matter, Section FM.1.5]).

#### I.1.1.5 Vision Artifacts and Narrative Materials

The vision is materialized as a curated set of artifacts, each with a defined owner, update cadence, and storage location. Artifacts exist so that the vision is quotable in the right register for the right decision without forcing a reader to derive it from the full specification. The artifact set is normative: the listed artifacts MUST exist and MUST be kept in sync with the normative vision, and any artifact that drifts from the specification is a documentation defect under DOC-SYNC-001.

The artifact inventory is as follows. The **Vision Statement Card** is a single-paragraph canonical statement owned by the platform lead, updated only via Vision Diff, and used as the authoritative citation in all three communication registers. The **2032 Aspiration Brief** is a two-page document owned by the strategy function that expands the five measurable states (scale, breadth, explanation depth, ethical enforceability, deployment sovereignty) into target ranges and current-status indicators; it is updated annually and on event-triggered reassessment. The **Vision at a Glance** one-pager (referenced in I.1.1.4) is owned by the documentation function and is the internal default citation; it MUST reflect the current Vision Diff within five working days of diff acceptance. The **Echoes of the Frontier Case Study** is owned by the demonstration-product team and is the primary external proof artifact; it MUST be updated whenever a PsyForge release meaningfully changes the demonstration product's measurable outcomes. The **Investor Claim Register** (referenced in I.1.1.4) is owned by the business architecture function and MUST record, for every quantitative claim made to investors, the source data and the benchmark entry that backs it. The **Vision Diff Archive** is the append-only record of all accepted vision changes and is the audit trail for vision evolution; it is owned by document governance (See [Front Matter, Section FM.1]).

Each artifact carries provenance metadata: owner, last-updated date, backing Vision Diff version, and a checksum over the normative claims it makes. Provenance is not decorative; it is what allows an auditor or a regulator to trace any external statement back to a specific version of the normative vision. Without provenance, a vision artifact is unverifiable narrative; with it, the artifact becomes evidence. This matters concretely under the EU AI Act's transparency and post-market-monitoring obligations (See [Volume IX, Section IX.5.4]), which require that claims made about an AI system's behavior be traceable to documented design choices.

Narrative materials — the storytelling layer over the artifacts — are constrained by the same overclaim prohibition as external communication (I.1.1.4). A narrative that dramatizes the vision (e.g., "10 million synthetic players stress-testing every patch before a single human sees it") is acceptable if and only if the underlying capability exists or is on the committed roadmap with a milestone (See [Volume X, Section X.2.2]). A narrative that invents capability not on the roadmap is a vision violation. The constraint is tight because behavioral-reasoning claims are exactly the kind of claim that erodes trust fastest when overclaimed: a customer who buys "explainable models" and receives a black-box score will not return, and a regulator who reads "ethics enforcement" and finds a manual checkbox will not be lenient.

**REQ-VIS-006:** Every vision artifact MUST carry provenance metadata (owner, last-updated, backing Vision Diff version, claim checksum). Artifacts MUST be regenerable from the normative specification, and any artifact whose claims cannot be regenerated MUST be flagged as defective.

The artifact set is intentionally small. A vision that requires a large library of materials to communicate is a vision that has not been distilled. The discipline of maintaining five artifacts, each with a single owner, is the discipline that keeps the vision quotable, auditable, and honest.

---

### I.1.2 Mission Statement

#### I.1.2.1 Formal Mission

The formal mission of PsyForge is to **equip every digital-experience team with a behavioral reasoning engine that converts observed human interaction into explainable models, predictive simulations, and evidence-backed recommendations — under enforceable ethical constraints and with a human decision-maker in every deployment loop.** The mission is operational, not aspirational: it states what the platform does, for whom, under what constraints, and with what governance. Where the vision (I.1.1) describes the future state the platform is built toward, the mission describes the present-day contract the platform makes with its users. Every shipped feature MUST be defensible as an execution of this mission; a feature that cannot be so defended is mission drift (See [I.1.2.4]).

The mission's four operative clauses each carry normative weight. *Equip every digital-experience team* commits the platform to usability across team sizes and skill levels — a tool usable only by PhD statisticians fails the mission — which is why the dashboard and Studio tools (See [Volume VII, Sections VII.2 and VII.4]) are first-class subsystems rather than afterthoughts, and why the local-first deployment (See [Volume II, Section II.5.5]) exists so that a solo indie developer can run the same engine an enterprise runs. *Converts observed interaction into explainable models* commits the platform to the behavioral stack (See [Volume I, Section I.4]) and to the explainability layer (See [Volume III, Section III.7.3]) — a model that predicts but cannot explain its prediction in a causal path is a mission failure even if accurate. *Predictive simulations* commits the platform to the simulation engine (See [Volume IV]) as a structural component, not an optional module — a release without simulation capability is not a PsyForge release (REQ-VIS-001). *Evidence-backed recommendations under enforceable ethical constraints* commits the platform to the recommendation gate (See [Volume II, Section II.2.6]) and the Ethics Layer (See [Volume I, Section I.5]) as non-negotiable — a recommendation that bypasses ethics scoring or that writes directly to production violates the mission structurally.

The mission is bounded by the product hierarchy. WPAI is the parent brand and studio; PsyForge is the platform; *Echoes of the Frontier* is the demonstration product that proves the platform on a real shipping game; the PsyForge SDK is the integration toolkit that lets external products emit conformant telemetry (See [Volume VI, Section VI.2]); PsyForge Cloud is the enterprise deployment for customers who prefer managed infrastructure (See [Volume II, Section II.5.1.2]); and PsyForge Research is the function that maintains and extends the behavioral corpus (See [Volume III, Section III.2]). The mission applies to the platform and its direct derivatives; it does not require that *Echoes of the Frontier* itself be a commercial hit, only that it be a faithful demonstration of the platform's capabilities. This separation is important: tying the platform's mission to a single game's commercial success would create a perverse incentive to tune the platform to that game rather than to keep it domain-agnostic.

**REQ-VIS-007:** The platform MUST execute all four mission clauses (equip, convert-to-explainable-models, simulate-predictively, recommend-under-ethics) in every supported deployment model. A deployment model that cannot execute all four is incomplete and MUST be labeled as such in its capability matrix.

The mission is reviewed for fitness on the same cadence as the vision (I.1.1.3) but under a stricter change bar: the four operative clauses are *foundational* and may be sharpened but not removed without a two-cycle deprecation. The mission is the bridge between the inspiring vision and the implementable requirements; if the mission moves, the requirements move, and the cost of that movement is paid by every downstream volume.

#### I.1.2.2 Mission Scope Boundaries

A mission without boundaries expands until it is indistinguishable from "build software." PsyForge's mission is bounded on five axes, and each boundary is a normative constraint that prevents scope creep into adjacent product categories. Stating boundaries explicitly is more valuable than stating ambitions, because the boundary violations are what actually kill platforms: every failed analytics company died not from lacking vision but from absorbing the adjacent product category until it could not maintain any of them.

The five scope boundaries are as follows. **Boundary 1 — Reasoning, not generation.** PsyForge reasons about behavior and recommends changes; it does not generate creative content (assets, levels, dialogue, marketing copy) as a core function. A narrative-designer agent (See [Volume V, Section V.9]) may recommend narrative changes and explain their likely emotional impact, but the platform MUST NOT become a generative content engine. Generative capability, where present, is a tool the agent invokes, not a product surface. **Boundary 2 — Recommendation, not deployment.** PsyForge outputs recommendations; it does not deploy changes to the customer's production system. The no-direct-production-write doctrine (See [Volume II, Section II.2.6.1]) is both an ethical and a scope boundary: PsyForge is an advisor, not an operator. A deployment integration MAY push an approved recommendation to a customer's CI/CD system, but only after human approval and only as a documented, auditable action. **Boundary 3 — Behavioral, not infrastructural.** PsyForge models behavior, emotion, motivation, and outcome; it does not model server load, network latency, or infrastructure cost. Operational telemetry that is behavioral (e.g., session abandonment after a crash) is in scope; operational telemetry that is purely infrastructural (e.g., p95 API latency) is out of scope and belongs in the customer's existing observability stack. **Boundary 4 — Engine, not corpus publisher.** PsyForge ships and maintains corpora, but it does not claim to be the authoritative publisher of behavioral science. Corpus content MUST cite primary sources (See [Volume III, Section III.2.12.4] and Appendix I), and contested findings MUST be marked with confidence and contestation rather than asserted as settled. **Boundary 5 — Platform, not single-vertical SaaS.** PsyForge is an engine with swappable corpus; it MUST NOT hardcode a single vertical's assumptions into the engine. A feature that cannot be expressed without referencing a specific vertical belongs in a corpus or plugin (See [Volume VII, Section VII.6]), not in the engine.

These boundaries are enforced at three points. At *design time*, every feature RFC (See Appendix J) MUST state which mission clause it executes and MUST be checked against the five boundaries; an RFC that crosses a boundary MUST be re-scoped or rejected. At *build time*, the principle-compliance CI checks (See [Volume I, Section I.2.3.2]) include boundary checks — for example, a code change that introduces a generative content surface in the engine MUST fail CI. At *review time*, the annual Vision Diff (I.1.1.3) re-examines the boundaries themselves, because boundaries that are never questioned become dogma.

**REQ-VIS-008:** Every feature RFC MUST declare its executing mission clause and MUST be evaluated against the five scope boundaries. RFCs crossing a boundary MUST be rejected or re-scoped to a corpus or plugin.

The boundaries are not limitations on ambition; they are the mechanism by which the platform remains coherent at scale. A platform that tries to be a reasoning engine, a content generator, a deployment system, an infrastructural monitor, a corpus publisher, and a vertical SaaS simultaneously will be none of them. The boundaries convert "be a behavioral reasoning engine" from a slogan into an enforceable contract.

#### I.1.2.3 Mission Alignment Criteria for New Initiatives

New initiatives — features, corpora, agents, plugins, partnerships, market expansions — MUST pass a mission-alignment gate before receiving resources. The gate exists because the most expensive form of waste is a well-executed initiative that was never in mission. The alignment criteria are structured as a five-question filter; an initiative MUST answer all five, and any "no" or "uncertain" answer triggers either re-scoping or rejection. The filter is normative: bypassing it to start an initiative is a process violation reportable under document governance (See [Front Matter, Section FM.1]).

The five alignment criteria are as follows. **Criterion 1 — Mission clause execution.** Which of the four mission clauses (equip, convert-to-explainable-models, simulate-predictively, recommend-under-ethics) does the initiative advance? An initiative that advances none is out of mission regardless of its other merits. Example: a proposed generative-dialogue feature for *Echoes of the Frontier* advances none of the four clauses and would be rejected; the same feature reframed as a narrative-agent capability that *recommends* dialogue changes and simulates their emotional impact advances the simulate and recommend clauses and passes. **Criterion 2 — Boundary compliance.** Does the initiative stay within all five scope boundaries (I.1.2.2)? An initiative that crosses a boundary MUST be re-scoped to a corpus or plugin or rejected. Example: a proposed infrastructure-monitoring feature crosses Boundary 3 and is rejected; the same insight reframed as a behavioral signal ("players abandon sessions after crashes at rate X") stays in scope. **Criterion 3 — Engine neutrality.** If the initiative adds capability to the engine (as opposed to a corpus or plugin), can it be expressed without referencing a specific vertical? If not, it MUST be relocated to a corpus or plugin. Example: a proposed "loot box optimization" engine feature is vertical-specific and belongs in the game psychology corpus; a proposed "variable-reward-schedule sensitivity" feature is a behavioral construct and may live in the engine. **Criterion 4 — Ethics compatibility.** Can the initiative be made to pass the seven-dimension ethics scoring (See [Volume I, Section I.5.2])? An initiative whose purpose is to increase manipulation capacity — e.g., a feature whose only use is to exploit sunk-cost fallacy without disclosure — fails this criterion and MUST be rejected even if commercially attractive. **Criterion 5 — Deployment sovereignty.** Does the initiative work in all supported deployment models, or does it create a cloud-only dependency? An initiative that requires cloud-only infrastructure (e.g., a feature depending on a proprietary cloud model) MAY be accepted only if it is clearly labeled as a Cloud-tier capability and a local-first fallback or degradation path is specified (See [Volume II, Section II.5.1]).

The filter is applied by the architecture function with input from the ethics and business functions. The output of the filter is a Mission Alignment Record attached to the initiative's RFC, classifying it as Aligned, Re-scoped, or Rejected, with reasons. Records are archived and form a dataset for the mission-drift detection process (I.1.2.4): a pattern of rejections for the same boundary violation indicates either a misread mission or a mission that should evolve.

**REQ-VIS-009:** No initiative MAY receive engineering resources without a recorded Mission Alignment Record classifying it as Aligned or Re-scoped. Rejected initiatives MUST NOT be restarted under a different name without addressing the rejection reasons.

The filter is deliberately conservative because behavioral-reasoning platforms carry asymmetric downside: a feature that subtly enables manipulation, even unintentionally, can destroy the trust capital that took years to build. The cost of a false rejection (a good idea not built) is recoverable; the cost of a false acceptance (a bad idea shipped) may not be.

#### I.1.2.4 Mission Drift Detection and Correction

Mission drift is the gradual, often unintentional, expansion of a platform's scope away from its stated mission. It is the single most common failure mode for platforms that survive their first product-market fit, because success generates requests that each look reasonable in isolation and only collectively constitute drift. PsyForge treats drift as a measurable, monitored condition, not a vague cultural risk. Detection is automated where possible, human-reviewed always, and correction is a documented process with escalation paths.

Drift is detected through five signals, each with a defined measurement method. **Signal 1 — RFC boundary-violation rate.** The proportion of submitted RFCs that cross a scope boundary (I.1.2.2) is tracked over rolling quarters. A rising rate indicates either a misread mission or pressure to expand scope; either condition requires intervention. The threshold for action is a sustained quarter-over-quarter increase; a single spike is investigated but not acted on without a trend. **Signal 2 — Feature-to-mission-clause coverage.** Each shipped feature is tagged with the mission clause it executes (REQ-VIS-008). A release in which more than a threshold proportion of features cannot be tagged to a clause indicates drift toward out-of-mission work. **Signal 3 — Corpus-vs-engine code ratio.** Code that should live in a corpus or plugin but is merged into the engine is direct evidence of Boundary 5 erosion. A periodic audit (See [Volume I, Section I.2.3.4]) flags engine modules containing vertical-specific logic; any finding is a drift signal. **Signal 4 — Customer-requested capabilities not on roadmap.** A rising volume of customer requests for capabilities outside the mission (e.g., content generation, infrastructural monitoring) indicates either unmet adjacent needs that justify a mission evolution conversation or pressure that, if acceded to, would constitute drift. The requests are logged and reviewed; a pattern may trigger an event-driven Vision Diff (I.1.1.3). **Signal 5 — Ethics-score erosion.** A trend toward recommendations with weaker ethics scores being approved, or toward human overrides of ethics vetoes (See [Volume V, Section V.11.5]) becoming more frequent, indicates drift toward manipulation-adjacent capability and is treated as a high-severity signal requiring immediate review.

Correction proceeds in three tiers. **Tier 1 — Documentation correction.** If drift is detected at the communication layer (an external artifact overclaims, an internal slide misstates), correction is via the errata process (See [Front Matter, Section FM.1.5]) and re-issuance of the artifact. **Tier 2 — Scope correction.** If drift is detected in shipped or in-flight features (code in the engine that should be in a corpus, a feature that crossed a boundary), correction is via re-scoping: the capability is relocated to a corpus or plugin, or deprecated under the two-cycle deprecation rule. Tier 2 corrections MUST be tracked to completion with owners and dates. **Tier 3 — Mission revision.** If drift signals persistently indicate that the mission itself is miscalibrated to market reality — for example, if a large majority of high-value customer requests consistently fall outside the mission — the response is not to silently absorb the requests but to formally consider a mission revision under the cadence in I.1.1.3. Tier 3 is rare and is treated as a structural change requiring Ethics Review Board consultation.

**REQ-VIS-010:** Drift signals MUST be reviewed at least quarterly. Any sustained drift signal MUST produce a documented correction plan with owner, tier (1–3), and target completion date, archived under document governance.

The discipline of drift detection is what converts the mission from a statement into a constraint. A mission that is never measured against is a decorative sentence; a mission that is measured, reviewed, and corrected against is the operating contract of the platform. PsyForge chooses the latter.

---

### I.1.3 Problem Statement

#### I.1.3.1 The Behavioral Intelligence Gap

The behavioral intelligence gap is the structural inability of digital-experience teams to reason from observed behavior to defensible design action. Teams collect vast quantities of behavioral data — every click, dwell, purchase, abandonment, and session is logged — yet they cannot, in general, answer the question "why did this player leave, and what change would have kept them, and what would that change cost the cohort that didn't leave." The data exists; the reasoning does not. This gap is not a tooling gap (better dashboards do not close it) and not a data gap (more events do not close it); it is a reasoning gap, and closing it requires a system that builds causal models, runs predictive simulations, and produces evidence-backed recommendations. That is the problem PsyForge is built to solve.

The gap has three dimensions that make it refractory to incremental improvement. First, **the description-prediction-prescription gap.** Existing tools describe ("retention dropped 4% last week"), some predict ("retention will drop further if trend holds"), almost none prescribe ("change the onboarding third step's reward schedule and retention recovers 2.5% with a 0.3% revenue trade-off, here is the causal chain and the confidence interval"). The prescription layer is missing because it requires causal reasoning, not just correlation, and because it requires simulating counterfactuals, not just observing history. PsyForge's behavioral stack (See [Volume I, Section I.4]) and simulation engine (See [Volume IV]) are the structural responses. Second, **the aggregation-individuality gap.** Existing tools aggregate ("the average player churns in 14 days") but design decisions act on individuals and segments ("this change helps the average player but disproportionately frustrates explorers"). Averages hide distributional effects, and distributional effects are where design decisions succeed or fail. PsyForge's player modeling (See [Volume III, Section III.5]) and disproportionate-impact analysis (See [Volume IV, Section IV.5.2.4]) are the structural responses. Third, **the insight-action gap.** Even when an insight is correctly identified, the path from insight to deployed change is uninstrumented: there is no record of what was recommended, what was approved, what was deployed, and what the outcome was, so the team cannot learn whether its reasoning was correct. PsyForge's recommendation lifecycle (See [Volume II, Section II.2.6.2]) and outcome measurement (See [Volume II, Section II.2.1.12]) close this loop.

The gap is widening, not narrowing, for two reasons. The volume and granularity of behavioral data are growing faster than the analytical capacity to reason about it, so teams drown in events they cannot interpret. And the regulatory environment is demanding explainability (See [Volume IX, Section IX.5.4]) for exactly the kinds of behavioral modeling that PsyForge performs, so the cost of *not* having an explainable reasoning layer is rising from "suboptimal" to "non-compliant." A platform that closes the behavioral intelligence gap is therefore not a nice-to-have; it is becoming a regulatory and commercial necessity.

**REQ-VIS-011:** The platform MUST close the description-prediction-prescription gap by producing, for every recommendation, a causal-chain explanation, a confidence interval, and a simulated counterfactual outcome. A recommendation without these three elements MUST NOT be presented as a PsyForge recommendation.

The behavioral intelligence gap is the root problem from which the more specific problems in I.1.3.2 through I.1.3.7 derive. Each subsequent subsection documents a facet of the gap as it manifests in a specific tool, cost, or stakeholder, and each is closed by a specific capability named elsewhere in this specification.

#### I.1.3.2 Limitations of Current Analytics Platforms

Current analytics platforms — the general-purpose BI tools, product-analytics suites, and game-analytics SaaS products that dominate the market — are, without exception, description tools. They answer "what happened" with filters, funnels, and cohorts. They are highly optimized for that question and genuinely useful within its scope. Their limitations become load-bearing problems when a team expects them to answer "why" and "what should we change," because the platforms lack the three capabilities required to answer those questions: behavioral feature extraction, causal modeling, and counterfactual simulation.

The first limitation is **the raw-event ceiling.** Current platforms surface raw events or simple aggregations of them (event counts, funnels, retention curves). They do not extract behavioral features — higher-level signals like Average Decision Time, Exploration Preference, Risk Tolerance, Recovery From Failure, or Habitual-vs-Exploratory Ratio — because feature extraction requires a domain-aware engine that knows which event combinations constitute a behavioral construct (See [Volume III, Section III.3]). Without features, teams reason about events directly, which is like reasoning about pixels instead of images: technically possible, cognitively prohibitive. The result is that teams build ad-hoc, non-reusable, non-validatable SQL queries to approximate features, and the features so derived are not comparable across teams, games, or time. PsyForge's Behavioral Feature Engine (See [Volume III, Section III.3]) and its feature catalog (See [Volume III, Section III.3.2]) are the structural response: features are first-class, versioned, validated artifacts, and they constitute a major IP opportunity (See [I.1.5.1]).

The second limitation is **the correlation ceiling.** Current platforms compute correlations (players who do X also tend to do Y) but cannot establish causation (doing X causes Y), and they cannot distinguish a cause from a common confounder. A platform that tells a team "players who craft more retain longer" invites the team to push crafting, but if the true cause is a third variable (e.g., engagement breadth) that drives both, pushing crafting will not improve retention and may waste a release. Causal inference requires a causal graph, confounder control, and counterfactual reasoning (See [Volume III, Section III.7.2]), none of which current analytics platforms provide. PsyForge's Knowledge Graph with causal edges (See [Volume III, Section III.1.3.8]) and do-calculus framework (See [Volume III, Section III.7.2.2]) are the structural response.

The third limitation is **the no-counterfactual ceiling.** Current platforms observe what happened; they cannot simulate what would have happened under a different design. A team considering a change must either ship it and observe (reactive design, with its costs documented in I.1.3.4) or rely on intuition. Neither is adequate. Simulation requires virtual personas derived from real player models (See [Volume IV, Section IV.2]), an economy and social model (See [Volume IV, Sections IV.3 and IV.4]), and the compute to run millions of personas through candidate changes (See [Volume IV, Section IV.9]). This is exactly the capability PsyForge's simulation engine provides and exactly the capability no current analytics platform provides, because simulation is a different product category than analytics, requiring different infrastructure, different modeling, and different validation.

**REQ-VIS-012:** The platform MUST NOT be positioned as an analytics platform or a BI tool. Marketing, sales, and documentation materials MUST distinguish PsyForge's reasoning capability from descriptive analytics, because conflating the two sets false expectations and undermines the platform's value proposition. See [Volume VIII, Section VIII.6.3].

Current analytics platforms are not competitors to PsyForge; they are upstream inputs and downstream consumers. A team will continue to use its BI tool for descriptive questions and will use PsyForge for the reasoning questions the BI tool cannot answer. The relationship is complementary, and PsyForge's data integration layer (See [Volume VI, Section VI.5]) is designed to consume from and export to existing analytics stacks rather than replace them.

#### I.1.3.3 Limitations of Current Game Telemetry

Game telemetry is, in principle, the richest behavioral data source in any digital medium: games produce high-frequency, emotionally loaded, goal-directed interaction at scale. In practice, current game telemetry is structured to answer operational questions (is the server up, is the economy inflating, are crashes spiking) rather than behavioral questions (is frustration rising, are explorers disengaging, is the difficulty curve failing the competence-seeking cohort). The mismatch between what telemetry collects and what behavioral reasoning requires is itself a problem PsyForge must address, both by accepting what studios currently emit and by guiding studios toward richer emission through the SDK (See [Volume VI, Section VI.2]).

The first limitation is **event schema poverty.** Most studios log a small set of high-level events (login, purchase, level-complete, session-end) plus a long tail of ad-hoc events added per-feature without a coherent schema. There is no standardized behavioral event taxonomy, so two studios' "engagement" events are not comparable, and a single studio's events drift in meaning as the game evolves. PsyForge's Event Schema Catalog (See [Volume VI, Section VI.1.2.2]) and normalization layer (See [Volume VI, Section VI.1.3]) address this by providing a canonical event taxonomy and a mapping pipeline that converts heterogeneous studio events into normalized behavioral events. The normalization is not optional: without it, the Feature Engine cannot reliably extract features across studios or across a single studio's versions.

The second limitation is **context loss.** A telemetry event in isolation is often uninterpretable: a "purchase" event is meaningless without the session context (was the player in a flow state or a frustrated state?), the economic context (was the currency inflated?), and the social context (was the purchase socially motivated?). Current telemetry typically logs the event and a thin envelope but discards the rich context that behavioral reasoning requires. PsyForge's interaction context capture (See [Volume I, Section I.4.2.3]) and enrichment pipeline (See [Volume VI, Section VI.1.3.6]) reconstruct context from correlated events, but reconstruction is lossy, and the platform SHOULD guide studios toward emitting richer context at source via SDK conventions (See [Volume VI, Section VI.2.6]).

The third limitation is **identity fragmentation.** A player who plays across platforms (PC, console, mobile), across titles, and across sessions is often represented as multiple disjoint identities, defeating longitudinal behavioral modeling. Cross-game identity is gated by privacy and consent (See [Volume VI, Section VI.3.1.4]), but within a single title and within consented scope, identity resolution is a prerequisite for player modeling. PsyForge's identity model (See [Volume VI, Section VI.3.1]) and pseudonymization strategy (See [Volume VI, Section VI.3.1.2]) address this within privacy constraints.

The fourth limitation is **no emotional or motivational signal.** Current telemetry records what a player did, never why, and never how they felt. Emotion and motivation are inferred, not measured, and the inference requires a behavioral stack (See [Volume I, Section I.4]) that maps interaction to behavior to emotion to motivation. Studios that try to infer emotion from raw events directly produce unreliable conclusions because the mapping is multi-step and confounded. PsyForge provides the stack; studios that lack it cannot close this gap no matter how much telemetry they collect.

**REQ-VIS-013:** The SDK MUST accept current studio telemetry as input (via normalization) AND MUST guide studios toward richer, context-bearing, schema-conformant emission over time. The platform MUST NOT require studios to have perfect telemetry before deriving value, because doing so would exclude the entire current market.

The telemetry problem is real but tractable. PsyForge's design choice is to meet studios where they are (accepting noisy, sparse, context-poor telemetry via normalization) while creating the conditions for them to improve over time (the SDK and schema registry make richer emission cheap and validated). This dual posture — accept-and-improve — is why the normalization layer is a first-class architectural component rather than a one-time migration script.

#### I.1.3.4 Cost of Reactive vs. Predictive Design

The dominant design methodology in digital-experience teams today is reactive: ship a change, observe its effect, react to the observation. This methodology is expensive, slow, and ethically risky, and the cost compounds with the scale of the player base. The problem PsyForge addresses is not that reactive design is wrong — observation is indispensable — but that reactive design as the *primary* methodology is structurally inferior to predictive design, and the gap is widening as player bases and design complexity grow.

The cost of reactive design has four components. First, **the shipping cost.** Every shipped change consumes a release cycle, a QA cycle, a deployment cycle, and a communication cycle. When a change is wrong, all of these costs are sunk, and the corrective change incurs them again. At a studio shipping monthly, a single wrong change costs two months of opportunity; at a studio shipping weekly, the same error costs two weeks but recurs more often. Second, **the player cost.** A wrong change that ships to production affects real players in real time. A difficulty change that frustrates a cohort, an economy change that inflates a currency, a monetization change that reads as manipulative — each harms real people and the trust relationship between studio and player. Reactive design treats players as the test population by default; predictive design treats simulation as the test population and reserves production for validated changes. Third, **the attribution cost.** When a shipped change produces an unexpected outcome, the team must attribute the outcome to a cause, and attribution from observation alone is confounded (See [I.1.3.2]). The team may conclude the change was wrong when in fact the change was right and a confounder moved, or vice versa, and the wrong lesson is learned and carried forward. Fourth, **the ethics cost.** A reactive monetization change that increases short-term revenue by exploiting a bias is indistinguishable from an ethical change in the observation window, because the harm manifests later and is not attributable. Predictive design with ethics scoring (See [Volume I, Section I.5.2]) flags manipulation-adjacent changes before they ship; reactive design discovers them after the harm.

Predictive design — simulating candidate changes against millions of virtual personas before shipping one — inverts the cost structure. The shipping cost is paid only for changes that survived simulation; the player cost is borne by synthetic personas, not humans; the attribution cost is paid in simulation where counterfactuals are observable; and the ethics cost is paid at scoring time, before any human is affected. The simulation engine's target of 10 million synthetic personas running through 500 candidate updates per patch decision (See [Volume IV, Section IV.9.1]) is the quantitative expression of this inversion: instead of shipping 1 change and observing, the team simulates 500 and ships the best-validated 1. The cost ratio is not 500:1 in compute (simulation is cheaper than production at scale); it is the difference between a methodology that learns from harm and one that prevents it.

**REQ-VIS-014:** The platform MUST enable predictive design as the default methodology: every candidate change surfaced as a recommendation MUST have been simulated before presentation, and the simulation results MUST accompany the recommendation. Reactive design (observe-then-react) remains available but MUST NOT be the only path, because doing so would perpetuate the cost structure PsyForge exists to eliminate.

The cost argument is not merely efficiency; it is ethics. A studio that can predict harm and chooses not to is in a different ethical position than a studio that cannot predict harm. PsyForge's position is that, once predictive capability exists, the choice not to use it before shipping changes that affect real people is increasingly indefensible, and the regulatory environment is moving toward requiring exactly this kind of pre-deployment evaluation for high-impact AI-influenced systems (See [Volume IX, Section IX.5.4]).

#### I.1.3.5 The Explainability Deficit in AI Systems

The explainability deficit is the gap between what an AI system predicts and what its operators can justify. A model that predicts "this player will churn in 7 days" with 92% accuracy is, in isolation, useless for design: the team cannot act on a prediction it cannot understand, cannot defend a decision based on a black box, and cannot learn from a model that does not expose its reasoning. The deficit is acute for behavioral AI because behavioral predictions inform decisions that affect real people — pricing, difficulty, content gating, monetization — and decisions that affect real people require justifications that survive scrutiny by players, regulators, and the studio's own ethics function.

The deficit has three sources. First, **model opacity.** Deep models predict without exposing intermediate reasoning; even interpretable models (logistic regression, decision trees) expose weights that are not causal and not actionable (a high weight on "session count" does not tell the team what to change). Second, **correlation-as-causation.** A model trained on observational data learns correlations, and acting on correlations as if they were causes produces interventions that fail or backfire (See [I.1.3.2]). Third, **audience mismatch.** An explanation suitable for a data scientist (SHAP values over a feature vector) is unsuitable for a designer ("the player feels under-skilled because the difficulty curve outpaces their mastery progression"), and an explanation suitable for a designer is unsuitable for an executive ("this change lifts 30-day retention by an estimated 2.4% with a 95% CI of [1.1%, 3.7%]"). Current systems produce one explanation or none; PsyForge must produce audience-adapted explanations (See [Volume III, Section III.7.3.6]).

PsyForge's response is structural, not cosmetic. The explainability layer (See [Volume III, Section III.7.3]) is a first-class subsystem that every recommendation passes through before presentation. It produces, for each recommendation, a feature attribution (which behavioral features drove the prediction), a graph-path explanation (the causal chain from player behavior through emotion and motivation to outcome), a natural-language explanation adapted to the requesting audience, and a confidence and uncertainty statement. The explanation is not generated post-hoc to justify a fixed prediction; it is co-produced with the prediction because the causal graph that produces the prediction is the same graph that explains it. This is the architectural meaning of "explainable by construction": the system reasons in a representation whose structure is itself the explanation, rather than reasoning in a black box and then reconstructing an explanation.

**REQ-VIS-015:** Every recommendation MUST ship with a four-part explanation: feature attribution, causal-chain path, audience-adapted natural-language summary, and confidence interval. A recommendation lacking any of the four MUST NOT be presented as a PsyForge recommendation and MUST NOT pass the recommendation gate. See [Volume II, Section II.2.6].

The explainability deficit is also a regulatory problem, not just a design problem. The EU AI Act (See [Volume IX, Section IX.5.4]) imposes transparency obligations on AI systems that influence decisions about people; emerging regulation in other jurisdictions is converging on similar requirements. A behavioral reasoning platform that cannot explain its recommendations is not merely suboptimal; it is, increasingly, non-compliant. PsyForge's explainability layer is therefore both a product feature and a compliance mechanism, and its presence is a strategic differentiator (See [I.1.5.5]).

#### I.1.3.6 Market Pain Points by Stakeholder Role

The behavioral intelligence gap manifests differently to each stakeholder role inside a digital-experience organization, and a platform that does not address role-specific pain will be bought by one role and abandoned by the others. This subsection enumerates the pain points by role; each is closed by a specific capability, and each role is a first-class user of the platform with role-specific interfaces (See [Volume VII, Section VII.1.2]).

**Game Designer.** The designer's pain is that design intuition is untestable before shipping. A designer believes a difficulty curve change will improve engagement; there is no way to test the belief cheaply, so the change ships on faith and is judged by aggregate metrics that may not reflect the designer's intent. PsyForge closes this with the Simulation Studio (See [Volume VII, Section VII.4.1]) and the Game Designer Agent (See [Volume V, Section V.8]): the designer proposes a change, the platform simulates it against the persona population, and the designer sees the predicted outcome by cohort before shipping. The designer's second pain is that post-hoc analysis is too slow to inform the next iteration; PsyForge's continuous simulation and recommendation pipeline shortens the loop.

**Producer / Executive.** The executive's pain is that decisions about roadmap, monetization, and live-ops are made under uncertainty without quantified trade-offs. "Should we ship the expansion in Q3 or Q4?" is answered by intuition, not by simulation. PsyForge closes this with forecasting (See [Volume IV, Section IV.5]), the Executive Overview dashboard (See [Volume VII, Section VII.2.2.1]), and audience-adapted explanations (See [Volume III, Section III.7.3.6]): the executive sees quantified predictions with confidence intervals and trade-off analysis, not opinions. The executive's second pain is reputational and regulatory risk from behavioral modeling; the Ethics Layer (See [Volume I, Section I.5]) and audit trail (See [Volume IX, Section IX.4]) convert this risk into a managed, demonstrable process.

**Data Analyst.** The analyst's pain is that the team expects behavioral insight from raw events, and the analyst must produce it with SQL and intuition, producing non-reusable, non-validatable artifacts. PsyForge closes this with the Feature Engine (See [Volume III, Section III.3]) and the Knowledge Graph Explorer (See [Volume VII, Section VII.2.2.9]): the analyst works with validated features and a queryable causal graph rather than reconstructing both from raw events per question.

**Economy / Monetization Lead.** This role's pain is that economy changes are high-stakes and slow to validate: an inflationary mistake takes weeks to surface and months to correct. PsyForge closes this with the Economy Simulation (See [Volume IV, Section IV.3]), the Economy Designer Agent (See [Volume V, Section V.7]), and the Monetization Advisor Agent (See [Volume V, Section V.10]), with the explicit constraint that monetization recommendations are ethics-scored before presentation (See [Volume V, Section V.10.1.5]).

**Ethics / Compliance Officer.** This role's pain is that behavioral modeling is opaque to oversight: there is no way to verify that a monetization change is not manipulative, no way to audit which recommendations were made and on what basis, no way to protect vulnerable populations systematically. PsyForge closes this with the Ethics Officer Agent (See [Volume V, Section V.11]), the seven-dimension scoring (See [Volume I, Section I.5.2]), the audit trail (See [Volume IX, Section IX.4]), and the vulnerable-population protections (See [Volume I, Section I.5.1.4]). Critically, the Ethics Officer Agent has veto authority (See [Volume V, Section V.11.5]) that cannot be bypassed without a fully-audited human override — oversight is structural, not advisory.

**Engineer / Integrator.** This role's pain is that integrating a behavioral platform must not slow the game, must not break privacy, and must not lock the studio into a cloud. PsyForge closes this with the local-first SDK (See [Volume VI, Section VI.2]), the minimal-performance-impact principle (See [Volume VI, Section VI.2.1.2]), and the air-gapped deployment model (See [Volume II, Section II.5.1.4] and [Volume IX, Section IX.6]).

**REQ-VIS-016:** The platform MUST provide role-specific interfaces and capability access for at least the six roles above. A role that lacks a first-class interface will receive degraded utility and MUST be documented as a known limitation with a remediation plan.

Each role's pain is real and each is independently sufficient to justify the platform. The platform's value proposition is that it addresses all of them with one coherent engine rather than six disconnected tools, and that coherence is itself a differentiator because the roles' decisions interlock: a designer's change affects the economy the monetization lead cares about, the ethics officer must approve, and the executive must fund. A platform that serves only one role leaves the interlock unmanaged.

#### I.1.3.7 Evidence and Research Supporting the Problem

The problem statement is not asserted from intuition; it is grounded in convergent evidence from academic research, industry practice, and regulatory direction. This subsection summarizes the evidence base and points to the corpus bibliography (Appendix I) for primary sources. The purpose of citing evidence here is not rhetorical; it is to establish that the problem is real enough and durable enough to justify building a multi-year platform against it, and to anchor the corpus (See [Volume III, Section III.2]) in validated findings rather than folklore.

The **behavioral science evidence** establishes that human interaction in digital environments is structured, inferable, and predictive of downstream outcomes. Self-Determination Theory (See [Volume III, Section III.2.4.1]) establishes that autonomy, competence, and relatedness are foundational motivations whose satisfaction predicts engagement and retention; the Hook Model (See [Volume III, Section III.2.4.4]) establishes that habit formation follows a measurable cue-routine-reward cycle; Flow Theory (See [Volume III, Section III.2.2.2]) establishes that engagement peaks when challenge and skill are matched within a measurable channel. These findings are not curiosities; they are the basis on which the behavioral stack (See [Volume I, Section I.4]) is constructed, and they justify the claim that emotion and motivation are inferable from behavior rather than requiring self-report. The corpus MUST cite the primary research (Appendix I) and MUST mark contested or replication-failed findings with confidence indicators (See [Volume III, Section III.2.12.4]).

The **behavioral economics evidence** establishes that decision-making in digital environments is systematically biased and that these biases are exploitable, which is both an opportunity (better design) and a risk (manipulation). Prospect theory and loss aversion (See [Volume III, Section III.2.3.1]), hyperbolic discounting (See [Volume III, Section III.2.3.6]), and variable reward schedules (See [Volume III, Section III.2.3.8]) are robust, replicated findings that explain why certain monetization patterns are effective and why they are ethically hazardous. This evidence base is the empirical foundation for the Ethics Layer's Manipulation Risk and Long-Term Trust dimensions (See [Volume I, Section I.5.2.1]): the scoring is not a moral opinion but a quantification of well-documented mechanisms.

The **industry evidence** establishes that studios and digital-experience teams currently operate without the reasoning layer PsyForge provides. Public post-mortems from live-service games routinely cite economy imbalances discovered only after shipping, difficulty curves that frustrated cohorts invisibly, and monetization changes that read as exploitative and damaged trust. The pattern across these post-mortems is consistent: the harm was predictable in principle but unpreventable in practice because the team lacked the simulation and causal-reasoning capability to predict it. This is direct evidence of the behavioral intelligence gap (I.1.3.1) as a live, costly problem, not a theoretical one.

The **regulatory evidence** establishes that the explainability and oversight gap is becoming a legal requirement, not just a best practice. The EU AI Act (See [Volume IX, Section IX.5.4]) classifies AI systems that influence decisions about people by risk tier and imposes transparency, human-oversight, and post-market-monitoring obligations on higher tiers; GDPR (See [Volume IX, Section IX.5.1]) imposes data-subject rights and purpose-limitation that constrain behavioral modeling; COPPA (See [Volume IX, Section IX.5.3]) imposes child-protection constraints that gate behavioral modeling of minors. A platform built without these in mind is a future liability; PsyForge's ethics-by-architecture and compliance matrix (See [Volume IX, Section IX.5.6]) are the structural response.

**REQ-VIS-017:** The corpus MUST cite primary research sources (Appendix I) for every behavioral claim it encodes, and contested or replication-failed findings MUST carry confidence indicators. The platform MUST NOT encode behavioral claims as settled without evidentiary support, because doing so would propagate the very ungrounded intuition the platform exists to replace.

The evidence base is not static. The corpus update process (See [Volume III, Section III.2.1.4]) exists because behavioral science evolves, replication failures revise prior findings, and new research emerges. A platform that hardcodes 2026 science will be wrong by 2032; a platform whose corpus is versioned, cited, and updatable will remain correctable. This is the deep reason the corpus is swappable (See [I.1.5.7]): not only to move between markets, but to move between states of scientific knowledge within a market.
