Each of the eight principles is derived from a specific class of failure observed in incumbent analytics and AI systems, and is justified by the gap between that failure and PsyForge's mission ([Volume I, I.1.2]). This Section records the origin and rationale in summary form; the full per-principle rationale is in [Sections I.2.1.1]–[I.2.1.8].

| Principle | Origin failure class | PsyForge response |
|---|---|---|
| Truth Over Assumptions | Confident fabrication; assumption laundering | Three epistemic tiers with mandatory provenance (`REQ-PHI-001`) |
| Psychographics Over Demographics | Demographic bias; protected-attribute targeting | Behavioral feature vector as primary key (`REQ-PHI-005`) |
| Simulation Before Deployment | Live population as experiment | Mandatory simulation gate pre-deployment (`REQ-PHI-008`) |
| Prediction Over Reaction | Lagging-indicator firefighting | Forward forecasts with confidence intervals (`REQ-PHI-011`) |
| Local-First | Cloud lock-in; privacy egress | Single-binary full-stack local capability (`REQ-PHI-014`) |
| Human-in-the-Loop | Unaccountable automation | State machine with no auto-deploy path (`REQ-PHI-017`) |
| Evidence Over Intuition | HiPPO decisions; unfalsifiable claims | Mandatory evidence citation on recommendations (`REQ-PHI-020`) |
| Ethics by Architecture | Ethics-washing; manipulation optimization | Structural Ethics Layer and Officer veto (`REQ-PHI-023`) |

The principles are not independent. Truth Over Assumptions is the epistemic foundation; Evidence Over Intuition is its decision-layer expression; Prediction Over Reaction is its temporal expression; Simulation Before Deployment is its operational instrument; Psychographics Over Demographics is its modeling constraint; Local-First is its sovereignty constraint; Human-in-the-Loop is its accountability constraint; Ethics by Architecture is its moral constraint. A change to any one principle reverberates through the others, which is why principle changes are governed by the process in [Section I.2.4.2].

The principles were selected against a negative criterion: each was retained only if its absence would permit a documented failure mode that no combination of the other seven would prevent. This criterion is the basis for the retirement criteria in [Section I.2.4.1]. For example, "Maximize Engagement" was considered and rejected as a candidate principle because it conflicts directly with Ethics by Architecture and Player Satisfaction and would, if elevated, subordinate welfare to a single metric; engagement remains an outcome the system *predicts* ([Volume I, I.4.8.2]), never a principle it *serves*. Similarly, "Move Fast" was rejected because it directly contradicts Simulation Before Deployment and Human-in-the-Loop, both of which are explicitly latency-accepting by design; velocity is a non-functional quality attribute ([Volume II, II.1.4.1]) governed elsewhere, not a principle.

The derivation also considered the platform's expansion trajectory ([Volume I, I.1.2.2]). A principle that holds only for game studios but breaks for marketing, education, or healthcare deployments would have to be re-derived on each domain entry, which would undermine the principle's authority. Each of the eight was tested against all six target corpus markets ([Volume III, III.2.13.4]) during derivation: Truth Over Assumptions holds equally for a patient-engagement model and a player model; Psychographics Over Demographics is, if anything, *more* binding in healthcare and fintech where protected-attribute law is stricter; Ethics by Architecture generalizes because manipulation risk and regulatory risk exist in every domain, merely under different statutes. This domain-generality is itself a criterion for principle changes ([Section I.2.4.1]) and is the reason the corpus is swappable ([Volume III, III.2.13]) while the principles are not.

Finally, the principles were ordered to reflect dependency, not merely importance: Truth Over Assumptions is foundational because Evidence Over Intuition, Prediction Over Reaction, and Simulation Before Deployment all depend on the epistemic integrity it enforces; Ethics by Architecture is terminal because it constrains the outputs of every downstream principle. This dependency structure is what makes the precedence hierarchy in [Section I.2.2.3] more than an arbitrary ranking.

#### I.2.2.2 Principles in Tension: Conflict Resolution Rules

Principles conflict in practice, and a specification that does not acknowledge the conflicts will be resolved ad hoc by implementers. This Section enumerates the recurring tensions and prescribes deterministic resolution rules. The rules are normative: where a tension arises that matches a rule, the rule MUST be applied and the resolution MUST be logged.

**Tension A — Local-First vs. Evidence Over Intuition.** Local models are smaller and may be less accurate than cloud models; the most accurate evidence may require cloud compute the studio is unwilling or unable to use. *Resolution:* Local-first prevails on the question of *where* data may be processed; Evidence Over Intuition prevails on the question of *what confidence may be claimed*. A locally-produced prediction MUST be labeled with its local-model confidence bound and MUST NOT inherit the confidence of a cloud model it did not use. If a studio voluntarily enables hybrid burst ([Volume II, II.5.1.3]), the higher-fidelity result supersedes, but only for that studio and only with logged consent. The system MUST NOT silently down-grade or up-grade confidence to mask the gap.

**Tension B — Prediction Over Reaction vs. Human-in-the-Loop.** A confident prediction might justify bypassing review for speed. *Resolution:* Human-in-the-Loop is a structural gate and prevails absolutely; no prediction confidence, however high, authorizes auto-deployment. The prediction's confidence is an *input* to the human reviewer, not a substitute for them. This is non-negotiable and is enforced by the state machine in [Volume II, II.2.6.2].

**Tension C — Ethics by Architecture vs. Simulation Before Deployment.** Ethics constraints may exclude regions of the simulation search space, potentially leaving the simulation unable to find any deployable solution, or narrowing it to a trivial one. *Resolution:* Ethics by Architecture prevails; an ethics-violating simulation outcome MUST be rejected ([Volume IV, IV.9.3.3]) and MUST NOT be softened to make the search feasible. The correct response to an infeasible simulation is to redesign the proposed change, not to relax the ethics constraint. The system MUST report the constraint that caused infeasibility so the human reviewer can act on it.

**Tension D — Truth Over Assumptions vs. Simulation Before Deployment.** Simulating a proposed change requires assumptions about persona behavior under novel conditions for which no telemetry exists. *Resolution:* The simulation MUST run with its assumptions explicitly tagged as the `assumed` epistemic tier; the simulation result MUST carry an aggregate assumption-density score; and a simulation whose assumption-density exceeds a corpus-defined threshold MUST be flagged as "low-evidence" on the recommendation it informs. The simulation is not forbidden, but its output is down-weighted in the recommendation's evidence profile.

**Tension E — Psychographics Over Demographics vs. Prediction Over Reaction.** A demographic proxy may, in some narrow case, predict an outcome more accurately than available psychographic features. *Resolution:* Psychographics prevails; the system MUST NOT adopt a demographic predictor even if it outperforms, because the short-term accuracy gain purchases long-term bias and regulatory exposure. Instead the Statistician Agent MUST investigate the psychographic mediator ([Volume V, V.6]) and the system MUST log the suppressed demographic correlation for fairness auditing.

**Tension F — Local-First vs. Simulation Before Deployment.** The full 10-million-persona simulation may exceed local compute. *Resolution:* Local-first prevails on sovereignty; Simulation Before Deployment prevails on the existence of a simulation. The system MUST run the highest-fidelity simulation feasible locally, MUST label the fidelity level explicitly ([Volume IV, IV.1.1.4]), and MUST block deployment if even minimum-fidelity simulation cannot complete. A studio MAY choose to delay deployment until cloud burst is available, but MUST NOT deploy unsimulated.

#### I.2.2.3 Principle Precedence Hierarchy

When two principles conflict and the specific tension is not enumerated in [Section I.2.2.2], the following precedence hierarchy MUST be applied. The hierarchy is ordered so that the principles whose violation causes irreversible or population-scale harm rank above those whose violation causes reversible or local harm.

1. **Ethics by Architecture** — highest precedence. An ethics violation can harm a population irreversibly and create regulatory liability. No other principle overrides it.
2. **Human-in-the-Loop** — structural accountability. Without it, no other principle can be corrected after the fact.
3. **Truth Over Assumptions** — epistemic foundation. Without it, downstream principles operate on fabricated inputs.
4. **Evidence Over Intuition** — decision-layer expression of Truth.
5. **Psychographics Over Demographics** — modeling constraint that prevents bias and regulatory exposure.
6. **Simulation Before Deployment** — operational instrument; valued highly but subordinate to the epistemic and ethical principles it depends on.
7. **Prediction Over Reaction** — temporal orientation; valuable but subordinate to the principles that make predictions trustworthy.
8. **Local-First** — lowest precedence as a *constraint* (sovereignty), though it remains a hard requirement (`REQ-PHI-014`) in its own domain. It ranks lowest here only because its violation is the most recoverable: a cloud-processed result can be re-processed locally, whereas an ethics violation or an unreviewed deployment cannot be un-deployed.

This hierarchy MUST be applied only when no specific rule in [Section I.2.2.2] governs the conflict. Where a specific rule exists, the rule prevails over the hierarchy, because specific rules were written against real failure modes and the hierarchy is a fallback. Every application of the hierarchy MUST be logged with the conflicting principles, the chosen resolution, and the rationale, so that recurring applications of the hierarchy can be promoted into explicit rules in future principle revisions ([Section I.2.4.2]).

The hierarchy deserves two cautions for implementers. First, it is not a license to invoke precedence to win an argument; an actor who reaches for the hierarchy before consulting the specific rules in [Section I.2.2.2] is misusing it, and the violation-detection system ([Section I.2.2.4]) treats such misuse as a procedural violation. Second, the hierarchy ranks *constraints*, not *preferences*; Local-First's low rank does not mean a studio's sovereignty preference is unimportant, only that, in the rare case where sovereignty conflicts with an ethics or accountability constraint, the latter prevails. In the overwhelming majority of real situations, the principles do not conflict at all — they reinforce — and the hierarchy is invoked only in the residual cases the specific rules did not anticipate. A rising rate of hierarchy invocations is itself a drift signal ([Section I.2.3.5]), because it indicates that the enumerated tension rules are no longer covering the conflicts the system actually encounters, and that new rules or new principles may be warranted.

The hierarchy is reviewed as a whole whenever a principle is added or retired ([Section I.2.4.2]), because a change to the set of principles can invalidate the dependency assumptions on which the ordering rests. A hierarchy that is not re-justified after a principle change is non-conformant.

#### I.2.2.4 Principle Violation Detection and Escalation

A principle violation is any state in which the system, an agent, a configuration, or a human action produces or attempts to produce an outcome inconsistent with a principle's normative requirements. Detection MUST be automated wherever the principle has an architectural enforcement mechanism ([Section I.2.3.1]); it MAY be manual for violations that have no automated signal. Detection sources include CI/CD principle compliance checks ([Section I.2.3.2]), agent recommendation checks ([Section I.2.3.3]), drift monitoring ([Section I.2.3.5]), and human reports through the whistleblower channel ([Volume I, I.5.5.4]).

Upon detection, the violation MUST be classified by severity:

- **Critical** — a violation of Ethics by Architecture, Human-in-the-Loop, or Truth Over Assumptions that has reached or could reach production. Requires immediate halt of the affected pipeline and escalation to the Ethics Review Board ([Volume I, I.5.3.1]) within one business hour.
- **Major** — a violation that degrades the quality or fairness of outputs but has not reached production, or a repeated minor violation. Requires remediation within one business day and escalation to the system owner.
- **Minor** — a procedural or logging violation that does not affect outputs. Requires remediation within one sprint.

Every violation MUST be recorded in the Principle Violation Log with: the principle violated, the requirement ID breached, the detection source, the severity, the affected artifact, the remediation action, the actor responsible, and the closure timestamp. The log is the primary input to the principle drift analysis in [Section I.2.3.5] and to the principle evolution decisions in [Section I.2.4].

Escalation paths are: automated halt → system owner → Ethics Review Board → executive escalation, with each step having a defined maximum response time. A Critical violation MAY invoke the emergency override process ([Volume II, II.2.6.6]) only to *halt* or *roll back*, never to *force through* a violating recommendation. An actor who attempts to force through a Critical violation by invoking emergency override MUST have the attempt flagged for post-hoc review, and repeated attempts MUST trigger an incident response ([Volume I, I.5.3.5]).

A non-obvious design property of this scheme is that detection is decoupled from response authority. The system can detect and halt, but it cannot absolve: only a human, through the escalation chain, can close a violation, and the closure MUST record the corrective action and the preventive control put in place to avoid recurrence. This decoupling is deliberate and reflects Human-in-the-Loop ([Section I.2.1.6]) generalized to the violation domain — the system flags, the human decides. It also reflects Ethics by Architecture ([Section I.2.1.8]): a violation of an ethics principle is too consequential to be closed by an automated "resolved" status; it requires human accountability for the corrective action and for the structural fix that prevents the same violation from recurring under slightly different conditions.

Detection coverage itself MUST be audited. The drift monitor in [Section I.2.3.5] tracks which requirement IDs have working automated detection and which rely on manual reporting; a principle whose requirements have no automated detection is a principle whose enforcement is, in practice, voluntary, and MUST be treated as a drift finding regardless of whether any violation has been recorded. The absence of detected violations can mean either good behavior or blind enforcement, and the audit system MUST distinguish the two.

#### I.2.2.5 Principles as Decision Filters in Engineering Workflow

Principles are not retrospective justifications; they are prospective filters applied at every decision point in the engineering workflow. The workflow stages at which principles MUST be applied as filters are:

1. **Requirements intake.** A new requirement is evaluated against the principles before it is accepted into the backlog. A requirement that conflicts with a principle MUST be re-scoped or rejected, with the conflict logged. *Filter owner: product owner + system owner.*
2. **Architecture decision (ADR).** Every Architecture Decision Record ([Volume II, II.1.3]) MUST include a "Principle Compliance" section stating which principles the decision affects and how it complies. An ADR that cannot demonstrate compliance MUST NOT be approved.
3. **Implementation.** Code review MUST include a principle-compliance checklist derived from the architectural mechanisms in [Section I.2.3.1]. The checklist is generated automatically from the requirement IDs touched by the change.
4. **CI/CD.** Automated principle compliance checks ([Section I.2.3.2]) MUST run on every build. A failing check MUST block merge.
5. **Agent recommendation generation.** Every recommendation MUST pass the agent-side principle checks ([Section I.2.3.3]) before it enters human review.
6. **Human review.** The reviewer interface MUST surface principle-relevant signals — epistemic tiers, ethics scores, simulation references, dissent — so the human applies the principles knowingly.
7. **Deployment.** The deployment gate ([Volume II, II.2.6]) is the final principle filter; it MUST refuse deployment on any unmet principle requirement.

At each stage, the filter MUST produce one of three outcomes: *pass* (proceed), *block* (halt with stated principle violation), or *exception* (proceed under a logged, time-limited, reviewed exception per [Section I.2.2.4]). The default outcome when a principle's status cannot be determined is *block*, not *pass*; uncertainty resolves against the principle, never around it. This conservative default is the operational expression of Truth Over Assumptions at the workflow level: an unverified compliance state is an `assumed` state, and `assumed` does not authorize deployment.

The filters are not redundant; they catch different failure classes. A requirement can pass intake and fail at implementation (the design was sound, the code was not). Code can pass review and fail CI/CD (the reviewer missed what the automated check catches). A recommendation can pass agent checks and be blocked at the deployment gate (a stale simulation reference). Each filter is REQUIRED, and the removal of any filter MUST be treated as a principle evolution decision under [Section I.2.4.2].

The filter model also implies a normative stance on where the burden of proof lies in engineering disputes. When a change is blocked by a principle filter, the burden falls on the change's proponent to demonstrate compliance — not on the system to prove non-compliance. This is the operational descendant of Truth Over Assumptions at the workflow level: a change is `assumed` non-compliant until demonstrated compliant, exactly as a behavioral datum is `assumed` until grounded in evidence. A proponent who cannot produce the compliance demonstration does not get to proceed by default; they get to either produce the demonstration, re-scope the change, or withdraw it. This stance is occasionally inconvenient and occasionally contested, but it is the only stance consistent with the principles, because the inverse — assuming compliance until proven otherwise — is precisely the silent-substitution failure mode that Truth Over Assumptions exists to prevent, transferred from the data layer to the change-management layer. The filter model is therefore not merely a process convenience; it is the load-bearing mechanism by which the principles retain authority over the day-to-day engineering activity that would otherwise drift from them under shipping pressure.

---

### I.2.3 Principle Enforcement Architecture

This Section specifies *how* the principles are technically enforced. A principle that exists only as policy is, by the definition in [Section I.2.1.8], not a principle of PsyForge. Every principle MUST have at least one architectural enforcement mechanism, and the mechanism MUST be capable of blocking the violating action, not merely logging it.

#### I.2.3.1 Architectural Mechanisms for Each Principle

| Principle | Architectural mechanism | Enforcement type |
|---|---|---|
| Truth Over Assumptions | `epistemic_tier` field on all behavioral data; provenance graph in Knowledge Graph; tier-promotion is a logged event | Data-schema + event-log enforcement |
| Psychographics Over Demographics | Feature vector schema excludes demographic fields; inferential-privacy check in model pipeline; Statistician Agent proxy-detection | Schema + pipeline + agent enforcement |
| Simulation Before Deployment | Deployment gate state machine requires a referenced Simulation Run; gate refuses missing/stale references | State-machine enforcement (cannot be bypassed by system) |
| Prediction Over Reaction | Forecast models with confidence intervals; calibration monitor quarantines drifting models; `prediction_unavailable` state prevents silent fallback | Model-governance enforcement |
| Local-First | Single-binary build artifact; conformance suite runs offline; cloud sync is opt-in replica | Build + conformance enforcement |
| Human-in-the-Loop | Recommendation state machine has no system-only path to `Deployed`; emergency override is human-initiated, time-limited, audited | State-machine enforcement |
| Evidence Over Intuition | Recommendation schema requires `evidence` array with provenance links; override triggers tracking experiment | Schema + workflow enforcement |
| Ethics by Architecture | Ethics Layer in data pipeline; Ethics Officer Agent veto in recommendation flow; ethics as hard constraints in simulation search | Pipeline + agent + constraint enforcement |

The enforcement types are not equivalent in strength. State-machine enforcement (Simulation Before Deployment, Human-in-the-Loop, parts of Ethics by Architecture) is the strongest: the system physically cannot perform the violating action because the code path does not exist. Schema enforcement (Truth Over Assumptions, Psychographics Over Demographics, Evidence Over Intuition) is strong but bypassable by a determined actor with write access to the schema; it is therefore paired with CI/CD checks ([Section I.2.3.2]) that detect schema drift. Agent enforcement (Ethics Officer veto, Statistician proxy-detection) is only as strong as the agent's authority; agent authority is therefore codified in the recommendation state machine so that an agent veto is a structural block, not an advisory. Where a principle admits multiple mechanisms, all MUST be present; defense in depth is REQUIRED because no single mechanism is sufficient against all failure modes (intentional bypass, accidental misconfiguration, agent failure).

The mechanisms are themselves versioned artifacts and are subject to the same change governance as the principles. An enforcement mechanism that is silently weakened — a CI check relaxed, a state-machine transition relaxed, an agent prompt edited to be less strict — is functionally a principle change and MUST pass the change process in [Section I.2.4.2]. This is the operational expression of Ethics by Architecture applied to the enforcement layer itself: the enforcement of the principles must itself be enforced against erosion, or the principles reduce to policy. The drift monitor in [Section I.2.3.5] treats weakening of an enforcement mechanism as a first-class drift signal, distinct from a violation of the principle the mechanism enforces, because the two have different root causes and different remedies. A violation says the principle was breached despite the mechanism; a weakened mechanism says the breach was made possible by the erosion of the mechanism, which is the more dangerous condition because it permits a class of violations rather than a single one.

#### I.2.3.2 Principle Compliance Checks in CI/CD

The CI/CD pipeline MUST run a suite of principle compliance checks on every build and on every pull request. The checks are normative and MUST block merge on failure; they are not advisory warnings. The check suite includes, at minimum:

- **EPISTEMIC-TIER CHECK** — verifies that every new behavioral datum type introduced by the change declares an `epistemic_tier` and that no datum type silently defaults to `observed`. Enforces `REQ-PHI-001`, `REQ-PHI-002`.
- **DEMOGRAPHIC-EXCLUSION CHECK** — verifies that the feature vector schema used for modeling contains no demographic fields and that no new feature definition references a demographic proxy. Enforces `REQ-PHI-005`, `REQ-PHI-006`.
- **SIMULATION-GATE CHECK** — verifies that the recommendation state machine code contains no path from a system state to `Deployed` without a verified simulation reference. Enforces `REQ-PHI-008`, `REQ-PHI-017`.
- **FORECAST-CONFIDENCE CHECK** — verifies that every forecasting model emits confidence intervals and that no model emits a bare point estimate as its primary output. Enforces `REQ-PHI-011`, `REQ-PHI-013`.
- **LOCAL-FIRST CHECK** — runs the conformance suite in an air-gapped sandbox and fails if any test attempts network egress. Enforces `REQ-PHI-014`, `REQ-PHI-015`.
- **EVIDENCE-CITATION CHECK** — verifies that the recommendation schema requires an `evidence` array and that no recommendation template omits it. Enforces `REQ-PHI-020`.
- **ETHICS-SCORE CHECK** — verifies that no recommendation template can enter the review state without all seven ethics dimensions populated. Enforces `REQ-PHI-023`.

Each check MUST emit a structured report identifying the requirement ID, the artifact checked, the result, and, on failure, the specific violation. Check definitions live in version control alongside the code they govern; changes to checks are themselves subject to the principle change process in [Section I.2.4.2], because weakening a check is functionally equivalent to weakening a principle. A check that is flaky or false-positives frequently MUST be hardened, not disabled; disabling a check requires a Critical-severity exception per [Section I.2.2.4] and MUST be time-limited.

Two operational properties of the check suite are normative. First, the suite MUST run on every build, including local developer builds, not only on the central CI server — because Local-First ([Section I.2.1.5]) requires that a developer working offline can verify principle compliance without cloud CI. The checks therefore MUST be packaged into the single-binary distribution and MUST run against the developer's local schema and code. Second, the suite MUST be exhaustive over the requirement IDs in this Part; a requirement ID with no corresponding check is a gap, and the audit system in [Section I.2.3.4] MUST flag such gaps. The mapping from requirement ID to check is itself a versioned artifact, so that an auditor can answer, for any requirement, "which check enforces this, and when did it last pass?" Coverage gaps discovered during audit are treated as Major-severity findings under [Section I.2.2.4], because an unenforced requirement is a principle that exists only on paper.

Finally, the check suite MUST be designed to fail loudly and early, in accordance with the general principle that uncertainty resolves against the principle. A check that cannot determine compliance — for instance, because a referenced artifact is absent — MUST fail the build rather than pass with a warning, because a warning is the CI analogue of an unmarked assumption ([Section I.2.1.1]).

#### I.2.3.3 Principle Compliance Checks in Agent Recommendations

Agent-generated recommendations are a high-risk surface because agents can produce fluent, plausible output that violates principles in subtle ways. The Orchestrator ([Volume V, V.2]) MUST run principle compliance checks on every recommendation draft before it enters human review. The checks run as a post-generation filter and MUST be independent of the generating agent's self-assessment — an agent does not certify its own compliance.

The agent-side check suite includes:

- **Epistemic consistency** — every claim in the recommendation's rationale MUST map to a `epistemic_tier`; claims that map to nothing MUST be rejected or downgraded to `assumed` with explicit marking.
- **Demographic neutrality** — the recommendation MUST NOT condition targeting on a demographic attribute; if it does, the Statistician Agent MUST be invoked to assess proxy substitution.
- **Simulation reference** — if the recommendation proposes a deployable change, it MUST reference a valid, fresh Simulation Run; otherwise it MUST be marked `simulation_pending` and MUST NOT enter the deployment-ready queue.
- **Forecast presence** — outcome claims MUST cite a forecast with confidence intervals; bare assertions of future outcomes MUST be rejected.
- **Evidence sufficiency** — the `evidence` array MUST contain at least one item per major claim, with provenance resolvable into the Knowledge Graph or experiment store.
- **Ethics score presence and threshold** — the Ethics Officer Agent MUST have scored the recommendation; if any dimension exceeds its blocking threshold, the recommendation MUST be vetoed or sent to escalation per [Volume V, V.11.5].
- **Dissent surfacing** — if two agents produced conflicting assessments, the dissent MUST be attached to the recommendation so the human reviewer sees it.

A recommendation that fails any check MUST be returned to the Orchestrator for remediation or marked as blocked. The checks MUST be idempotent and deterministic so that a recommendation cannot pass by retry alone; a persistently-failing recommendation is itself a signal of a model or agent problem and MUST be logged for drift analysis ([Section I.2.3.5]). The checks do not replace human review ([Section I.2.1.6]); they ensure that what reaches the human is already principle-compliant, so the human's attention is spent on judgment, not on catching violations the system should have caught itself.

The agent-side checks are deliberately structured as a *separate* evaluation pass from the recommendation generation, performed by components that did not generate the recommendation. This separation is a defense against a known agent failure mode — the tendency of generative agents to be self-consistent and to rationalize their own outputs ([Volume V, V.1.6.1]) — by ensuring that compliance is not assessed by the same reasoning that produced the output. In practice this means the Ethics Officer Agent, the Statistician Agent, and the dedicated principle-check harness operate on the recommendation as an artifact, not as collaborators in its generation. Where the generating agent is permitted to self-correct and resubmit, the resubmission is itself logged, and a high resubmission rate on a given agent or principle is a drift signal, because it indicates that the agent is systematically producing outputs that require correction rather than producing compliant outputs initially.

The agent-side checks MUST also cover the negative space: a recommendation that *omits* a required element (no simulation reference, no evidence array, no ethics score) is a violation of the same severity as one that includes a non-compliant element, because omission is the more common and more dangerous failure mode — an absent field is easier to overlook in review than a present-but-wrong field. The checks therefore verify presence and validity, and a recommendation missing a required field is blocked identically to one whose field fails validation.

#### I.2.3.4 Principle Auditing and Reporting

Principle compliance MUST be auditable retroactively. The audit system MUST reconstruct, for any deployed recommendation or any production-affecting decision, the full chain of principle checks it passed, the evidence it cited, the simulations it referenced, the ethics scores it carried, the human approvals it received, and any exceptions logged. The audit trail is the substrate on which post-hoc accountability, regulatory response, and principle evolution all depend.

Audit artifacts include: the Principle Violation Log ([Section I.2.2.4]); the CI/CD check reports ([Section I.2.3.2]); the agent-side check reports ([Section I.2.3.3]); the recommendation state-machine transition log ([Volume II, II.2.6.4]); the Ethics Score audit trail ([Volume I, I.5.2.5]); and the epistemic-tier promotion log. These artifacts MUST be retained per the data-retention policy ([Volume VI, VI.3.4]) with a minimum retention sufficient to cover the longest applicable regulatory statute of limitations, and MUST be exportable for external audit on demand.

Reporting MUST be role-adapted. Executive reports aggregate principle compliance into trend indicators (e.g., "ethics-veto rate over the quarter," "assumption-density of deployed recommendations"). Engineering reports drill into per-check failure rates and per-requirement compliance. Ethics Review Board reports focus on Critical violations and on the tension resolutions invoked under [Section I.2.2.2]. All reports MUST cite the underlying requirement IDs so that a reader can trace any reported number to the normative source. A report that cannot be traced to requirement IDs is non-conformant and MUST NOT be issued as an official audit report.

The audit system MUST itself be auditable: the integrity of the audit logs is a Critical concern, because an audit log that can be silently altered defeats the purpose of every other enforcement mechanism. Audit logs MUST be append-only, MUST be tamper-evident (via chained hashes or equivalent), and MUST be replicated to a store that is administratively separate from the system being audited, so that an actor with write access to the system does not by that access gain write access to the audit of the system. This separation is the audit analogue of the independence property required for agent-side checks ([Section I.2.3.3]) and reflects the same reasoning: the auditor of a thing must not be the operator of the thing. Audit log integrity is itself a drift-monitored signal ([Section I.2.3.5]); any indication of log tampering or unexplained gap is treated as a Critical violation and escalated immediately, because loss of auditability is the precondition for every other class of concealable violation.

External audit support is a first-class requirement, not an afterthought. PsyForge deployments in regulated industries ([Volume III, III.2.13.4.d–f]) will be subject to external audit, and the audit export MUST be sufficient for that purpose without bespoke engineering. The export format, the retention period, and the access controls are specified in [Volume VI, VI.3] and cross-referenced from the regulatory mapping in [Volume I, I.5.5].

#### I.2.3.5 Principle Drift Monitoring Over Time

Principles can erode without any single violating act: a schema field becomes optional, a CI check grows flaky and is silently skipped, an agent's compliance output is overridden increasingly often, an exception becomes routine. This slow erosion is "principle drift," and it is the primary long-term threat to the principles, more dangerous than any single Critical violation because it is invisible to per-event detection. PsyForge MUST monitor for drift explicitly.

Drift signals include: rising `assumed`-tier proportion in deployed recommendations over time; rising ethics-veto override rate; rising frequency of emergency overrides; rising proportion of recommendations reaching review with missing or stale simulation references; declining CI check coverage (checks disabled, skipped, or excluded from the build); rising agent-check failure-then-pass-on-retry rate; and rising invocation of the principle precedence hierarchy in [Section I.2.2.3], which indicates that named tension rules are no longer covering the real conflicts. Each signal MUST have a baseline and a threshold; crossing a threshold MUST raise a drift alert routed to the system owner and, for ethics-related signals, to the Ethics Review Board.

Drift analysis MUST run on a defined cadence (RECOMMENDED monthly) and MUST produce a Principle Drift Report that feeds the principle evolution process in [Section I.2.4]. A sustained drift signal is itself sufficient grounds to propose a principle change — either to sharpen the existing principle against the observed erosion, or to add an explicit tension rule ([Section I.2.2.2]) for a conflict that was being resolved ad hoc. The system MUST NOT treat drift as a tuning problem to be silenced; drift is information about the gap between the specified principles and the operating reality, and closing that gap is the purpose of the evolution machinery in the next Section.

Drift detection is itself a statistical process and is therefore subject to the same rigor the system applies to its behavioral forecasts: drift baselines MUST be calibrated, drift thresholds MUST be set with stated false-positive and false-negative targets, and the drift monitor MUST NOT be tuned to silence alerts any more than a behavioral model MAY be tuned to hide forecast error. The drift monitor is, in effect, a forecasting model of the platform's own principle compliance, and it inherits the Prediction Over Reaction obligation ([Section I.2.1.4]) to report uncertainty honestly: a drift signal of uncertain significance MUST be reported as uncertain, not suppressed, and the uncertainty MUST propagate to the Principle Drift Report so that the human consumers of the report can weigh it. A drift monitor that has been silently retuned to reduce alert volume is a Critical-severity finding, because it is functionally equivalent to disabling an enforcement mechanism ([Section I.2.3.1]) and conceals exactly the slow erosion the monitor exists to catch.

Finally, drift monitoring MUST distinguish local drift (a single studio or deployment) from systemic drift (across all deployments). Local drift indicates a studio-specific practice that should be corrected through that studio's governance; systemic drift indicates a principle or enforcement mechanism that is mismatched to the operating reality and should be addressed through the evolution process. Conflating the two leads either to over-broad principle changes (treating a local practice as a systemic flaw) or to under-responsive principles (treating a systemic flaw as a local anomaly), and the drift report MUST make the local/systemic distinction explicit so that the correct remediation path is chosen.

---

### I.2.4 Principle Evolution

The principles are stable but not immutable. They MUST evolve as the system encounters situations the original principles did not anticipate, as the domain expands ([Volume I, I.1.2.2]), and as drift and violation data ([Section I.2.3.5], [Section I.2.2.4]) reveal gaps. Evolution is governed to prevent two failure modes: stagnation (principles that no longer match reality) and capricious change (principles rewritten under pressure to permit a favored action). The criteria and process below balance these.

#### I.2.4.1 Criteria for Adding or Retiring Principles

A candidate principle MAY be added only if it satisfies all of the following criteria:

1. **Failure-mode criterion.** The candidate prevents a failure mode that no combination of the existing eight principles prevents, and the failure mode is documented with at least one observed or plausibly-simulated instance. A principle that merely rephrases an existing principle is rejected.
2. **Enforceability criterion.** The candidate can be enforced by at least one architectural mechanism in [Section I.2.3.1]. A principle that can only be enforced by policy is rejected, consistent with Ethics by Architecture ([Section I.2.1.8]) generalized to all principles.
3. **Tension criterion.** The candidate's tensions with each existing principle are enumerable and resolvable under a rule of the form in [Section I.2.2.2]. A candidate that creates unresolvable tensions is rejected or re-scoped.
4. **Precedence criterion.** The candidate can be placed in the precedence hierarchy ([Section I.2.2.3]) without invalidating the existing ordering. If it cannot, the hierarchy itself must be re-justified as part of the proposal.
5. **Domain-generality criterion.** The candidate holds across all corpus domains PsyForge targets ([Volume III, III.2.13.4]). A principle specific to a single domain is rejected as a domain rule, not a platform principle.

An existing principle MAY be retired only if it satisfies all of the following:

1. **Obsolescence criterion.** The failure mode the principle prevents is demonstrably prevented by other principles or by structural changes, such that the principle adds no independent protection.
2. **Drift-evidence criterion.** Retirement is supported by drift data ([Section I.2.3.5]) showing the principle is either inert (never triggered) or harmful (consistently producing false blocks). Inertia alone is insufficient; a dormant principle may be dormant because it is working.
3. **Replacement criterion.** Any protection the retiring principle provided that is still needed MUST be explicitly transferred to another principle or to an architectural mechanism, with the transfer traced requirement-by-requirement.
4. **Reversibility criterion.** Retirement MUST be reversible: the principle's requirements and enforcement mechanisms MUST be retained in version control so that retirement can be undone if the failure mode re-emerges.

A principle MUST NOT be added or retired to permit a specific favored recommendation, deployment, or business outcome. Proposals that correlate suspiciously with a pending business decision MUST be flagged and require external review by the Ethics Review Board. This criterion is the single most important guard against capricious evolution: principles exist to constrain business outcomes, and a principle change whose only effect is to remove a constraint on a business outcome is, by definition, not a principle evolution but a principle circumvention.

#### I.2.4.2 Principle Change Process

Principle changes follow a formal process with mandatory stages. Each stage produces an artifact that is retained for the Historical Principle Decisions Log ([Section I.2.4.3]).

1. **Proposal.** Any stakeholder MAY submit a Principle Change Proposal (PCP) using the standard template. The PCP MUST state the change, the criterion or criteria it satisfies ([Section I.2.4.1]), the affected requirement IDs, the affected architectural mechanisms, and the expected impact on each existing principle's enforcement.
2. **Impact analysis.** The system owner MUST produce an impact analysis covering: every requirement ID touched, every CI/CD check affected ([Section I.2.3.2]), every agent check affected ([Section I.2.3.3]), every tension rule affected ([Section I.2.2.2]), and the precedence hierarchy ([Section I.2.2.3]). The analysis MUST be reviewed by the Statistician Agent for any quantitative claim and by the Ethics Officer Agent for any ethics-relevant change.
3. **Ethics Review Board review.** For any change touching Ethics by Architecture, Human-in-the-Loop, or Truth Over Assumptions — and RECOMMENDED for all changes — the Ethics Review Board ([Volume I, I.5.3.1]) MUST review and approve. The Board's review is binding; it MAY reject or require modification.
4. **Implementation planning.** The change MUST be decomposed into implementation tasks: schema migrations, CI check additions/removals, agent prompt updates, documentation updates across all Volumes. Each task MUST reference the requirement IDs it implements.
5. **Approval.** Final approval rests with the documented principle owner (the executive accountable for the principles, named in [Volume I, FM.1.1]). Approval MUST be recorded with the approver, the date, and the rationale.
6. **Implementation and verification.** Implementation MUST pass the full CI/CD suite, including any new checks the change introduces. A change that weakens an existing check MUST, before merge, demonstrate that the weakened check still prevents the failure mode the principle targets; this demonstration is itself a reviewable artifact.
7. **Communication.** Approved changes MUST be communicated to all studios and operators, with migration guidance, because principle changes can alter the meaning of recommendations already in their review queues.

The process is deliberately heavyweight. Principle changes are infrequent and consequential, and the cost of a wrong change — a principle circumvented under pressure — exceeds the cost of process friction. Emergency principle changes are NOT permitted; emergencies are handled by the exception and override mechanisms in [Section I.2.2.4] and [Volume II, II.2.6.6], which are time-limited and fully audited, not by rewriting the principles to fit the emergency. This distinction is load-bearing: an exception is an acknowledged deviation from a principle that remains in force, whereas a principle change to accommodate an emergency would make the deviation permanent and invisible. By forbidding the latter, the process ensures that every emergency leaves a trace that the evolution machinery can later evaluate, rather than erasing the principle that the emergency violated.

The process also MUST be asymmetric with respect to direction. Strengthening a principle (adding a requirement, raising a threshold, adding an enforcement mechanism) MAY proceed under a lighter review than weakening one, because strengthening moves the system toward the principles' intent whereas weakening moves away from it. Weakening a principle — removing a requirement, lowering a threshold, retiring a check, relaxing a constraint — MUST always receive the full review including Ethics Review Board sign-off, regardless of which principle is affected, because weakening is the direction in which pressure operates and in which capricious change is most likely. This asymmetry is itself a defense against the most common evolution failure: the gradual, individually-reasonable weakening that, accumulated over time, hollows out a principle without any single change appearing significant enough to warrant scrutiny.

All artifacts produced by the process — the PCP, the impact analysis, the review decisions, the approval, the implementation tasks, the communication — MUST be filed to the Historical Principle Decisions Log ([Section I.2.4.3]) at the stage that produces them, not only at completion, so that an in-flight change is itself auditable and so that an abandoned proposal leaves a record of why it was abandoned.

#### I.2.4.3 Historical Principle Decisions Log

The Historical Principle Decisions Log (HPDL) is the append-only record of every principle change, every granted exception, and every Critical-severity violation. It is the institutional memory of the principles and the primary input to future evolution decisions. The HPDL MUST be retained for the lifetime of the platform and MUST be exportable for external audit.

Each HPDL entry MUST contain: a unique decision ID; the decision date; the decision type (principle added, principle retired, requirement added, requirement retired, exception granted, Critical violation resolved, tension rule added, precedence hierarchy revised); the PCP reference if applicable; the criteria satisfied ([Section I.2.4.1]); the approving authority; the affected requirement IDs; the before and after states; the rationale; and the review status (approved, rejected, superseded). Superseded entries MUST remain in the log with a forward pointer to the superseding entry; history is never deleted, consistent with the auditability requirement in [Section I.2.3.4].

The HPDL serves three functions. First, it makes principle evolution legible: a new stakeholder can read the log and understand why the principles are what they are, which is essential for principled adherence rather than rote compliance. Second, it prevents re-litigation: a proposal that duplicates a previously-rejected change MUST reference the prior rejection and MUST explain what has changed; the log makes such duplication visible. Third, it supports drift analysis ([Section I.2.3.5]): a cluster of exceptions or violations around a particular principle is evidence that the principle needs sharpening, not that it needs relaxing, and the log provides the longitudinal evidence to distinguish the two.

The initial state of the HPDL, as of PsyForge v1.0, records the adoption of the eight principles in [Section I.2.1] with their initial requirement sets (`REQ-PHI-001` through `REQ-PHI-025`) and the precedence hierarchy in [Section I.2.2.3]. No principle has been retired; no exception has been granted at v1.0 issuance. All subsequent entries are forward-dated from the platform's first operational deployment.

The HPDL is itself protected by the audit-integrity controls established in [Section I.2.3.4]: append-only semantics, tamper-evidence, and administrative separation from the system it records. This protection is essential because the HPDL is the ultimate ground truth for what the principles are and have been; if the HPDL could be altered retroactively, then every principle, requirement, and historical decision in it would become unreliable, and the entire enforcement architecture would rest on a foundation that could be silently rewritten. Compromise of the HPDL is therefore classified as a Critical violation under [Section I.2.2.4] and triggers immediate escalation, including, where applicable, notification to external auditors and regulators who have relied on the log's integrity.

Access to the HPDL MUST be read-only for all roles except the documented principle owner and the Ethics Review Board acting in their change-approval capacity; even those roles MUST perform writes through the change process in [Section I.2.4.2], not through direct log mutation. The log's append-only guarantee is enforced at the storage layer, not merely at the application layer, so that an application-layer compromise cannot bypass it. This defense-in-depth reflects the same reasoning applied to enforcement mechanisms generally ([Section I.2.3.1]): the integrity of the record must not depend on a single layer that, if compromised, would defeat the entire system of principle accountability. The HPDL is, in this sense, the keystone of the principle enforcement architecture, and its protection is treated with corresponding rigor.

---

**End of Part I.2 — Philosophy and Core Principles.**
## Part I.3 — Terminology and Glossary

> **Volume:** I — Strategic Foundation
> **Part:** I.3 — Terminology and Glossary
> **Specification Status:** Normative
> **Governing Law:** [WPAI Constitution](https://github.com/MrWizard94-Compile/Agents-Constitution), frozen per AGENTS.md (CONST-COMPLETE-001, DOC-SYNC-001, DOC-WHY-001)
> **Last Updated:** August 7, 2026

This Part establishes the controlled vocabulary for the PsyForge v1.0 specification. Every term used with domain-specific meaning elsewhere in the specification MUST be defined here, or in a normatively referenced appendix, before it may be relied upon for conformance. The glossary is the single source of terminological truth: where a term is used inconsistently in prose elsewhere, the definition in this Part prevails (REQ-GLOS-001).

The glossary is organized into four chapters. Chapter I.3.1 specifies the structural rules — format, lifecycle, cross-referencing, and maintenance — that govern the glossary itself. Chapter I.3.2 defines the core domain terms that constitute PsyForge's behavioral-intelligence vocabulary. Chapter I.3.3 defines the system-architecture terms that name the platform's components and tiers. Chapter I.3.4 defines the process and governance terms that govern how artifacts move from proposal to deployment. Together, these chapters provide the lexical substrate upon which Volumes II through X build their normative requirements.

---

### I.3.1 Glossary Structure

#### I.3.1.1 Glossary Format and Conventions

Every glossary entry in the PsyForge specification MUST conform to a uniform structural template so that readers, implementers, and automated tooling can parse any term identically. The canonical entry format comprises six required fields and two optional fields, defined as follows. The **Term** field states the canonical term in Title Case, accompanied by any approved abbreviations. The **Definition** field provides a single-paragraph formal definition stated in normative prose; it MUST be self-contained, MUST NOT reference the reader's prior knowledge, and MUST NOT contain circular references to the term being defined. The **Rationale** field answers the DOC-WHY-001 obligation by stating the problem the term exists to solve and the design decision it embodies. The **Differentiation** field disambiguates the term from its nearest neighbors, because PsyForge deliberately introduces fine-grained distinctions (e.g., *Behavioral Feature* vs. *Feature Vector* vs. *Embedding*) that adjacent industry vocabularies conflate. The **Cross-References** field lists the volume and section where the term is specified in operational detail, using the form "See [Volume X, Section X.N.N.N]". The **See Also** field enumerates related terms by their canonical names. The optional **Aliases** field records deprecated or informal synonyms, and the optional **Normative Keywords** field records which RFC 2119 keywords bind the term's usage.

All definitions MUST use ISO/RFC-grade specification language consistent with the Shared Writing Guidelines. Normative keywords (MUST, MUST NOT, SHOULD, SHOULD NOT, MAY, REQUIRED, RECOMMENDED, OPTIONAL) are reserved for binding constraints; a definition that contains no binding constraint SHOULD use descriptive voice without these keywords. Terms MUST be alphabetized within each chapter, except where the chapter follows the master TOC ordering for traceability, in which case the TOC ordering prevails and an alphabetical index is maintained separately in Appendix F (REQ-GLOS-002).

Conformance to this format is itself a normative requirement: any glossary entry that omits a required field, or that introduces a term into the body of the specification without first registering it here, is non-conformant. Tooling in the specification build pipeline MUST validate every cross-reference resolves to an existing section and every "See Also" entry resolves to a defined term. The format is stable across minor versions of the specification; structural changes to the entry template require a major-version increment and a migration of all existing entries.

**See also:** I.3.1.2 (Term Lifecycle), I.3.1.3 (Cross-Reference and Dependency Graph), Appendix F (Glossary Index).

#### I.3.1.2 Term Lifecycle (Proposed, Accepted, Deprecated)

Glossary terms are not immutable; they evolve as the platform's understanding matures. To prevent terminological drift — the silent redefinition of a term across volumes that undermines cross-references — every term MUST carry a lifecycle state recorded in the glossary registry. The lifecycle comprises four normative states: **Proposed**, **Accepted**, **Deprecated**, and **Retired**. A term enters the **Proposed** state when a contributor submits a definition that has not yet been reviewed; Proposed terms MAY appear in draft sections but MUST NOT be relied upon in normative requirements elsewhere until Accepted. A term transitions to **Accepted** when the specification governance body (see I.3.1.4) approves the definition following review; only Accepted terms are binding for conformance purposes (REQ-GLOS-003).

A term enters the **Deprecated** state when the governance body determines the term is misleading, redundant, or superseded, but removal would break existing cross-references. Deprecated terms MUST retain their definition with a visible deprecation marker, MUST list the preferred successor term, and MUST NOT be introduced in new normative prose. The deprecation period MUST last at least one minor-version cycle to permit downstream consumers to migrate. After the deprecation period, a term transitions to **Retired**: it is removed from the active glossary and preserved only in a historical appendix so that archived cross-references remain interpretable. Retired terms MUST NOT be reintroduced with a different meaning for at least two major versions to prevent confusion (REQ-GLOS-004).

The rationale for a formal lifecycle, rather than silent edits, is DOC-SYNC-001 compliance: the specification is a living document consumed by multiple teams and automated systems, and an untracked redefinition of, for example, *Player Model* would silently invalidate every requirement that depends on that term. Each lifecycle transition MUST be recorded with a timestamp, the transitioning authority, a rationale, and the affected cross-references, producing an auditable terminological history. This lifecycle mirrors the requirement lifecycle defined in [Front Matter, Section FM.0.10.3] and is consistent with the model-governance lifecycle in [Volume III, Section III.5.5].

**See also:** I.3.1.1 (Glossary Format and Conventions), I.3.1.4 (Glossary Maintenance Process), FM.0.10.3 (Requirement Lifecycle), III.5.5 (Model Governance).

#### I.3.1.3 Cross-Reference and Dependency Graph for Terms

A glossary is a graph, not a list. Terms depend on other terms: *Virtual Persona* is defined in terms of *Player Model*, which is defined in terms of *Feature Vector* and *Embedding*, which derive from *Behavioral Feature* and *Normalized Event*. To keep this dependency structure explicit and acyclic, the glossary MUST maintain a machine-readable cross-reference and dependency graph (REQ-GLOS-005). Each Accepted term declares its normative dependencies — the other Accepted terms whose definitions it relies upon — and its dependents — the terms that rely upon it. The graph MUST be acyclic at every commit; a cycle indicates a circular definition and is a build-breaking defect.

The graph serves three operational purposes. First, it enables impact analysis: when a term is proposed for redefinition or deprecation (I.3.1.2), the graph yields the transitive set of dependent terms and normative sections that must be re-reviewed, preventing silent breakage. Second, it supports onboarding: a reader encountering a term can traverse the dependency graph downward to foundational terms and upward to applied contexts, constructing a learning path. Third, it drives the alphabetical and topical indices in Appendix F, which are generated rather than hand-maintained.

The graph is encoded in the specification repository alongside the glossary source, in a format consumable by the build pipeline. Edges are typed: *depends-on* (normative definitional reliance), *contrasts-with* (explicit disambiguation, recorded in the Differentiation field), *composes* (one term is a structural part of another), and *evolves-into* (lifecycle succession). The build pipeline MUST render a visualization of this graph into Appendix D and MUST fail on orphaned references — a "See Also" pointing to a non-existent term — and on dependency cycles. Where a term's definition references an external standard (e.g., an ISO or RFC term), the edge is typed *external-reference* and the external source MUST be cited; such terms are informative unless re-stated as normative.

**See also:** I.3.1.1 (Glossary Format and Conventions), I.3.1.2 (Term Lifecycle), Appendix D (Architecture Diagrams Index), Appendix F (Glossary Index).

#### I.3.1.4 Glossary Maintenance Process

The glossary is maintained under the same change-control regime as the rest of the specification (see [Front Matter, Section FM.1.2]). Any contributor MAY propose a new term, a redefinition, or a deprecation by submitting a glossary change request against the specification repository. The request MUST include: the proposed term and definition in canonical format (I.3.1.1); the rationale; the dependency edges to add or modify (I.3.1.3); the affected cross-references in the body of the specification; and, for deprecations, the proposed successor term and migration guidance (REQ-GLOS-006).

Change requests are triaged by the specification governance body, which owns terminological authority for the specification. Triage classifies each request as Editorial (clarification without semantic change), Minor (semantic change with no impact on conformance), or Major (semantic change that affects conformance). Editorial changes MAY be merged directly. Minor changes require review by at least one domain reviewer and an updated dependency graph. Major changes require review by the full governance body, a deprecation or migration period per I.3.1.2 if they alter an Accepted term's binding meaning, and regeneration of all derived artifacts (indices, traceability matrix, compliance mappings) (REQ-GLOS-007).

The maintenance cadence is rolling: there is no fixed release window for glossary changes, but a snapshot of the glossary MUST be frozen at every minor-version tag so that conformance claims are pinned to a terminological state. A quarterly audit MUST reconcile the glossary against actual term usage in the specification body; terms used normatively but absent from the glossary, or defined but never referenced, are flagged as defects. This audit is the terminological analogue of the requirements traceability matrix (Appendix G) and exists to enforce the invariant that the glossary is the single source of terminological truth.

**See also:** I.3.1.1 (Glossary Format and Conventions), I.3.1.2 (Term Lifecycle), FM.1.2 (Change Control Process), FM.1.4 (Review and Approval Workflow), Appendix G (Requirements Traceability Matrix).

---

### I.3.2 Core Domain Terms

#### I.3.2.1 Behavioral Feature

**Definition.** A *Behavioral Feature* is a named, versioned, computed signal that summarizes a quantifiable aspect of a human actor's behavior over a defined temporal window, derived from one or more Normalized Events rather than recorded directly. A Behavioral Feature is an abstraction above raw telemetry: where a Telemetry Event captures *what happened*, a Behavioral Feature captures *what that pattern of events means about the actor's disposition, propensity, or state*.

**Rationale.** Raw telemetry is voluminous, noisy, and game-specific. A designer cannot reason over millions of click events, and a model trained on raw events overfits to surface mechanics and fails to transfer. The Behavioral Feature exists to transform raw interaction noise into higher-level, interpretable, and cross-game-reusable signals — such as *Average Decision Time*, *Exploration Preference*, *Risk Tolerance* — that are stable, explainable, and directly meaningful to a human designer. The Behavioral Feature Engine that produces them is a declared intellectual-property asset of PsyForge (REQ-FEAT-001). See [Volume I, Section I.1.5.1] and [Volume III, Section III.3.1].

**Differentiation.** A Behavioral Feature is distinct from a *Feature Vector* (I.3.2.14), which is the assembled, ordered, numeric array of feature values consumed by a model. It is distinct from an *Embedding* (I.3.2.15), which is a learned dense representation produced by a model rather than a hand-defined signal. It is distinct from a *Normalized Event* (I.3.2.3), which is the cleaned input from which features are computed. A Behavioral Feature is a single named signal with a definition and a value; a vector is a collection of values; an embedding is a learned representation.

**Cross-References.** See [Volume III, Section III.3] (Feature Engineering Pipeline), [Volume III, Section III.3.2] (Feature Catalog), [Volume III, Section III.3.4] (Feature Validation).

**See also:** I.3.2.2 (Telemetry Event), I.3.2.3 (Normalized Event), I.3.2.14 (Feature Vector), I.3.2.15 (Embedding), I.3.3.4 (Feature Engineering Pipeline).

#### I.3.2.2 Telemetry Event

**Definition.** A *Telemetry Event* is the atomic, timestamped record of a single discrete occurrence within an instrumented product, emitted by the PsyForge SDK at the point of observation and transmitted to the telemetry collector. A Telemetry Event comprises a common envelope (event ID, type, client and server timestamps, pseudonymized player ID, session ID, client version, platform, sequence number, schema version) and a type-specific payload. See [Volume VI, Section VI.1.2.1].

**Rationale.** Telemetry Events are the foundational input of the entire platform — the ground truth from which all higher-order artifacts (features, models, recommendations) are ultimately derived. Without a rigorously specified event format, downstream normalization and feature extraction operate on ambiguous data, and the platform's core principle of *Truth Over Assumptions* (see [Volume I, Section I.2.1.1]) becomes unsupportable. The Telemetry Event exists to make observation explicit, structured, schema-validated, and traceable to its source so that every downstream claim can be audited back to evidence (REQ-TEL-001).

**Differentiation.** A Telemetry Event is the raw, as-emitted observation; it is game-specific and may use game-specific units, identifiers, and field names. It is distinct from a *Normalized Event* (I.3.2.3), which is the cleaned, schema-canonical, deduplicated, unit-standardized artifact produced by the Event Normalization Layer. A Telemetry Event has not yet been validated against quality rules; a Normalized Event has. A Telemetry Event is distinct from a *Behavioral Feature* (I.3.2.1): the former records an occurrence, the latter summarizes a pattern of occurrences.

**Cross-References.** See [Volume VI, Section VI.1.2] (Event Schema), [Volume VI, Section VI.1.1] (Telemetry Architecture), [Volume II, Section II.2.1.1] (Telemetry Collection).

**See also:** I.3.2.1 (Behavioral Feature), I.3.2.3 (Normalized Event), I.3.3.1 (Event Normalization Layer), I.3.3.2 (Operational Storage).

#### I.3.2.3 Normalized Event

**Definition.** A *Normalized Event* is a Telemetry Event that has passed through the Event Normalization Layer and been transformed into PsyForge's canonical internal schema: fields mapped to canonical names, units standardized, timestamps normalized to a single time zone and epoch, identifiers resolved and deduplicated, and enrichment fields appended. A Normalized Event is the form in which events are persisted to the Data Lake and made available to the Feature Engineering Pipeline. See [Volume VI, Section VI.1.3].

**Rationale.** Telemetry originates from heterogeneous SDKs across engines, platforms, and versions, each emitting fields in its own dialect. Direct consumption of raw telemetry forces every downstream consumer to re-implement mapping, deduplication, and unit reconciliation, producing inconsistent results and a maintenance burden that scales with the number of sources. The Normalized Event exists to establish a single internal contract: every component downstream of the normalization layer MAY assume canonical structure, validated fields, and resolved identities (REQ-NORM-001). This separation is what makes the platform's domain-agnostic corpus-swapping (see [Volume III, Section III.2.13]) feasible — the corpus reasons over canonical events, not game-specific ones.

**Differentiation.** A Normalized Event is the cleaned and canonical descendant of a *Telemetry Event* (I.3.2.2); the transformation is one-way and lossy only in the sense of removing redundancy (deduplication) and game-specific naming. It is distinct from a *Behavioral Feature* (I.3.2.1), which is a computed summary *over* Normalized Events, not a single event. It is distinct from a *Knowledge Graph Entity* (I.3.2.6), which is a node in the behavioral graph constructed from events and features. The Normalized Event retains a reference to its source Telemetry Event for lineage (see [Volume II, Section II.2.2]).

**Cross-References.** See [Volume VI, Section VI.1.3] (Event Normalization Layer), [Volume II, Section II.2.1.2] (Event Normalization), [Volume II, Section II.3.3.1.b] (Normalized Events Zone).

**See also:** I.3.2.2 (Telemetry Event), I.3.2.1 (Behavioral Feature), I.3.3.1 (Event Normalization Layer), I.3.3.3 (Data Lake).

#### I.3.2.4 Player Model

**Definition.** A *Player Model* is the persistent, per-actor representation maintained by PsyForge that integrates a human actor's Behavioral Features, Embeddings, Knowledge Graph subgraph, and predictive inferences into a single queryable artifact. A Player Model is not a flat profile; it is a structured composite whose components are individually versioned and individually governed. See [Volume III, Section III.5.1].

**Rationale.** A behavioral-intelligence platform that reasons about humans must hold a faithful, current, and explainable representation of each human it models. Scattering this representation across feature stores, vector stores, and graph databases without a unifying abstraction forces every consumer (agents, simulation, dashboard) to reassemble it ad hoc, producing inconsistent views and untraceable inferences. The Player Model exists as the canonical, governed, single-queryable representation of an actor, with explicit confidence and uncertainty quantification, so that every downstream reasoning step cites the same source (REQ-PM-001). It also enforces the privacy principle: because the model is the governed abstraction, deletion and right-to-be-forgoken propagation has a single, well-defined target (see [Volume III, Section III.5.4]).

**Differentiation.** A Player Model represents an observed real human and is built from empirical data. It is distinct from a *Virtual Persona* (I.3.2.5), which is a synthetic or derived agent used inside simulation and which MAY be initialized from a Player Model but is not itself a record of a real human. It is distinct from a *Feature Vector* (I.3.2.14), which is one component of the model; from an *Embedding* (I.3.2.15), another component; and from a *Behavioral Archetype* (I.3.2.16), which is a population-level label that a Player Model MAY be assigned but which is not the model itself.

**Cross-References.** See [Volume III, Section III.5] (Player Modeling), [Volume III, Section III.5.1] (Model Overview), [Volume III, Section III.5.4] (Player Model Privacy).

**See also:** I.3.2.5 (Virtual Persona), I.3.2.14 (Feature Vector), I.3.2.15 (Embedding), I.3.2.16 (Behavioral Archetype), I.3.2.6 (Knowledge Graph Entity).

#### I.3.2.5 Virtual Persona

**Definition.** A *Virtual Persona* is a simulated agent that behaves according to a behavioral, emotional, motivational, and economic model, used inside the Simulation Engine to forecast outcomes of proposed changes without deploying them to real humans. A Virtual Persona MAY be derived from a Player Model (representative persona), constructed from a Behavioral Archetype (archetypal persona), or generated synthetically (novel persona). See [Volume IV, Section IV.2.1].

**Rationale.** The platform's core principle of *Simulation Before Deployment* (see [Volume I, Section I.2.1.3]) requires a population of agents that can stand in for real humans when predicting the effect of a game update, an economy rebalance, or a monetization change. Running experiments directly on live populations is slow, ethically fraught, and incapable of evaluating hundreds of candidate changes. The Virtual Persona exists to make forecasting tractable at scale — PsyForge runs up to ten million personas continuously through hundreds of candidate updates before shipping one patch (see [Volume IV, Section IV.9.1.1]) — while remaining faithful enough to observed behavior that predictions are actionable (REQ-SIM-001).

**Differentiation.** A Virtual Persona is a simulation-time actor and is distinct from a *Player Model* (I.3.2.4), which is the empirical record of a real human. A persona MAY be initialized from a model (via the *Simulated_As* edge, see [Volume III, Section III.1.3.13]) but diverges from it as the simulation progresses and the persona reacts to the simulated environment. A Virtual Persona is distinct from an *Agent* (I.3.2.11) in the agent-framework sense: a persona is a simulated player whose behavior is generated by behavioral models, whereas an Agent is an AI reasoning component (such as the Game Designer Agent) that produces recommendations. Personas are simulated *subjects*; agents are reasoning *analysts*.

**Cross-References.** See [Volume IV, Section IV.2] (Virtual Personas), [Volume IV, Section IV.2.1] (Persona Architecture), [Volume IV, Section IV.2.4] (Persona Validation), [Volume III, Section III.4.3.5] (Simulation Persona Initialization).

**See also:** I.3.2.4 (Player Model), I.3.2.10 (Simulation Run), I.3.2.16 (Behavioral Archetype), I.3.2.11 (Agent).

#### I.3.2.6 Knowledge Graph Entity

**Definition.** A *Knowledge Graph Entity* is a node in PsyForge's property-graph Knowledge Graph, typed according to the node taxonomy (Player, Behavior, Emotion, Motivation, Mechanic, Economy, Outcome, Session, Feature, Pattern, Event, Recommendation, Experiment, Simulation Result, Agent Decision, Corpus Concept, Domain Entity) and bearing typed edges to other entities that encode relationships — including causal edges. See [Volume III, Section III.1.2] and [Volume III, Section III.1.4].

**Rationale.** Behavioral intelligence requires relational reasoning: a recommendation that a mechanic change will improve retention is only defensible if the causal chain from Mechanic → Emotion → Motivation → Outcome is explicit and queryable. A flat feature store cannot express such chains; a correlation matrix cannot distinguish causation from coincidence. The Knowledge Graph Entity exists as the unit of relational structure so that reasoning, explanation, and causal inference operate over a shared, typed, evidence-backed graph rather than over ad hoc joins (REQ-KG-001). This is the substrate that makes PsyForge's causal-not-correlational differentiation concrete (see [Volume I, Section I.1.5.3]).

**Differentiation.** A Knowledge Graph Entity is a node, distinct from a *Behavioral Feature* (I.3.2.1), which is a signal that MAY be represented as a Feature-typed entity but is more generally a computed value. It is distinct from a *Corpus Concept* entity (a node of type Corpus Concept), which represents a theoretical construct from the Psychology Corpus rather than an observed datum. It is distinct from a *Player Model* (I.3.2.4), which is a per-actor composite artifact that *includes* a graph subgraph but is not itself a single entity. Edges, not nodes, carry the causal semantics central to the platform; an entity in isolation is structural, an entity with its edges is relational.

**Cross-References.** See [Volume III, Section III.1.2] (Node Types), [Volume III, Section III.1.3] (Edge Types), [Volume III, Section III.1.7] (Causal Chain Example), [Volume II, Section II.3.4] (Knowledge Graph Storage).

**See also:** I.3.2.7 (Behavioral Ontology), I.3.2.17 (Causal Chain), I.3.2.1 (Behavioral Feature), I.3.2.4 (Player Model), I.3.2.8 (Psychology Corpus).

#### I.3.2.7 Behavioral Ontology

**Definition.** A *Behavioral Ontology* is the formal, versioned taxonomy of behavior types, emotion types, motivation types, and the typed relationships among them, used to classify Knowledge Graph Entities and to constrain the edges that may connect them. The Behavioral Ontology is the controlled vocabulary layer of the Knowledge Graph: it defines what kinds of behaviors exist, how they relate to emotions and motivations, and which relationships are admissible. See [Volume III, Section III.1.2] and [Volume VII, Section VII.4.4.5].

**Rationale.** A graph without an ontology is a bag of nodes whose meaning is implicit and inconsistent across contributors. To reason reliably — for example, to assert that a "frustration" emotion is a valid trigger for a "rage-quit" behavior but not for a "purchase" behavior — the admissible node and edge types must be formally constrained and machine-checkable. The Behavioral Ontology exists to make the graph's semantics explicit, consistent, and validatable, so that agent reasoning over the graph is grounded in a shared taxonomy rather than in implicit assumptions that drift over time (REQ-ONT-001). It is the bridge between the empirical graph (observed) and the Psychology Corpus (theoretical).

**Differentiation.** The Behavioral Ontology is the *schema and taxonomy*; the *Knowledge Graph* is the *populated instance* of entities and edges conforming to that schema. The ontology is distinct from the *Psychology Corpus* (I.3.2.8), which supplies the theoretical content (motivation theories, cognitive biases, etc.) that the ontology structures; the ontology is the formal skeleton, the corpus is the substantive knowledge. It is distinct from a *Behavioral Archetype* (I.3.2.16), which is a clustering-derived population label, not an ontological category. The ontology is edited through the Behavioral Ontology Editor (see [Volume VII, Section VII.4.4.5]) under governance.

**Cross-References.** See [Volume III, Section III.1.2] (Node Types), [Volume III, Section III.1.3] (Edge Types), [Volume III, Section III.1.4] (Property Schemas), [Volume III, Section III.1.5.6] (Graph Consistency Validation).

**See also:** I.3.2.6 (Knowledge Graph Entity), I.3.2.8 (Psychology Corpus), I.3.2.16 (Behavioral Archetype), I.3.2.17 (Causal Chain).

#### I.3.2.8 Psychology Corpus

**Definition.** A *Psychology Corpus* is a versioned, citable body of domain knowledge — theories, taxonomies, empirical findings, and citation-tracked evidence — organized into domains (Game Psychology, Behavioral Economics, Motivation Theory, Cognitive Science, Game Design, Social Dynamics, UX Research, Marketing Science, Education, Decision Theory) that the platform consults to interpret observed behavior and to ground recommendations in established science. The corpus is domain-isolated and swappable, enabling the platform to operate across markets by exchanging the active corpus. See [Volume III, Section III.2].

**Rationale.** Behavioral interpretation without a grounding theory is pattern-matching that cannot explain itself and that overfits to the observed dataset. The platform's principles of *Evidence Over Intuition* (see [Volume I, Section I.2.1.7]) and *Psychographics Over Demographics* (see [Volume I, Section I.2.1.2]) require that every behavioral inference cite a theoretical basis. The Psychology Corpus exists to supply that basis: when an agent asserts that a player's behavior indicates loss aversion, the assertion MUST trace to a corpus entry citing prospect theory (REQ-CORP-001). The swappable design is what makes the engine domain-agnostic (see [Volume I, Section I.1.5.7]): the same platform serves games, marketing, education, HR, healthcare, and fintech by activating the relevant corpus.

**Differentiation.** The Psychology Corpus is the *knowledge base*; the *Behavioral Ontology* (I.3.2.7) is the *formal structure* into which corpus concepts are registered; the *Knowledge Graph* is the *populated instance* of observed entities conforming to that structure. The corpus is distinct from a *Corpus Domain* (I.3.3.7), which names an architectural isolation unit (a corpus *partition*) rather than the knowledge itself. Corpus concepts are registered into the graph via the *Belongs_To* edge (see [Volume III, Section III.1.3.14]) and carry citation tracking (see [Volume III, Section III.2.12.4]).

**Cross-References.** See [Volume III, Section III.2] (Psychology Corpus), [Volume III, Section III.2.1] (Corpus Architecture), [Volume III, Section III.2.12] (Corpus-to-Graph Mapping), [Volume III, Section III.2.13] (Domain-Agnostic Corpus Swapping).

**See also:** I.3.2.7 (Behavioral Ontology), I.3.2.6 (Knowledge Graph Entity), I.3.3.7 (Corpus Domain), I.3.2.12 (Specialist Agent).

#### I.3.2.9 Recommendation
