**Definition.** A *Recommendation* is the single, governed artifact through which any proposed change to a customer's product, configuration, or model is communicated for human review and, on approval, deployed. A Recommendation carries: a human-readable rationale; the evidence and causal chains supporting it; the simulation or experiment results predicting its effect; the Ethics Layer score card across all seven dimensions; and a structured, machine-actionable payload describing the change. See [Volume II, Section II.2.6] and [Volume VII, Section VII.4.2].

**Rationale.** The platform's foundational doctrine is that *nothing writes directly into production; everything flows through recommendation* (see [Volume I, Section I.1.4.5] and [Volume II, Section II.2.6.1]). Without a single, structured, audited artifact mediating between automated reasoning and live change, the platform would be an unaccountable autonomous system — ethically unacceptable and operationally dangerous. The Recommendation exists as the mandatory gateway: every agent conclusion, every simulation result, every optimization output MUST be packaged as a Recommendation, scored by the Ethics Layer, and reviewed by a human before any production write occurs (REQ-REC-001). This is the architectural embodiment of *Human-in-the-Loop* (see [Volume I, Section I.2.1.6]) and *Ethics by Architecture* (see [Volume I, Section I.2.1.8]).

**Differentiation.** A Recommendation is the *artifact under review*; a *Simulation Run* (I.3.2.10) is one of its *inputs*; an *Agent* (I.3.2.11) is one of its *producers*. It is distinct from an *Experiment* (I.3.4.2), which is the live validation that MAY follow a deployed Recommendation to measure whether the predicted effect materialized. A Recommendation is distinct from a raw agent output: agents produce proposals and analyses; the Orchestrator (I.3.2.13) assembles these into a governed Recommendation that then enters the Human Review State machine (I.3.2.19).

**Cross-References.** See [Volume II, Section II.2.6] (Recommendation Gate Architecture), [Volume VII, Section VII.4.2] (Recommendation Studio), [Volume VII, Section VII.5.8] (Recommendation API), [Volume I, Section I.5.2] (Ethics Scoring System).

**See also:** I.3.2.10 (Simulation Run), I.3.2.11 (Agent), I.3.2.13 (Orchestrator), I.3.2.18 (Manipulation Risk Score), I.3.2.19 (Human Review State), I.3.2.20 (Deployment Gate), I.3.4.1 (Recommendation Lifecycle).

#### I.3.2.10 Simulation Run

**Definition.** A *Simulation Run* is a single, reproducible execution of the Simulation Engine against a defined scenario: an initial state, a population of Virtual Personas, a set of parameters or proposed changes, and termination conditions, producing a structured result set of outcomes and metrics. A Simulation Run is identified, versioned, and persisted with full provenance so that any prediction can be traced to the exact run that produced it. See [Volume IV, Section IV.1] and [Volume IV, Section IV.1.2].

**Rationale.** Forecasting the effect of a change requires executing the future in simulation. A Simulation Run is the atomic unit of such execution: it bounds the scenario, fixes the inputs (including random seeds for reproducibility), and yields the predictions that feed Recommendations. Without a first-class, persisted, reproducible run artifact, predictions become unattributable oracles — a violation of *Truth Over Assumptions* and a blocker on the explainability requirements that bind agent outputs. The Simulation Run exists to make every prediction an auditable, replayable computational artifact (REQ-SIM-002). At scale, PsyForge executes runs of up to ten million personas through hundreds of candidate updates (see [Volume IV, Section IV.9.1.5]).

**Differentiation.** A Simulation Run is the *execution*; a *Virtual Persona* (I.3.2.5) is an *actor within* the run; a *Recommendation* (I.3.2.9) is the *governed artifact* that consumes the run's results. A Simulation Run is distinct from an *Experiment* (I.3.4.2): a simulation forecasts in silico, an experiment measures in vivo. A run is distinct from a *Benchmark Suite* (I.3.4.4) evaluation, which scores the simulation engine itself rather than a customer scenario. Runs are stored as Simulation Result entities in the Knowledge Graph (see [Volume III, Section III.1.2.14]) and persisted per the storage policy in [Volume II, Section II.3.3].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume IV, Section IV.1] (Simulation Architecture), [Volume IV, Section IV.1.3] (Simulation Configuration), [Volume IV, Section IV.1.2.5] (Simulation Result Storage), [Volume IV, Section IV.9] (Simulation at Scale).

**See also:** I.3.2.5 (Virtual Persona), I.3.2.9 (Recommendation), I.3.2.15 (Embedding), I.3.4.2 (Experiment Framework), I.3.4.3 (Evaluation Corpus).

#### I.3.2.11 Agent

**Definition.** An *Agent* is an AI reasoning component within PsyForge's agent framework that, given assembled context, produces structured analytical output — interpretations, predictions, hypotheses, or proposed changes — by combining a large language model with tools, memory, and evaluation criteria. An Agent operates under a defined prompt, a permitted tool set, scoped data access, and per-agent evaluation criteria. See [Volume V, Section V.1.1].

**Rationale.** The reasoning workload of a behavioral-intelligence platform — interpreting behavior, designing economies, planning experiments, assessing ethics — is too diverse and too specialized for a single monolithic model. Decomposing reasoning into named, scoped, evaluable Agents enables specialization (each agent reasons within its discipline), accountability (each agent's outputs are attributable and benchmarked), and safety (each agent's tool and data access is least-privilege). The Agent exists as the unit of AI reasoning so that the platform's intelligence is composable, governable, and individually verifiable rather than opaque (REQ-AGT-001). This multi-agent architecture is a stated strategic differentiator (see [Volume I, Section I.1.5.4]).

**Differentiation.** An Agent is a *reasoning component*; a *Virtual Persona* (I.3.2.5) is a *simulated subject*. Agents analyze; personas are analyzed. An Agent is distinct from a *Specialist Agent* (I.3.2.12), which is the typed, role-specific subclass (Psychologist, Economist, etc.); "Agent" is the generic term, "Specialist Agent" the specific. An Agent is distinct from the *Orchestrator* (I.3.2.13), which coordinates agents but is not itself a domain reasoner. Agent outputs flow into Recommendations via the Orchestrator; agents do not write to production (see [Volume II, Section II.2.6.1]).

**Cross-References.** See [Volume V, Section V.1] (Agent Architecture), [Volume V, Section V.1.1] (Agent Model Overview), [Volume V, Section V.1.4] (Agent Tools), [Volume V, Section V.1.6] (Agent Failure Modes).

**See also:** I.3.2.12 (Specialist Agent), I.3.2.13 (Orchestrator), I.3.2.9 (Recommendation), I.3.2.5 (Virtual Persona), I.3.3.8 (Local LLM Ecosystem).

#### I.3.2.12 Specialist Agent

**Definition.** A *Specialist Agent* is an Agent typed by a disciplinary role and equipped with role-specific prompts, tools, corpus-domain access, and evaluation criteria. PsyForge's v1.0 specialist catalog includes the Chief Research Officer, Psychologist, Behavioral Economist, Statistician, Economy Designer, Game Designer, Narrative Designer, Monetization Advisor, Ethics Officer, and Experiment Planner. See [Volume V, Parts V.3 through V.12].

**Rationale.** Generalist reasoning over behavioral data produces shallow, ungrounded outputs: a single agent cannot simultaneously hold the rigor of a statistician, the domain knowledge of a psychologist, and the ethical vigilance of an ethics officer. The Specialist Agent exists to enforce disciplinary depth — each specialist reasons within its expertise, cites the relevant corpus domain, and is evaluated against discipline-appropriate criteria (REQ-AGT-002). This specialization is what makes agent outputs trustworthy enough to feed governed Recommendations. The Ethics Officer Agent, in particular, holds veto authority over ethically problematic recommendations (see [Volume V, Section V.11.5]), embodying *Ethics by Architecture*.

**Differentiation.** A Specialist Agent is a *typed instance* of the generic *Agent* (I.3.2.11); every Specialist Agent is an Agent, not every Agent is a Specialist Agent (custom and third-party agents exist, see [Volume V, Section V.14]). It is distinct from the *Orchestrator* (I.3.2.13), which dispatches specialists but is not itself a specialist. Specialists are distinguished from one another by role, tool access, and corpus scope (see the per-agent tool and access sections, e.g., [Volume V, Section V.4.3]). Third-party Specialist Agents are sandboxed and certified per [Volume V, Section V.14.2].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume V, Part V.3] through [Volume V, Part V.12] (individual specialist specifications), [Volume V, Section V.13] (Agent Interaction Specifications), [Volume V, Section V.14] (Agent Extension Framework).

**See also:** I.3.2.11 (Agent), I.3.2.13 (Orchestrator), I.3.2.8 (Psychology Corpus), I.3.2.18 (Manipulation Risk Score), I.3.3.6 (Ethics Layer).

#### I.3.2.13 Orchestrator

**Definition.** The *Orchestrator* is the central coordination component of the agent framework that receives a reasoning request, classifies it, selects and dispatches the appropriate Specialist Agents (in parallel or sequence), assembles their context, collects and merges their outputs, resolves conflicts, triggers Ethics Layer scoring, and assembles the resulting draft Recommendation for human review. The Orchestrator operates as a state machine. See [Volume V, Section V.2].

**Rationale.** Multi-agent systems without a coordinator suffer from unmanaged parallelism, unresolved conflicts, and unattributed synthesis: two specialists may produce contradictory analyses with no defined resolution path, and the human reviewer receives an incoherent bundle rather than a coherent proposal. The Orchestrator exists as the single accountable coordinator that turns a set of agent outputs into one governed Recommendation, with a defined workflow, conflict-resolution rules, and escalation to human review when agents disagree irreconcilably (REQ-AGT-003). It is the structural mechanism that makes the multi-agent architecture safe and reviewable rather than chaotic.

**Differentiation.** The Orchestrator is a *coordinator*, not a *domain reasoner*; it does not itself interpret behavior or design economies. It is distinct from a *Specialist Agent* (I.3.2.12), which it dispatches; from an *Agent* (I.3.2.11) generically; and from the *Recommendation Engine* (I.3.3.5), which is the broader system that consumes orchestrator output, applies ethics scoring, and manages the recommendation lifecycle. The Orchestrator's state machine (Idle, Planning, Dispatching, Collecting, Synthesizing, Reviewing, Complete) is specified in [Volume V, Section V.2.3].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume V, Section V.2] (Orchestrator), [Volume V, Section V.2.2] (Orchestration Workflow), [Volume V, Section V.2.3] (Orchestrator State Machine), [Volume V, Section V.1.2] (Agent Communication Protocol).

**See also:** I.3.2.11 (Agent), I.3.2.12 (Specialist Agent), I.3.2.9 (Recommendation), I.3.3.5 (Recommendation Engine), I.3.3.6 (Ethics Layer).

#### I.3.2.14 Feature Vector

**Definition.** A *Feature Vector* is the ordered, typed, numeric array assembled from a defined set of Behavioral Feature values for a single actor (or session, or aggregate unit), normalized and standardized according to a versioned schema, and consumed as input by predictive models, embedding generators, and similarity computations. A Feature Vector is the materialized, machine-consumable form of a feature set. See [Volume III, Section III.3.5].

**Rationale.** Models require fixed-shape, ordered, numeric input. Behavioral Features, as individually named signals, are not directly consumable by most models; they must be assembled into a vector with consistent ordering, missing-value handling, and normalization. The Feature Vector exists as the contract between the Feature Engineering Pipeline and downstream model and embedding consumers, so that a model trained against vector schema version N is guaranteed compatible with vectors produced under that schema (REQ-FEAT-002). This contract is what makes models reproducible, versionable, and swappable.

**Differentiation.** A Feature Vector is the *assembled array*; a *Behavioral Feature* (I.3.2.1) is a *single named signal* that contributes one (or more) dimensions to the vector. It is distinct from an *Embedding* (I.3.2.15), which is a *learned* dense representation produced by a model from features, sequences, or graphs — the vector is hand-defined and interpretable per dimension, the embedding is learned and not directly interpretable. It is distinct from a *Player Model* (I.3.2.4), which *contains* a feature vector as one component among several. Feature vectors are versioned independently of features per [Volume III, Section III.3.5.4].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume III, Section III.3.5] (Feature Vector Assembly), [Volume III, Section III.3.5.1] (Vector Schema and Ordering), [Volume III, Section III.4.2.1] (Player Embeddings from Feature Vectors), [Volume II, Section II.3.3.1.c] (Features Zone).

**See also:** I.3.2.1 (Behavioral Feature), I.3.2.15 (Embedding), I.3.2.4 (Player Model), I.3.3.4 (Feature Engineering Pipeline).

#### I.3.2.15 Embedding

**Definition.** An *Embedding* is a learned dense vector representation of an entity — a player, a behavioral sequence, a graph node, a corpus concept, or a cross-modal combination — produced by a trained embedding model such that semantically similar entities map to nearby points in the embedding space. Embeddings are stored in a vector database and queried via similarity search. See [Volume III, Section III.4].

**Rationale.** Hand-defined feature vectors capture interpretable signals but cannot compactly represent the full structure of behavioral sequences, graph topology, or corpus semantics. Learned embeddings complement features by capturing latent structure that is expensive or impossible to specify by hand, enabling similarity search, clustering, anomaly detection, and persona initialization at scale (REQ-EMB-001). The combination of interpretable features (for explanation and governance) and learned embeddings (for scale and latent structure) is a deliberate design decision: features keep the system explainable; embeddings keep it tractable. See [Volume III, Section III.4.3] for use cases.

**Differentiation.** An Embedding is *learned and dense*; a *Feature Vector* (I.3.2.14) is *hand-defined and interpretable*. The two are complementary, not interchangeable: a Player Model (I.3.2.4) typically contains both. An Embedding is distinct from a *Behavioral Feature* (I.3.2.1): a feature is a named signal; an embedding is a learned coordinate. Embedding dimensionality is governed separately from feature vector schema (see [Volume III, Section III.4.1.3]); migration on schema change is specified in [Volume III, Section III.4.4.3]. Embeddings initialize Virtual Personas (see [Volume III, Section III.4.3.5]).

**Cross-References.** See [Volume III, Section III.4] (Embedding Architecture), [Volume III, Section III.4.2] (Embedding Generation), [Volume III, Section III.4.4] (Embedding Management), [Volume II, Section II.3.5] (Vector Storage).

**See also:** I.3.2.1 (Behavioral Feature), I.3.2.14 (Feature Vector), I.3.2.4 (Player Model), I.3.2.5 (Virtual Persona), I.3.2.16 (Behavioral Archetype), II.3.5 (Vector Storage).

#### I.3.2.16 Behavioral Archetype

**Definition.** A *Behavioral Archetype* is a population-level label, discovered by clustering over Feature Vectors and Embeddings, that names a recurrent pattern of behavior, emotion, and motivation shared by a segment of the player population (e.g., "Explorer," "Achiever," "Socializer," "Min-Maxer"). Archetypes are versioned and MAY be standard (platform-defined) or custom (studio-defined). See [Volume III, Section III.5.2].

**Rationale.** Reasoning about every individual player is intractable at population scale, and reasoning about undifferentiated aggregates loses the behavioral resolution that is the platform's value. Behavioral Archetypes exist as the intermediate granularity: segments large enough to reason about strategically and small enough to be behaviorally meaningful. They enable disproportionate-impact analysis (does this update frustrate explorers disproportionately?), archetype-targeted recommendations, and simulation persona population composition (see [Volume IV, Section IV.2.2.4]). Archetypes also provide a vocabulary that designers and executives share, bridging data and design (REQ-ARCH-001).

**Differentiation.** A Behavioral Archetype is a *population-level clustering label*; a *Player Model* (I.3.2.4) is an *individual-level representation* that MAY be assigned one or more archetype labels. It is distinct from a *Virtual Persona* (I.3.2.5), which MAY be *constructed from* an archetype (archetypal persona, see [Volume IV, Section IV.2.2.2]) but is a simulation actor, not a label. It is distinct from a *Behavioral Ontology* (I.3.2.7) category: ontology categories are formally defined types; archetypes are data-discovered segments. Archetype drift and transition are tracked per [Volume III, Section III.5.2.3].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume III, Section III.5.2] (Behavioral Archetypes), [Volume III, Section III.4.3.2] (Behavioral Archetype Discovery), [Volume IV, Section IV.2.2.2] (From Archetypes), [Volume IV, Section IV.5.2.4] (Disproportionate Impact Analysis).

**See also:** I.3.2.4 (Player Model), I.3.2.5 (Virtual Persona), I.3.2.14 (Feature Vector), I.3.2.15 (Embedding), I.3.2.7 (Behavioral Ontology).

#### I.3.2.17 Causal Chain

**Definition.** A *Causal Chain* is a directed path through the Knowledge Graph, traversing typed causal edges, that connects an upstream cause (e.g., a Mechanic change) to a downstream effect (e.g., an Outcome such as retention), with each edge carrying evidence and a confidence estimate. Causal Chains are first-class queryable artifacts, distinct from mere correlations. See [Volume III, Section III.1.7] and [Volume III, Section III.7.2].

**Rationale.** The platform's stated differentiation is causal, not correlational, reasoning (see [Volume I, Section I.1.5.3]). A correlation between a feature and an outcome cannot justify a recommendation, because intervening on the feature may not produce the outcome (confounding, common cause). A Causal Chain exists to make the intervention logic explicit and queryable: a recommendation that changing mechanic M will improve retention R is defensible only if a causal path M → ... → R exists, is supported by evidence, and survives confounder analysis (REQ-CAUS-001). The canonical example — Housing → Ownership → Identity → Community → Retention — is walked through in [Volume III, Section III.1.7].

**Differentiation.** A Causal Chain is a *path with causal semantics*; a generic Knowledge Graph path (e.g., a *Correlates_With* traversal) is not. It is distinct from a *Behavioral Feature* (I.3.2.1), which is a node-level signal, not a path. It is distinct from a *Simulation Run* (I.3.2.10) result, which *estimates* an effect numerically; the causal chain *explains* why the effect occurs structurally. Causal inference methodology (do-calculus, counterfactuals, confounder control) is specified in [Volume III, Section III.7.2].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume III, Section III.1.7] (Causal Chain Example), [Volume III, Section III.7.2] (Causal Inference Framework), [Volume III, Section III.1.3.8] (Causes Edge), [Volume III, Section III.1.6.2] (Causal Chain Queries).

**See also:** I.3.2.6 (Knowledge Graph Entity), I.3.2.9 (Recommendation), I.3.2.10 (Simulation Run), I.3.3.4 (Feature Engineering Pipeline), III.7.3 (Explainability Layer).

#### I.3.2.18 Manipulation Risk Score

**Definition.** The *Manipulation Risk Score* is one of the seven dimensions of the Ethics Layer score card, quantifying the degree to which a Recommendation, if deployed, would exploit a human actor's cognitive biases, vulnerabilities, or informational asymmetries to influence behavior in a way that diverges from the actor's informed interests. The score is produced by the Ethics Layer and, critically, by the Ethics Officer Agent. See [Volume I, Section I.5.2.1.e] and [Volume V, Section V.11.1.2].

**Rationale.** A behavioral-intelligence platform that optimizes engagement and revenue could, by default, become an instrument of manipulation — nudging players toward spending or habits that serve the operator against the player's welfare. The platform's ethical charter (see [Volume I, Section I.5.1.3]) explicitly refuses to optimize for manipulation. The Manipulation Risk Score exists as a quantitative, gating constraint: a Recommendation whose score exceeds threshold MUST NOT deploy without human override, and the Ethics Officer Agent holds veto authority (see [Volume V, Section V.11.5]) (REQ-ETH-001). This is the operational embodiment of *Ethics by Architecture* (see [Volume I, Section I.2.1.8]) — the boundary is enforced structurally, not by policy aspiration.

**Differentiation.** The Manipulation Risk Score is one *dimension* of the seven-dimension Ethics Score Card (the others being Retention Impact, Revenue Impact, Player Satisfaction, Fairness, Long-Term Trust, Regulatory Risk; see [Volume I, Section I.5.2.1]). It is distinct from the *Compliance Matrix* (I.3.4.5), which maps regulatory rather than ethical obligations, though high manipulation risk often correlates with regulatory risk. It is distinct from a *Human Review State* (I.3.2.19): the score is an *input* to review; the review state is the *lifecycle position* of the Recommendation under review. Scoring methodology and thresholds are specified in [Volume I, Section I.5.2.2] and [Volume I, Section I.5.2.3].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume I, Section I.5.2.1.e] (Manipulation Risk), [Volume I, Section I.5.1.3] (Manipulation Boundary), [Volume V, Section V.11] (Ethics Officer Agent), [Volume I, Section I.5.2.3] (Score Thresholds and Gating Rules).

**See also:** I.3.2.9 (Recommendation), I.3.2.19 (Human Review State), I.3.2.20 (Deployment Gate), I.3.3.6 (Ethics Layer), I.3.4.5 (Compliance Matrix).

#### I.3.2.19 Human Review State

**Definition.** A *Human Review State* is one of the states in the Human Review State Machine that governs the lifecycle of a Recommendation from proposal through deployment and possible rollback. The states are: Proposed, Reviewed, Rejected, Modified, Approved, Deployed, and Rolled Back. State transitions are guarded, audited, and irreversible without a new audited transition. See [Volume II, Section II.2.6.2].

**Rationale.** The no-direct-production-write doctrine (see [Volume II, Section II.2.6.1]) requires that every change pass through human review. Without a formal state machine, "review" is an unenforced social process: changes leak to production unreviewed, modifications are untracked, and rollbacks are ad hoc. The Human Review State Machine exists to make review a structural, auditable, code-enforced gate rather than a procedural hope (REQ-REC-002). Each transition records who, when, from-state, to-state, and rationale, producing the audit trail required for regulatory compliance (see [Volume IX, Section IX.4.2.6]) and for post-incident review.

**Differentiation.** A Human Review State is the *lifecycle position* of a *Recommendation* (I.3.2.9); it is not a property of the change itself but of the governed artifact. It is distinct from the *Manipulation Risk Score* (I.3.2.18), which is an *input* that may influence a transition (e.g., a high score may force Reviewed → Rejected unless overridden). It is distinct from a *Deployment Gate* (I.3.2.20): the review state tracks the Recommendation's progress; the deployment gate is the technical enforcement point that physically prevents writes unless the state is Approved. The state machine is specified in [Volume II, Section II.2.6.2]; transition rules in [Volume II, Section II.2.6.3].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume II, Section II.2.6.2] (Human Review State Machine), [Volume II, Section II.2.6.3] (State Transition Rules and Guards), [Volume II, Section II.2.6.4] (Gate Decision Audit Trail), [Volume II, Section II.2.6.5] (Gate Bypass Prevention).

**See also:** I.3.2.9 (Recommendation), I.3.2.20 (Deployment Gate), I.3.2.18 (Manipulation Risk Score), I.3.4.1 (Recommendation Lifecycle), I.3.3.5 (Recommendation Engine).

#### I.3.2.20 Deployment Gate

**Definition.** A *Deployment Gate* is the technical enforcement point — implemented at the code and infrastructure level — that physically prevents any system change from being written to a customer's production environment unless the corresponding Recommendation is in the Approved Human Review State, has passed all Ethics Layer thresholds, and has not been vetoed by the Ethics Officer Agent. The gate is code-level, not policy-level, and includes bypass-prevention and emergency-override controls. See [Volume II, Section II.2.6.1] and [Volume II, Section II.2.6.5].

**Rationale.** A review policy that is not technically enforced is a suggestion. The platform's safety depends on the guarantee that no agent, no simulation, no optimization output, and no administrative convenience can write to production except through the governed Recommendation path. The Deployment Gate exists as the architectural realization of that guarantee: it is the mechanism by which *Ethics by Architecture* and *Human-in-the-Loop* become structural invariants rather than aspirational principles (REQ-REC-003). Bypass is technically prevented; the only exception is a human-only, time-limited, fully-audited emergency override (see [Volume II, Section II.2.6.6]).

**Differentiation.** The Deployment Gate is the *enforcement mechanism*; the *Human Review State* (I.3.2.19) is the *lifecycle state* the gate reads to decide whether to permit a write; the *Recommendation* (I.3.2.9) is the *artifact* under review. It is distinct from the *Ethics Layer* (I.3.3.6), which *produces* the scores the gate consults; the gate is the consumer, the layer is the producer. It is distinct from CI/CD principle-compliance checks (see [Volume I, Section I.2.3.2]), which are broader; the Deployment Gate is the specific write-prohibition enforcement point. Code-level write prohibition is specified in [Volume II, Section II.2.6.1.c].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume II, Section II.2.6.1] (No-Direct-Production-Write Doctrine), [Volume II, Section II.2.6.5] (Gate Bypass Prevention), [Volume II, Section II.2.6.6] (Emergency Override Process), [Volume I, Section I.5.4.4] (Ethics Gates in Recommendation Deployment).

**See also:** I.3.2.9 (Recommendation), I.3.2.19 (Human Review State), I.3.2.18 (Manipulation Risk Score), I.3.3.6 (Ethics Layer), I.3.4.1 (Recommendation Lifecycle).

---

### I.3.3 System Architecture Terms

#### I.3.3.1 Event Normalization Layer

**Definition.** The *Event Normalization Layer* is the pipeline stage that ingests Telemetry Events from the telemetry collector and emits Normalized Events conforming to the canonical internal schema. It performs field mapping and transformation, unit standardization, time-zone normalization, ID resolution and deduplication, enrichment with derived fields, and validation with rejection handling. See [Volume VI, Section VI.1.3] and [Volume II, Section II.2.1.2].

**Rationale.** Heterogeneous telemetry sources emit in their own dialects; downstream consumers cannot be expected to each re-implement reconciliation. The Event Normalization Layer exists as the single boundary at which source-specific form is converted to canonical form, establishing the internal contract on which every downstream component — Feature Engineering Pipeline, Knowledge Graph construction, Operational Storage — may rely (REQ-NORM-002). Centralizing normalization also centralizes quality enforcement (completeness, accuracy, timeliness, consistency checks; see [Volume VI, Section VI.1.4]) and lineage tracking (see [Volume II, Section II.2.2]).

**Differentiation.** The Event Normalization Layer is the *pipeline stage*; a *Normalized Event* (I.3.2.3) is the *artifact it produces*; a *Telemetry Event* (I.3.2.2) is the *artifact it consumes*. It is distinct from the *Feature Engineering Pipeline* (I.3.3.4), which operates downstream of normalization and computes Behavioral Features *over* normalized events rather than cleaning individual events. It is distinct from the telemetry collector (see [Volume VI, Section VI.1.1.1]), which transports events but does not transform them. The layer is depicted in [Volume II, Diagram D.18].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume VI, Section VI.1.3] (Event Normalization Layer), [Volume II, Section II.2.1.2] (Event Normalization), [Volume II, Section II.3.3.1.b] (Normalized Events Zone), [Volume II, Diagram D.18] (Event Normalization Pipeline Diagram).

**See also:** I.3.2.2 (Telemetry Event), I.3.2.3 (Normalized Event), I.3.3.2 (Operational Storage), I.3.3.3 (Data Lake), I.3.3.4 (Feature Engineering Pipeline).

#### I.3.3.2 Operational Storage

**Definition.** *Operational Storage* is the hot-tier storage substrate holding data that must be served to live system operations with low latency: current Player Models, in-flight Recommendations, active simulation state, session data, and dashboard query caches. Operational Storage is optimized for read/write latency and freshness rather than for analytical scan over history, and is technically and contractually distinct from the Data Lake. See [Volume II, Section II.3.2].

**Rationale.** Conflating operational and analytical workloads in a single store produces contention: analytical scans starve live reads, and freshness requirements force constant writes that degrade scan performance. Separating Operational Storage from the Data Lake follows the polyglot persistence model (see [Volume II, Section II.3.1.1]) and lets each tier be optimized, scaled, and costed for its access pattern (REQ-STOR-001). Operational Storage holds the *current truth*; the Data Lake holds the *historical truth*; reconciliation between them is governed by the data lifecycle (see [Volume II, Section II.3.1.3]).

**Differentiation.** Operational Storage is the *hot, low-latency* tier; the *Data Lake* (I.3.3.3) is the *cold/warm, analytical* tier. It is distinct from *Caching Layer* (see [Volume II, Section II.3.8]), which is ephemeral and derived, whereas Operational Storage is durable and authoritative for current state. It is distinct from *Knowledge Graph Storage* (see [Volume II, Section II.3.4]) and *Vector Storage* (see [Volume II, Section II.3.5]), which are specialized stores that may be co-located with but are not identical to Operational Storage. Technology selection and schema are specified in [Volume II, Section II.3.2.2] and [Volume II, Section II.3.2.3].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume II, Section II.3.2] (Operational Storage), [Volume II, Section II.3.1.1] (Polyglot Persistence Model), [Volume II, Section II.3.1.2] (Data Classification by Storage Tier), [Volume II, Section II.3.2.6] (Replication and Consistency Model).

**See also:** I.3.3.3 (Data Lake), I.3.3.1 (Event Normalization Layer), I.3.2.4 (Player Model), I.3.2.9 (Recommendation).

#### I.3.3.3 Data Lake

**Definition.** The *Data Lake* is the cold/warm-tier analytical storage substrate holding the historical, append-mostly record of the platform's data, organized into zones (Raw Events, Normalized Events, Features, Embeddings, Behavior Graph, Training Sets, Long-Term Archive). The Data Lake is optimized for analytical scan, batch processing, and long-term retention rather than for low-latency point reads. See [Volume II, Section II.3.3].

**Rationale.** Behavioral intelligence is inherently historical: detecting drift, training models, backtesting simulations, and auditing recommendations all require access to the full historical record, often spanning years. Operational Storage cannot economically or performantly hold this volume. The Data Lake exists as the durable, zone-partitioned, retention-governed repository of the platform's history, with explicit zones that encode the data's stage of processing and intended use (REQ-STOR-002). Zoning also enforces the right-to-be-forgotten: deletion propagates across zones per [Volume II, Section II.3.3.6].

**Differentiation.** The Data Lake is the *cold/warm analytical* tier; *Operational Storage* (I.3.3.2) is the *hot operational* tier. The Data Lake is distinct from *Knowledge Graph Storage* (see [Volume II, Section II.3.4]), which is the live graph; the Behavior Graph *Zone* within the Data Lake (see [Volume II, Section II.3.3.1.e]) is a historical snapshot/derivative, not the live graph. It is distinct from object storage for artifacts (models, reports; see [Volume II, Section II.3.7]), which holds discrete artifacts rather than tabular/analytical data. Partitioning and compaction are specified in [Volume II, Section II.3.3.3] and [Volume II, Section II.3.3.4].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume II, Section II.3.3] (Data Lake), [Volume II, Section II.3.3.1] (Data Lake Layers), [Volume II, Section II.3.3.5] (Data Retention Policy by Zone), [Volume II, Section II.3.3.6] (Data Purge and Right-to-Be-Forgotten), [Volume II, Diagram D.17] (Data Lake Zone Diagram).

**See also:** I.3.3.2 (Operational Storage), I.3.2.3 (Normalized Event), I.3.3.4 (Feature Engineering Pipeline), I.3.2.15 (Embedding), I.3.2.6 (Knowledge Graph Entity).

#### I.3.3.4 Feature Engineering Pipeline

**Definition.** The *Feature Engineering Pipeline* is the system that computes Behavioral Features from Normalized Events, according to definitions expressed in a Feature Definition DSL, in real-time and batch modes, with windowing (short, medium, long-term), dependency-ordered computation, caching and materialization, backfilling, and schema evolution. The pipeline emits Feature Vectors and persists features to the Data Lake Features Zone. See [Volume III, Section III.3.3].

**Rationale.** Features must be computed consistently, reproducibly, and at scale across streaming and batch contexts; ad hoc computation by each consumer produces inconsistent values for the same nominal feature. The Feature Engineering Pipeline exists as the single, governed producer of features, ensuring that a feature named X has one definition, one computation, and one validated value at any point in time across the platform (REQ-FEAT-003). It is also the operational vehicle for the Behavioral Feature Engine, a declared IP asset (see [Volume I, Section I.1.5.1] and [Volume III, Section III.3.1.2]). Feature validation (statistical, predictive, causal, stability, bias, drift; see [Volume III, Section III.3.4]) is enforced within the pipeline.

**Differentiation.** The Feature Engineering Pipeline is the *producer system*; a *Behavioral Feature* (I.3.2.1) is a *defined signal* it produces; a *Feature Vector* (I.3.2.14) is the *assembled output* it materializes for consumers. It is distinct from the *Event Normalization Layer* (I.3.3.1), which is the preceding stage that supplies its input. It is distinct from the *Embedding Architecture* (see [Volume III, Section III.4]), which consumes features to produce embeddings; the pipeline produces interpretable signals, the embedding subsystem produces learned representations. Custom feature extractors may be contributed as plugins (see [Volume VII, Section VII.6.2.1]).

**Cross-References.** See [Volume III, Section III.3] (Feature Engineering Pipeline), [Volume III, Section III.3.3] (Feature Extraction Architecture), [Volume III, Section III.3.4] (Feature Validation), [Volume III, Section III.3.5] (Feature Vector Assembly), [Volume II, Diagram D.3] (Feature Pipeline Diagram, D.III.3).

**See also:** I.3.2.1 (Behavioral Feature), I.3.2.2 (Telemetry Event), I.3.2.3 (Normalized Event), I.3.2.14 (Feature Vector), I.3.3.1 (Event Normalization Layer).

#### I.3.3.5 Recommendation Engine

**Definition.** The *Recommendation Engine* is the subsystem that consumes Orchestrator output, Simulation Run results, agent analyses, and Knowledge Graph evidence, applies Ethics Layer scoring, assembles the governed Recommendation artifact, and manages the Recommendation through the Human Review State Machine to the Deployment Gate. It is the bridge between automated reasoning and governed human review. See [Volume VII, Section VII.4.2] and [Volume II, Section II.2.6].

**Rationale.** The platform's no-direct-production-write doctrine requires a single, accountable subsystem that turns analytical outputs into governed, deployable artifacts. Scattering this responsibility across agents would make review inconsistent and audit impossible. The Recommendation Engine exists as the authoritative assembler and lifecycle manager of Recommendations, ensuring every change reaching the Deployment Gate carries complete evidence, ethics scores, and audit trail (REQ-REC-004). It exposes the Recommendation API (see [Volume VII, Section VII.5.8]) consumed by the dashboard and studio tools.

**Differentiation.** The Recommendation Engine is the *lifecycle and assembly system*; an *Agent* (I.3.2.11) is a *producer of inputs*; the *Orchestrator* (I.3.2.13) is the *coordinator* of agents whose output the engine consumes; a *Recommendation* (I.3.2.9) is the *artifact* the engine produces and manages. It is distinct from the *Ethics Layer* (I.3.3.6), which it *invokes* for scoring; the engine assembles, the layer scores. It is distinct from the *Deployment Gate* (I.3.2.20), which *enforces* the write prohibition the engine respects. The engine's API surface is specified in [Volume VII, Section VII.5.8].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume II, Section II.2.6] (Recommendation Gate Architecture), [Volume VII, Section VII.4.2] (Recommendation Studio), [Volume VII, Section VII.5.8] (Recommendation API), [Volume II, Diagram D.7] (Recommendation Gate Architecture).

**See also:** I.3.2.9 (Recommendation), I.3.2.13 (Orchestrator), I.3.2.19 (Human Review State), I.3.2.20 (Deployment Gate), I.3.3.6 (Ethics Layer), I.3.4.1 (Recommendation Lifecycle).

#### I.3.3.6 Ethics Layer

**Definition.** The *Ethics Layer* is the architectural subsystem that scores every Recommendation across seven dimensions (Retention Impact, Revenue Impact, Player Satisfaction, Fairness, Manipulation Risk, Long-Term Trust, Regulatory Risk), enforces score thresholds as gating constraints, maintains the score audit trail, and hosts the Ethics Officer Agent's veto authority. The Ethics Layer is invoked by the Orchestrator and consulted by the Deployment Gate. See [Volume I, Section I.5.4] and [Volume I, Section I.5.2].

**Rationale.** Ethics bolted on as policy is bypassable; ethics enforced structurally is not. The Ethics Layer exists as the architectural embodiment of *Ethics by Architecture* (see [Volume I, Section I.2.1.8]), ensuring that no Recommendation reaches deployment without a complete, threshold-checked, audited ethics score card (REQ-ETH-002). Its placement in the data pipeline (see [Volume I, Section I.5.4.1]) — between reasoning and deployment — is deliberate: it cannot be bypassed by the reasoning path because the Deployment Gate consults its outputs. The seven dimensions and their weighting are specified in [Volume I, Section I.5.2.2].

**Differentiation.** The Ethics Layer is the *scoring and gating subsystem*; the *Manipulation Risk Score* (I.3.2.18) is *one dimension* it produces; the *Ethics Officer Agent* (see [Volume V, Section V.11]) is the *agent* that operates within and is hosted by the layer. It is distinct from the *Compliance Matrix* (I.3.4.5), which maps *regulatory* obligations; the Ethics Layer addresses *ethical* obligations, which are broader than legal minimums (see [Volume I, Section I.5.5.3]). It is distinct from the *Deployment Gate* (I.3.2.20), which *consumes* the layer's outputs to enforce gating. Override of an ethics veto is human-only (see [Volume V, Section V.11.5.4]).

**Cross-References.** See [Volume I, Section I.5.2] (Ethics Scoring System), [Volume I, Section I.5.4] (Ethics in the Architecture), [Volume I, Section I.5.4.1] (Ethics Layer in the Data Pipeline), [Volume V, Section V.11] (Ethics Officer Agent), [Volume I, Diagram D.I.2] (Ethics Scoring System Flowchart).

**See also:** I.3.2.9 (Recommendation), I.3.2.18 (Manipulation Risk Score), I.3.2.20 (Deployment Gate), I.3.2.13 (Orchestrator), I.3.4.5 (Compliance Matrix).

#### I.3.3.7 Corpus Domain

**Definition.** A *Corpus Domain* is an isolated partition of the Psychology Corpus corresponding to a discipline (Game Psychology, Behavioral Economics, Motivation Theory, Cognitive Science, Game Design, Social Dynamics, UX Research, Marketing Science, Education, Decision Theory), with domain-isolated storage, versioning, and cross-domain reference controls. The active set of Corpus Domains determines the market the platform serves, and domains are swappable to retarget the platform. See [Volume III, Section III.2.1.2] and [Volume III, Section III.2.13].

**Rationale.** Mixing knowledge from all disciplines into a single unpartitioned corpus produces cross-contamination: a concept from marketing applied in a healthcare context may be invalid or harmful. Domain isolation exists to keep each discipline's knowledge internally consistent, independently versionable, and independently reviewable, while permitting controlled cross-domain references where theory legitimately bridges disciplines (REQ-CORP-002). The swappable-domain design is what makes the engine domain-agnostic: the same platform serves games, marketing, education, HR, healthcare, and fintech by activating the relevant domain set (see [Volume I, Section I.1.5.7] and [Volume III, Section III.2.13.4]).

**Differentiation.** A Corpus Domain is the *architectural isolation unit* (a corpus partition); the *Psychology Corpus* (I.3.2.8) is the *body of knowledge*, of which domains are partitions. It is distinct from a *Behavioral Ontology* (I.3.2.7), which is the formal taxonomy structuring concepts within and across domains. It is distinct from a *Knowledge Graph Entity* of type Corpus Concept (see [Volume III, Section III.1.2.16]), which is the *graph node* registered from a corpus concept; the domain is the source partition, the entity is the registered instance. Cross-domain references are controlled per [Volume III, Section III.2.12.3].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume III, Section III.2.1.2] (Domain Separation Model), [Volume III, Section III.2.12.3] (Corpus Domain Isolation and Cross-Domain References), [Volume III, Section III.2.13] (Domain-Agnostic Corpus Swapping), [Volume III, Section III.2.13.4] (Supported Corpus Markets).

**See also:** I.3.2.8 (Psychology Corpus), I.3.2.7 (Behavioral Ontology), I.3.2.6 (Knowledge Graph Entity), I.3.2.12 (Specialist Agent).

#### I.3.3.8 Local LLM Ecosystem

**Definition.** The *Local LLM Ecosystem* is the set of locally hosted large language models, inference runtimes, model registries, and routing logic that allow PsyForge's Agents to reason without depending on external model providers, enabling local-first, air-gapped, and data-residency-constrained deployments. The ecosystem includes a local model registry, model selection and routing, and resource-constrained sizing. See [Volume II, Section II.5.5.3], [Volume IX, Section IX.6.1.2], and [Volume X, Section X.5.2.4].

**Rationale.** The platform's *Local-First* principle (see [Volume I, Section I.2.1.5]) requires that the system function without mandatory cloud dependencies, including for AI reasoning. Sending behavioral data to external model providers may violate data-residency obligations, customer trust, or air-gap requirements. The Local LLM Ecosystem exists so that Agent reasoning can run on-premise, on the customer's hardware, with models whose provenance and versioning are under the customer's control (REQ-LLM-001). It also enables cost control via model routing — selecting cheaper local models for routine reasoning and reserving capacity for demanding tasks (see [Volume X, Section X.5.2.4]).

**Differentiation.** The Local LLM Ecosystem is the *local inference substrate*; an *Agent* (I.3.2.11) is the *reasoning component* that consumes it; a *Specialist Agent* (I.3.2.12) is a *typed agent* whose prompts may be tuned for local-model capabilities. It is distinct from cloud model access, which MAY be used in hybrid deployments but is not a dependency. It is distinct from the *Psychology Corpus* (I.3.2.8), which supplies *knowledge*, not *reasoning capacity*. The local model runtime for air-gapped deployment is specified in [Volume IX, Section IX.6.1.2]; the known risk of a quality gap between local and cloud models is documented in [Volume X, Section X.7.1.6].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume II, Section II.5.5.3] (Local Model Runtime), [Volume IX, Section IX.6.1.2] (Local LLM Runtime for Air-Gapped), [Volume IX, Section IX.6.1.3] (Local Model Registry), [Volume X, Section X.5.2.4] (Model Routing Strategy).

**See also:** I.3.2.11 (Agent), I.3.2.12 (Specialist Agent), I.3.2.8 (Psychology Corpus), I.3.2.13 (Orchestrator).

---

### I.3.4 Process and Governance Terms

#### I.3.4.1 Recommendation Lifecycle

**Definition.** The *Recommendation Lifecycle* is the end-to-end process by which a Recommendation is conceived, assembled, scored, reviewed, deployed, measured, and potentially rolled back. The lifecycle spans: agent reasoning and Orchestrator synthesis; Recommendation Engine assembly; Ethics Layer scoring; Human Review State transitions (Proposed → Reviewed → {Rejected | Modified → Approved} → Deployed → {Rolled Back}); live deployment; outcome measurement; and feedback-loop closure that feeds observed effects back into the Knowledge Graph and models. See [Volume II, Section II.2.1] and [Volume II, Section II.2.6].

**Rationale.** Without a defined lifecycle, a Recommendation is a one-shot artifact: it is proposed, deployed, and forgotten, with no measurement of whether the predicted effect materialized. This defeats the platform's *Prediction over Reaction* and *Evidence over Intuition* principles (see [Volume I, Section I.2.1.4] and [Volume I, Section I.2.1.7]). The Recommendation Lifecycle exists to make the loop closed: every deployed Recommendation is followed by outcome measurement, and the measured effect updates the Knowledge Graph, calibrates simulation, and adjusts confidence in the causal chains that justified it (REQ-REC-005). Closure of this feedback loop is what makes the platform self-improving rather than merely advisory.

**Differentiation.** The Recommendation Lifecycle is the *end-to-end process*; the *Human Review State* (I.3.2.19) is the *state machine* governing the review portion; the *Experiment Framework* (I.3.4.2) is the *measurement mechanism* used in the outcome-measurement phase. It is distinct from the *term lifecycle* (I.3.1.2), which governs glossary terms, not Recommendations. The lifecycle's feedback-loop closure phase is specified in [Volume II, Section II.2.1.13]; outcome measurement in [Volume II, Section II.2.1.12].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume II, Section II.2.1] (End-to-End Data Pipeline), [Volume II, Section II.2.6] (Recommendation Gate Architecture), [Volume II, Section II.2.1.12] (Outcome Measurement), [Volume II, Section II.2.1.13] (Feedback Loop Closure), [Volume II, Diagram D.12] (Recommendation Lifecycle State Machine).

**See also:** I.3.2.9 (Recommendation), I.3.2.19 (Human Review State), I.3.2.20 (Deployment Gate), I.3.3.5 (Recommendation Engine), I.3.4.2 (Experiment Framework), I.3.4.6 (Acceptance Criteria).

#### I.3.4.2 Experiment Framework

**Definition.** The *Experiment Framework* is the subsystem that designs, executes, and analyzes controlled experiments (A/B, multivariate, sequential) on live populations to measure the actual effect of a deployed Recommendation and to validate simulation predictions against reality. It encompasses hypothesis formulation, variable definition, sample-size and power analysis, randomization, isolation and interference detection, significance testing, effect-size estimation, heterogeneous treatment effects, and causal attribution. See [Volume IV, Section IV.6].

**Rationale.** Simulation forecasts effects in silico; only live experimentation confirms them. Without a rigorous experiment framework, the platform cannot close the feedback loop (see I.3.4.1), cannot calibrate its simulation engine against reality (see [Volume IV, Section IV.6.4.3]), and cannot defend its predictions empirically. The Experiment Framework exists to make validation scientific rather than anecdotal: every deployed Recommendation of sufficient impact SHOULD be accompanied by an experiment whose design is statistically powered and whose analysis distinguishes causal effect from noise (REQ-EXP-001). The Experiment Planner Agent (see [Volume V, Section V.12]) automates design; simulation pre-screens hypotheses to avoid wasteful live experiments (see [Volume IV, Section IV.6.4]).

**Differentiation.** The Experiment Framework is the *live-measurement system*; a *Simulation Run* (I.3.2.10) is the *in-silico forecast* that the experiment validates; a *Recommendation* (I.3.2.9) is the *artifact* whose effect the experiment measures. It is distinct from the *Evaluation Corpus* (I.3.4.3), which evaluates the platform's *own components*, whereas the Experiment Framework measures *customer outcomes*. It is distinct from the *Benchmark Suite* (I.3.4.4), which is standardized and recurring rather than per-recommendation. Statistical methodology is specified in [Volume IV, Section IV.6.3].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume IV, Section IV.6] (Experimentation Framework), [Volume IV, Section IV.6.4] (Simulation-as-Experiment), [Volume V, Section V.12] (Experiment Planner Agent), [Volume VII, Section VII.5.9] (Experiment API), [Volume II, Diagram D.14] (Experiment Lifecycle Diagram).

**See also:** I.3.2.9 (Recommendation), I.3.2.10 (Simulation Run), I.3.4.1 (Recommendation Lifecycle), I.3.4.3 (Evaluation Corpus), I.3.4.4 (Benchmark Suite).

#### I.3.4.3 Evaluation Corpus

**Definition.** An *Evaluation Corpus* is a curated, versioned dataset of gold-standard and human-labeled cases — together with synthetic evaluation data — used to evaluate the performance of the platform's AI components (agents, predictive models, simulation, recommendation, ethics scoring) on a recurring cadence. It is the substrate of the AI Evaluation Framework. See [Volume X, Section X.4.6] and [Volume X, Section X.4.1].

**Rationale.** AI components drift, regress across releases, and may silently degrade in ways invisible in production traffic. Without a fixed, labeled evaluation set, quality claims are uncheckable and regressions are detected only after harm. The Evaluation Corpus exists as the held-out, ground-truth dataset against which every component is scored per-release and continuously, enabling regression detection, benchmark comparison, and conformance to quality claims (REQ-EVAL-001). Its construction blends human-labeled gold-standard cases (for components where ground truth is definable) and synthetic data (for coverage of rare or edge cases), with maintenance and evolution specified in [Volume X, Section X.4.6.4].

**Differentiation.** The Evaluation Corpus evaluates *the platform's own components*; the *Psychology Corpus* (I.3.2.8) supplies *domain knowledge* the platform reasons with; the *Experiment Framework* (I.3.4.2) measures *customer outcomes* in live populations. The three are distinct: corpus is knowledge, experiment is live measurement, evaluation is held-out testing. The Evaluation Corpus is distinct from the *Benchmark Suite* (I.3.4.4): the corpus is the *dataset*, the benchmark suite is the *suite of tests and metrics* that runs against it (and against live performance data). The corpus is constructed per [Volume X, Section X.4.6.1] through [Volume X, Section X.4.6.3].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume X, Section X.4.6] (Evaluation Corpus), [Volume X, Section X.4.1] (Evaluation Architecture), [Volume X, Section X.4.2] (Agent Evaluation), [Volume X, Section X.4.5] (Recommendation Evaluation), [Volume III, Section III.5.5.4] (Evaluation Cards).

**See also:** I.3.2.8 (Psychology Corpus), I.3.4.2 (Experiment Framework), I.3.4.4 (Benchmark Suite), I.3.4.6 (Acceptance Criteria).

#### I.3.4.4 Benchmark Suite

**Definition.** A *Benchmark Suite* is the organized collection of standardized benchmarks — for behavioral prediction (retention, churn, spending, engagement, satisfaction), simulation accuracy, ethics detection, and system performance — against which the platform's components are scored on a recurring cadence, with results recorded on a leaderboard. The Benchmark Suite runs against the Evaluation Corpus and against live performance telemetry. See [Volume X, Section X.6] and [Volume X, Section X.4.2.1].

**Rationale.** Evaluation against a corpus yields per-component scores; without standardized benchmarks and targets, those scores are uninterpretable in isolation and incomparable across releases or competitors. The Benchmark Suite exists as the reference framework that defines what "good" means for each component, fixes accuracy targets (e.g., retention prediction within ±2%; see [Volume X, Section X.6.2.1]), and tracks performance over time so that regressions and improvements are visible (REQ-EVAL-002). The leaderboard (see [Volume X, Section X.6.1.5]) supports internal accountability and, where appropriate, external claims. Benchmark maintenance and evolution are coupled to the Evaluation Corpus per [Volume X, Section X.4.6.4].

**Differentiation.** The Benchmark Suite is the *suite of tests, metrics, and targets*; the *Evaluation Corpus* (I.3.4.3) is the *dataset* the suite runs against. It is distinct from the *Experiment Framework* (I.3.4.2), which measures live customer outcomes, not standardized component performance. It is distinct from *Acceptance Criteria* (I.3.4.6): benchmarks score ongoing performance; acceptance criteria gate whether a component is fit to release. The four benchmark categories — behavioral prediction, simulation accuracy, ethics, and performance — are specified in [Volume X, Sections X.6.1 through X.6.4].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume X, Section X.6] (Validation and Benchmarking), [Volume X, Section X.6.1] (Behavioral Prediction Benchmarks), [Volume X, Section X.6.2] (Simulation Accuracy Benchmarks), [Volume X, Section X.6.3] (Ethics Benchmarks), [Volume X, Section X.6.4] (Performance Benchmarks).

**See also:** I.3.4.3 (Evaluation Corpus), I.3.4.2 (Experiment Framework), I.3.4.6 (Acceptance Criteria), I.3.2.18 (Manipulation Risk Score).

#### I.3.4.5 Compliance Matrix

**Definition.** A *Compliance Matrix* is the governed mapping that relates each applicable regulatory and standards obligation (GDPR, CCPA/CPRA, COPPA, EU AI Act, HIPAA where the healthcare corpus variant is active, SOC 2, ISO 27001, PCI DSS where applicable, and regional regulations such as LGPD and PIPL) to the specific PsyForge features, controls, and audit artifacts that satisfy it, together with a status dashboard and gap-analysis tracking. See [Volume IX, Section IX.5.6] and Appendix H.

**Rationale.** A behavioral-intelligence platform handling human data across jurisdictions faces a dense and evolving regulatory landscape. Without an explicit, maintained mapping from obligation to control, compliance becomes anecdotal, gaps go undetected until audit or incident, and the platform cannot defensibly claim conformance. The Compliance Matrix exists as the single source of regulatory truth: for each obligation, it names the satisfying feature, the evidence artifact, the responsible control, and the current conformance status, with gap analysis driving remediation (REQ-COMP-001). This is the operational counterpart to the Ethics Layer (I.3.3.6): ethics addresses obligations *beyond* legal minimums (see [Volume I, Section I.5.5.3]); the Compliance Matrix addresses the legal minimums themselves.

**Differentiation.** The Compliance Matrix maps *regulatory* obligations to controls; the *Ethics Layer* (I.3.3.6) scores *ethical* dimensions of Recommendations, which are broader. It is distinct from the *Manipulation Risk Score* (I.3.2.18), which is a per-Recommendation score, whereas the matrix is a platform-wide mapping. It is distinct from *Acceptance Criteria* (I.3.4.6): acceptance criteria gate release fitness; the compliance matrix tracks ongoing regulatory conformance. The matrix is maintained per [Volume IX, Section IX.5.6.3] and rendered as a heatmap in [Volume IX, Diagram D.IX.5].

**Cross-References.** See [Volume IX, Section IX.5.6] (Compliance Matrix), [Volume IX, Section IX.5.1] through [Volume IX, Section IX.5.5] (per-regulation compliance), Appendix H (Compliance Matrix appendix), [Volume IX, Diagram D.IX.5] (Compliance Matrix Heatmap).

**See also:** I.3.3.6 (Ethics Layer), I.3.2.18 (Manipulation Risk Score), I.3.4.6 (Acceptance Criteria), I.3.2.19 (Human Review State).

#### I.3.4.6 Acceptance Criteria

**Definition.** *Acceptance Criteria* are the explicit, verifiable conditions, expressed using the conformance levels MUST, SHOULD, and MAY and classified by verification method (Inspection, Analysis, Test, Demonstration), that a PsyForge component or feature MUST satisfy to be deemed fit for release. Acceptance Criteria are defined per specification area and traceably linked to the requirements and architecture they validate. See [Front Matter, Section FM.0.11] and Appendix K.

**Rationale.** Vague "definition of done" statements produce components that are subjectively complete but objectively unverifiable, and that regress silently after release. Acceptance Criteria exist to make fitness for release an objective, auditable judgment: each criterion is binary or thresholded, tied to a verification method, and traceable through the requirements traceability matrix (Appendix G) from vision to test (REQ-AC-001). This traceability is what makes conformance claims defensible to customers, auditors, and regulators. The conformance-level mapping (MUST → mandatory acceptance; SHOULD → recommended; MAY → optional) is specified in [Front Matter, Section FM.0.11.2]; the verification method classification in [Front Matter, Section FM.0.11.3].

**Differentiation.** Acceptance Criteria gate *release fitness*; the *Benchmark Suite* (I.3.4.4) scores *ongoing performance*; the *Compliance Matrix* (I.3.4.5) tracks *regulatory conformance*. A component may pass acceptance criteria at release, drift on benchmarks afterward, and remain compliant throughout — these are distinct judgments. Acceptance Criteria are distinct from the *Human Review State* (I.3.2.19), which gates individual Recommendations, not component releases. Acceptance criteria are specified per area in Appendix K (e.g., [Appendix K, Section K.9] for the Ethics Layer, [Appendix K, Section K.8] for the Recommendation Engine).

**Cross-References.** See [Front Matter, Section FM.0.11] (Acceptance Criteria Conventions), [Front Matter, Section FM.0.10.4] (Traceability Chain), Appendix K (Acceptance Criteria by Specification Area), Appendix G (Requirements Traceability Matrix).

**See also:** I.3.4.4 (Benchmark Suite), I.3.4.5 (Compliance Matrix), I.3.2.19 (Human Review State), I.3.2.20 (Deployment Gate), I.3.4.1 (Recommendation Lifecycle).

---

*End of Part I.3 — Terminology and Glossary.*

*This Part is normative. All terms used with domain-specific meaning elsewhere in the PsyForge v1.0 specification MUST be defined here or in a normatively referenced appendix prior to reliance for conformance. The alphabetical term index and term-to-section cross-reference are maintained in Appendix F; the dependency graph is rendered in Appendix D.*

## Part I.4 — Behavioral Model

> **Status:** Normative
> **Governing Law:** [WPAI Constitution](https://github.com/MrWizard94-Compile/Agents-Constitution), frozen per AGENTS.md
> **Scope:** This Part defines the Behavioral Stack — the layered causal model that is the intellectual core of PsyForge. It specifies the seven layers (Interaction, Behavior, Emotion, Motivation, Mechanic, Economy, Outcome), their inputs, outputs, inferences, confidence expectations, ethical bounds, and the cross-layer causal inference machinery that binds them. All downstream volumes — Knowledge Graph (Volume III), Simulation (Volume IV), and Agents (Volume V) — operationalize the model defined here.

---

### I.4.1 Model Overview

#### I.4.1.1 The Behavioral Stack: Interaction → Behavior → Emotion → Motivation → Mechanic → Economy → Outcome

PsyForge's central intellectual contribution is the **Behavioral Stack**: a seven-layer, strictly ordered causal hierarchy that transforms raw human-system interactions into explainable, attributable, and ultimately predictable outcomes. The stack, in its canonical ordering, is:

**Interaction → Behavior → Emotion → Motivation → Mechanic → Economy → Outcome**

Each layer consumes the structured output of the layer immediately beneath it and produces a higher-order abstraction consumed by the layer above. An *Interaction* is the lowest-level observable — a discrete, timestamped act such as a click, a navigation, a purchase, a craft, or a combat ability activation. A *Behavior* is a recognized pattern of interactions that signifies intent or disposition (e.g., "systematic exploration of unmapped regions"). An *Emotion* is a transient affective state *inferred* from behavioral and contextual signals — never measured directly. A *Motivation* is the enduring psychological driver that explains why a behavior recurs (e.g., achievement, relatedness, autonomy). A *Mechanic* is the game or product system that the motivated user engages with (e.g., a crafting system, a leaderboard). The *Economy* is the virtual or real value-exchange substrate through which mechanic engagement is denominated and constrained. The *Outcome* is the observable business or experiential result — retention, revenue, satisfaction, churn — to which every prior layer can be causally attributed.

The ordering is not arbitrary; it reflects a defensible theory of how digital experience produces consequence. Outcomes do not arise from interactions in isolation; they arise from a chain in which interactions reveal behaviors, behaviors betray emotions, emotions point to motivations, motivations select mechanics, mechanics transact through economies, and economies realize outcomes. Flattening this chain — as flat correlation-based analytics does — destroys the explanatory structure that makes a recommendation trustworthy. A correlation between "housing purchases" and "retention" tells a studio *that* the two co-occur; the Behavioral Stack tells them *why*, and therefore *whether the relationship will hold under intervention*.

The canonical housing example illustrates the full depth of the stack:

**Housing → Ownership → Identity → Community Recognition → Long-Term Retention**

A player *clicks* to acquire a housing plot (Interaction). Over weeks, the pattern of furnishing, decorating, and returning to the plot is classified as a *nesting/ownership* Behavior. That behavior, combined with personalization intensity and visit regularity, supports an inference of *pride and attachment* (Emotion). The pride maps to an *identity-expression* Motivation, which in turn is served by the *housing mechanic* and reinforced by *community recognition* within the social economy — and the cumulative causal chain produces *long-term retention* (Outcome). This chain is the unit of reasoning PsyForge exists to construct, validate, and intervene upon.

The stack is the contract every other volume fulfills. Volume III persists each layer as typed graph nodes and typed causal edges (see [Volume III, Section III.1.2] and [Volume III, Section III.1.3]). Volume IV instantiates the stack inside virtual personas so that simulated interventions can be evaluated before deployment (see [Volume IV, Section IV.2.3]). Volume V's specialist agents — Psychologist, Behavioral Economist, Game Designer, Monetization Advisor — each reason over a defined subset of layers (see [Volume V, Parts V.4, V.5, V.8, V.10]).

**REQ-BEH-001 (Stack Ordering Inviolability):** The seven-layer ordering Interaction → Behavior → Emotion → Motivation → Mechanic → Economy → Outcome MUST be treated as normative and immutable. Implementations MUST NOT invert, skip, or merge layers. Layer reordering is a specification change requiring Architecture Decision Record approval per [Volume II, Section II.1.3].

**REQ-BEH-002 (Layer Contract):** Each layer MUST publish a defined input contract (the structured output of the layer below) and a defined output contract (the structured abstraction consumed by the layer above). A layer MUST NOT consume data from a non-adjacent layer except through an explicitly declared cross-layer path registered in the Knowledge Graph (see [Volume III, Section III.1.6.2]).

#### I.4.1.2 Stack Layer Definitions

This subsection provides the formal definition of each layer: what data it consumes, what it produces, what inferences it makes, the confidence level expected of it, and the ethical considerations that attach. These definitions are the authoritative reference; every later subsection (I.4.2 through I.4.8) elaborates a single layer.

**1. Interaction Layer (I.4.2).** *Consumes:* raw telemetry events from the SDK (see [Volume VI, Part VI.1]) after normalization. *Produces:* typed, context-enriched interaction records. *Inferences:* none — the Interaction Layer is descriptive, not inferential. *Confidence expected:* effectively 1.0 for the event's occurrence (subject to telemetry quality; see [Volume VI, Section VI.1.4]); the *type* assignment carries the confidence of the classification rule. *Ethical considerations:* consent and lawful basis for collection (see [Volume VI, Part VI.3]); no inferences are made about the person, only about the act.

**2. Behavior Layer (I.4.3).** *Consumes:* sequences of interaction records over temporal windows. *Produces:* classified behavioral patterns and behavioral-state-machine transitions. *Inferences:* pattern membership and state transitions. *Confidence expected:* moderate to high for well-populated patterns (target ≥ 0.7), lower for novel or sparse patterns. *Ethical considerations:* behavioral labeling must avoid pathologizing language and must not be exposed to the data subject in a stigmatizing form.

**3. Emotion Layer (I.4.4).** *Consumes:* behavioral signals, interaction micro-features (timing, pacing, abandon events), and contextual state. *Produces:* probabilistic emotion-state estimates with confidence intervals. *Inferences:* *affective state inference from behavior* — explicitly an inference, never a measurement. *Confidence expected:* inherently bounded; PsyForge MUST treat any single emotion estimate as low-to-moderate confidence and MUST aggregate before acting. *Ethical considerations:* substantial — see I.4.4.5. PsyForge MUST NOT represent inferred emotion as measured fact.

**4. Motivation Layer (I.4.5).** *Consumes:* persistent behavioral patterns, emotion trajectories, and corpus-grounded motivation frameworks (SDT, Fogg, etc.). *Produces:* motivation-archetype membership weights and motivational-shift detections. *Inferences:* the latent driver(s) explaining observed behavioral and emotional regularities. *Confidence expected:* moderate; motivation is a theoretical construct, not an observable. *Ethical considerations:* motivation labels are interpretive and carry stereotype risk; PsyForge MUST use probabilistic, multi-motivation representations rather than single-label assignments.

**5. Mechanic Layer (I.4.6).** *Consumes:* mechanic definitions from the product's design manifest and player engagement telemetry against those mechanics. *Produces:* mechanic-engagement metrics and mechanic-emotion feedback loops. *Inferences:* which mechanics serve which motivations, and which mechanics are over- or under-serving player populations. *Confidence expected:* high for engagement metrics (they are largely descriptive); moderate for mechanic-motivation fit. *Ethical considerations:* mechanic design that exploits inferred vulnerabilities (e.g., variable-reward targeting of loss-averse players) is gated by the Ethics Layer (see [Volume I, Section I.5.2]).

**6. Economy Layer (I.4.7).** *Consumes:* virtual or real transaction events, resource balances, price histories, and sink/source definitions. *Produces:* economic behavior patterns, economy-health indicators, and economy-to-outcome linkages. *Inferences:* economic rationality, spending propensities, and economy-wide health. *Confidence expected:* high for accounting-derived metrics; moderate for behavioral-economics inferences. *Ethical considerations:* spending-pattern inference and "whale" classification carry manipulation risk and require Ethics Officer sign-off before any monetization recommendation is deployed (see [Volume V, Section V.11.5]).

**7. Outcome Layer (I.4.8).** *Consumes:* all prior layers plus external outcome signals (retention logs, revenue, surveys, churn events). *Produces:* outcome predictions, outcome attribution across the stack, and validation evidence. *Inferences:* causal attribution of outcomes to upstream layers, and forward predictions of outcomes under hypothetical interventions. *Confidence expected:* outcome predictions MUST always carry calibrated confidence intervals; no point estimate without an uncertainty band. *Ethical considerations:* outcome optimization must respect the manipulation boundary (see [Volume I, Section I.5.1.3]) — optimizing retention is legitimate; engineering compulsion is not.

**REQ-BEH-003 (Per-Layer Confidence Publication):** Every layer's output records MUST carry a `confidence` field and a `confidence_method` field identifying how the confidence was computed. Outputs without these fields MUST NOT be admitted to the Knowledge Graph.

**REQ-BEH-004 (Layer Boundary Discipline):** A layer MUST NOT write inferences that belong to a higher layer. The Behavior Layer MUST NOT emit emotion labels; the Emotion Layer MUST NOT emit motivation labels. Cross-layer inferences flow through registered causal edges in the Knowledge Graph, not through implicit layer-boundary violations.

#### I.4.1.3 Cross-Layer Causal Inference Model

The Behavioral Stack's decisive advantage over flat correlation-based analytics is that it is a *causal* model, not merely a statistical one. A correlation between housing purchases and retention is, by itself, unfalsifiable as a guide to action: it cannot distinguish a *causal* relationship (building housing causes retention) from a *confounded* one (some third factor — disposable income, social embedding, veteran status — causes both). Acting on a confounded correlation wastes engineering effort and can actively harm the player base. PsyForge's cross-layer causal inference model exists to make this distinction rigorously and at scale.

The model is built on three primitives. First, **structural layering**: because the stack imposes an ordering, it functions as a *structural causal model* (SCM) in the sense of Pearl. Edges are directed and acyclic at the layer granularity — Emotion can cause Motivation, but Motivation cannot cause the Emotion that gave rise to it within the same causal instant. This ordering supplies the identifiability assumptions that pure observational correlation lacks. Second, **mediation analysis**: each layer is a candidate mediator of the layer below's effect on the layer above. When PsyForge asks "does housing cause retention?", it decomposes the total effect through the chain Housing → Ownership → Identity → Community Recognition → Retention, estimating the direct effect and the mediated effect at each hop using the methods of [Volume III, Section III.7.2]. Third, **interventional validation**: causal claims that survive observational analysis are then tested via the simulation-as-experiment pipeline (see [Volume IV, Section IV.6.4]) and, where ethical and feasible, live A/B experimentation (see [Volume IV, Part IV.6]).

The model handles confounders explicitly. Known confounders — veteran status, spend capacity, session cadence — are registered as candidate confound variables and controlled via backdoor adjustment, instrumental variables, or sensitivity analysis as specified in [Volume III, Section III.7.2.4] through [Volume III, Section III.7.2.5]. When a confounder is unmeasured, PsyForge MUST downgrade the causal confidence of the affected path and flag the residual uncertainty in the explanation layer (see [Volume III, Section III.7.3]).

Crucially, the cross-layer model produces *interventional* predictions, not just associational ones. Given a candidate change to a mechanic, PsyForge uses do-calculus (see [Volume III, Section III.7.2.2]) to propagate the intervention up the stack: the mechanic change alters engagement, which alters economy flows, which alters predicted outcomes — each step carrying forward its confidence band. This is precisely what enables the simulation engine's headline capability: running 10 million synthetic players through hundreds of candidate updates before shipping one patch (see [Volume IV, Section IV.9.1]).

**REQ-BEH-005 (Causal Edge Typing):** Every cross-layer edge persisted in the Knowledge Graph MUST be typed as one of: `causal`, `mediating`, `confounding`, `correlational_only`, or `unverified`. Edges of type `correlational_only` MUST NOT be used as the basis for a deployment recommendation without an accompanying unmeasured-confounder sensitivity analysis.

**REQ-BEH-006 (Intervention Propagation):** Any recommendation that asserts an outcome change under a hypothetical intervention MUST trace a complete causal path from the intervention layer to the Outcome Layer. Recommendations with broken or `unverified` edges in the path MUST be flagged `causally_unsupported` and routed to human review per [Volume II, Section II.2.6.2].

**REQ-BEH-007 (Confounder Registration):** Each tenant deployment MUST maintain a registered confounder catalog. Causal estimates that ignore a registered confounder MUST be marked `confounded` and downgraded in confidence.

#### I.4.1.4 Model Assumptions and Limitations

No behavioral model is assumption-free, and PsyForge's value depends on stating its assumptions explicitly so that operators can recognize when the model's guarantees degrade. This subsection enumerates the load-bearing assumptions and the known limitations that bound the model's applicability.

**Assumption 1 — Telemetry Faithfulness.** The stack assumes that the interaction stream is a faithful, sufficiently complete record of the player's behavior in the product. This assumption breaks under telemetry loss, SDK mis-instrumentation, offline play without buffering (see [Volume VI, Section VI.2.2.4]), or deliberate evasion. When telemetry completeness drops below the threshold defined in [Volume VI, Section VI.1.4.5], the Behavior Layer's inferences degrade non-gracefully, and the model MUST annotate affected downstream layers with a `data_quality_degraded` marker that propagates to the Outcome Layer.

**Assumption 2 — Behavioral Stability Within a Session.** Within a single session, the model assumes that motivational drivers and emotional baselines are relatively stable, permitting emotion inference from within-session behavioral signals. Cross-session emotional carryover is modeled explicitly through the persistence-and-decay machinery of I.4.4.4 rather than assumed away.

**Assumption 3 — Construct Validity of the Corpus.** The Motivation and Emotion layers depend on the Psychology Corpus (see [Volume III, Part III.2]). The model assumes that the corpus's constructs (e.g., Self-Determination Theory's autonomy/competence/relatedness) are *valid* for the population under study. This assumption is culturally and demographically bounded; a corpus validated on Western adult gamers may not transfer to a mobile education product in a different cultural context. Corpus swapping (see [Volume III, Section III.2.13]) is the mechanism for restoring construct validity when the assumption fails, and corpus mismatch MUST be detected and surfaced as a model-validity warning.

**Assumption 4 — Layer Locality of Causation.** The stack assumes that, at the granularity of the model, the dominant causal flow is upward (Interaction → Outcome). It does not deny downward feedback (an Outcome such as a forced-economy rebalance can change Mechanics, which changes Motivations), but treats such feedback as a separate, explicitly modeled loop rather than as a violation of layer ordering. Downward feedback edges are typed `feedback` in the Knowledge Graph and are subject to stability analysis before being admitted.

**Limitations.** The model cannot infer private mental states with certainty; emotion inferences are bounded by the bandwidth of behavioral signals (see I.4.4.5). The model cannot establish causation from a single observational dataset without untestable identifying assumptions; it therefore requires simulation or experiment to upgrade `correlational` edges to `causal`. The model's predictions degrade in cold-start conditions (new players, new mechanics, new markets) where the corpus is thin. The model does not handle adversarial populations — players deliberately generating misleading telemetry — beyond anomaly detection (see [Volume III, Section III.4.3.3] and [Volume III, Section III.6.1.3]).

**REQ-BEH-008 (Assumption Violation Reporting):** When any of Assumptions 1–4 is detected to be violated, the system MUST emit an `assumption_violation` event to the audit trail, tag affected downstream inferences, and downgrade their confidence. Recommendations derived from inferences with active assumption violations MUST be routed to human review and MUST NOT be auto-deployed.

**REQ-BEH-009 (Cold-Start Boundary):** In cold-start conditions (insufficient behavioral history or corpus coverage), the Emotion, Motivation, and Outcome layers MUST emit predictions with explicitly widened confidence intervals. The system MUST NOT present cold-start predictions as production-grade forecasts.

#### I.4.1.5 Model Validity Criteria

A behavioral model is only as useful as its demonstrated validity. PsyForge defines five criteria by which the Behavioral Stack's validity is continuously judged, and against which any individual inference or recommendation can be evaluated. These criteria are not aspirational; they are operational, instrumented, and reported on the dashboard described in [Volume VII, Section VII.2.2].

**Criterion 1 — Predictive Validity.** The model's outcome predictions must be calibrated against realized outcomes. A prediction that "Version 1.8 will increase 7-day retention by 4% ± 1.5%" is valid only if, post-deployment, realized retention falls within the predicted confidence interval at the calibrated rate. Calibration is measured continuously via the backtesting and forecast-validation machinery in [Volume IV, Section IV.5.3]. A model whose 90% confidence intervals contain the realized outcome less than 80% of the time is *miscalibrated* and triggers retraining per [Volume III, Section III.5.5.5].
