**Criterion 2 — Causal Validity.** Where the model asserts causal relationships, those assertions must survive the falsification tests available to them: natural-experiment analysis, instrumental-variable checks, sensitivity to unmeasured confounders, and ultimately live experimentation. Causal edges that have never been experimentally validated are typed `correlational_only` or `unverified` and cannot anchor a deployment recommendation without human override (see REQ-BEH-006).

**Criterion 3 — Construct Validity.** The constructs used by the Emotion and Motivation layers must demonstrably correspond to the corpus-grounded definitions (see [Volume III, Part III.2]). Construct drift — where an operationalization of, say, "frustration" ceases to correspond to the corpus's definition — is monitored via the feature-drift and corpus-quality metrics in [Volume III, Section III.3.4.6] and [Volume III, Section III.2.1.5].

**Criterion 4 — Discriminant Validity.** Layers must measure distinct constructs. If the Emotion Layer's outputs are perfectly predicted by the Behavior Layer's outputs, the Emotion Layer is not adding information and its existence as a separate layer is unjustified. Discriminant validity is assessed via mutual-information and conditional-independence tests between adjacent layers; significant conditional independence between a layer and the next given its inputs is a *positive* signal of layer justification.

**Criterion 5 — Ecological Validity.** The model must perform not only on historical or simulated data but on real, deployed product changes under real player populations. Ecological validity is established through the simulation-to-live validation pipeline (see [Volume IV, Section IV.6.4.2]): a recommendation that performs well in simulation but poorly in live A/B test is an ecological-validity failure that MUST trigger a simulation-fidelity investigation.

**REQ-BEH-010 (Validity Reporting):** Each of the five validity criteria MUST have a continuously updated metric published to the dashboard and a defined alerting threshold. A criterion falling below threshold MUST block new recommendations of the affected type until the underlying model is re-validated.

**REQ-BEH-011 (Validity-Tiered Confidence):** Outputs derived from layers or edges failing one or more validity criteria MUST carry a `validity_tier` field (`gold`, `silver`, `bronze`, `unvalidated`) reflecting the worst-performing criterion applicable. The Ethics Officer Agent (see [Volume V, Section V.11]) MUST treat `bronze` and `unvalidated` outputs as requiring heightened scrutiny.

---

### I.4.2 Interaction Layer

#### I.4.2.1 Interaction Taxonomy (Click, Navigate, Dwell, Purchase, Craft, Combat, Social, etc.)

The Interaction Layer is the foundation of the Behavioral Stack and the only layer that is purely descriptive rather than inferential. Its taxonomy defines the vocabulary in which all higher-order reasoning is ultimately grounded: a behavioral pattern is, in the end, a structured arrangement of interactions, and an emotion inference is, in the end, a function of interaction micro-features. The taxonomy's coverage and precision therefore bound the entire stack's expressive power.

PsyForge defines a normative interaction taxonomy organized into seven families. The **Pointer/Selection family** comprises atomic input acts: `click`, `tap`, `double_click`, `long_press`, `hover`, `right_click`, `drag_start`, `drag_end`. These are the granular events from which higher-level input gestures are composed. The **Navigation family** captures spatial and logical movement through the product: `navigate` (explicit change of screen, scene, or view), `scroll`, `pan`, `zoom`, `back`, `forward`, `menu_open`, `menu_close`, `tab_switch`. The **Attention family** captures the absence of explicit input but the presence of engagement: `dwell` (sustained focus on an element above a duration threshold), `idle` (no input within a session window), `afk` (session present but user disengaged). The **Transactional family** captures value exchange: `purchase`, `earn`, `spend`, `trade_initiate`, `trade_complete`, `refund`, `gift`. The **Gameplay family** captures domain verbs and is extensible per product: `craft`, `combat_ability`, `damage_dealt`, `damage_taken`, `death`, `respawn`, `gather`, `build`, `quest_accept`, `quest_complete`, `level_up`. The **Social family** captures inter-agent acts: `message_send`, `message_read`, `emote`, `group_join`, `group_leave`, `friend_add`, `follow`, `block`. The **System family** captures non-player-originated events that nonetheless affect the interaction context: `error`, `crash`, `disconnect`, `reconnect`, `performance_hitch`, `loading_start`, `loading_complete`.

Each interaction type carries a *granularity class* — `atomic`, `composite`, or `contextual` — that downstream layers use to decide whether to treat the event as a unit or to decompose it. A `drag` is `composite` (composed of pointer-down, move, pointer-up); a `dwell` is `contextual` (defined relative to element bounds and a duration threshold rather than as a single input act).

The taxonomy is extensible. Products with novel verbs (a rhythm game's `tap_to_beat`, a flight sim's `trim_adjust`) register custom interaction types via the Custom Event Extension mechanism (see [Volume VI, Section VI.1.2.3]). Registered custom types MUST declare their family, granularity class, and a mapping to the nearest standard analogue, so that cross-product behavioral reasoning can fall back to the standard type when a custom type is unknown.

**REQ-BEH-012 (Taxonomy Coverage):** Every interaction event emitted by the SDK MUST be classifiable to exactly one interaction type in the taxonomy. Events that cannot be classified MUST be routed to an `uncategorized` bucket and MUST trigger a schema-completeness warning per [Volume VI, Section VI.1.4.1].

**REQ-BEH-013 (Family Assignment):** Each registered interaction type MUST declare exactly one of the seven families. Cross-family polymorphism is forbidden at the taxonomy level; a single event that conceptually spans families (e.g., a `purchase` triggered by a `click`) MUST be emitted as separate interaction records or as a composite with a declared primary family.

**REQ-BEH-014 (Custom-Type Registration):** Custom interaction types MUST NOT be admitted to the Behavior Layer until they are registered with a family, granularity class, and standard-analogue mapping. Unregistered custom types MUST be quarantined and reported.

#### I.4.2.2 Interaction Metadata Schema

An interaction record is more than its type; the metadata surrounding the type is what makes downstream inference possible. The Interaction Metadata Schema specifies the normative envelope that every interaction record MUST carry, in addition to the telemetry envelope defined in [Volume VI, Section VI.1.2.1].

Every interaction record MUST contain the following fields. `interaction_id` — a globally unique, monotonic identifier. `interaction_type` — a value from the taxonomy of I.4.2.1. `family` — the interaction family. `granularity` — `atomic`, `composite`, or `contextual`. `player_id` — the pseudonymized player identifier per [Volume VI, Section VI.3.1.2]. `session_id` — the session within which the interaction occurred. `client_timestamp` and `server_timestamp` — both required, with clock-skew handled per [Volume II, Section II.8.1.12]. `sequence_number` — monotonic within the session, used for ordering and deduplication. `client_version` and `schema_version` — for compatibility reasoning. `platform` — the originating platform. `target_element` — a structured identifier of the UI or game object the interaction targeted, drawn from the product's element manifest. `target_element_type` — the semantic class of the target (button, NPC, inventory slot, region, etc.). `coordinates` — for spatial interactions, the world or screen coordinates. `duration_ms` — for contextual and composite interactions, the measured duration. `outcome` — the immediate system result of the interaction (`success`, `failure`, `blocked`, `cancelled`, `noop`), distinguishing, for example, a `craft` that succeeded from one that failed for lack of materials.

The schema additionally carries a `context_ref` field — a foreign key to the Interaction Context record of I.4.2.3 — and a `feature_provenance` list, initially empty, that downstream layers populate as the interaction is aggregated into features (see [Volume III, Section III.3]). This provenance chain is what makes a behavior inference auditable back to its constituent interactions.

Optional fields are permitted and encouraged: `input_device` (mouse, touch, gamepad, keyboard), `viewport_state` (relevant for responsive UIs), `accessibility_mode_active` (a boolean that downstream layers MUST honor when comparing behavior across accessibility configurations, to avoid mistaking accessibility-driven interaction patterns for motivational differences). The `accessibility_mode_active` field is not merely informational; it is a confound-control field, and its presence is required for fair behavioral comparison per [Volume I, Section I.5.2.1.d].

**REQ-BEH-015 (Mandatory Envelope Fields):** An interaction record missing any of the mandatory fields listed above MUST be rejected at the normalization layer per [Volume VI, Section VI.1.3.7] and routed to the dead-letter queue. Partial records MUST NOT be admitted to the Behavior Layer.

**REQ-BEH-016 (Provenance Population):** The `feature_provenance` list of any interaction that contributes to a behavior classification MUST be populated with the feature IDs that consumed it. The Behavior Layer MUST be able to reconstruct, for any classified behavior, the exact set of contributing interaction records.

**REQ-BEH-017 (Accessibility Confound Control):** Behavioral comparisons across players MUST condition on `accessibility_mode_active` when the comparison concerns motivation or emotion. Failure to do so MUST be flagged as a fairness violation by the Ethics Officer Agent.

#### I.4.2.3 Interaction Context Capture

An interaction's meaning depends on the context in which it occurred. A `click` on a "Buy" button during a tutorial is categorically different from a `click` on "Buy" during an endgame economy transaction, yet both are `click` interactions of the same type. The Interaction Context Capture subsystem enriches each interaction record with the contextual state necessary for the Behavior Layer to distinguish them.

Context is captured at four levels. **Session context** includes the session's start time, total duration to the interaction, the player's session-count and tenure, the device class, and the network conditions at interaction time. **Product-state context** includes the current scene or screen, the current game state (menu, gameplay, cutscene, loading), the player's progression state (level, chapter, unlocked features), and the active modal or overlay. **Economic context** includes the player's current balance of each virtual currency, recent spend velocity, and any active promotion or discount. **Social context** includes whether the player is in a group, the group composition, recent social interactions, and the presence of nearby players (for spatially-social products).

Context is captured as a structured *context record* referenced by the interaction's `context_ref` field (see I.4.2.2). The context record is *snapshotted* at interaction time rather than referenced by a live pointer, because the product state at interaction time is the relevant state for behavioral interpretation — a later state change must not retroactively alter the interpretation of a past interaction. Snapshots are subject to a retention policy that balances auditability against storage cost; the raw interaction record is retained per [Volume VI, Section VI.3.4], while the context snapshot MAY be downsampled or summarized after a configurable window, provided the summary preserves the fields the Behavior Layer depends on.

Context capture must be *causally non-invasive*: the act of capturing context must not itself change the player's experience or the interaction's outcome. This rules out instrumentation that introduces frame stalls, network round-trips on the hot path, or visible UI artifacts. The SDK's context-capture implementation MUST satisfy the minimal-performance-impact principle of [Volume VI, Section VI.2.1.2].

**REQ-BEH-018 (Context Snapshot Immutability):** Context records MUST be immutable once written. Retroactive modification of a context record MUST be rejected. Corrections require a new context record and a redirect in the interaction's `context_ref`, with the original preserved for audit.

**REQ-BEH-019 (Context Coverage):** Every interaction record MUST carry a non-null `context_ref`. Context capture failure MUST degrade the interaction's quality score per [Volume VI, Section VI.1.4.5] and MUST NOT silently produce a context-free interaction record.

**REQ-BEH-020 (Performance Bound):** Context capture MUST add no more than 2 milliseconds of median overhead per interaction on the target device class. Exceeding this bound is a performance regression that MUST block SDK release per [Volume VI, Section VI.2.1.2].

#### I.4.2.4 Interaction-to-Behavior Mapping Rules

The boundary between the Interaction Layer and the Behavior Layer is governed by mapping rules that determine how streams of interactions are aggregated into behavioral primitives. These rules are the contract that the Interaction Layer fulfills and the Behavior Layer consumes; their precision directly determines the Behavior Layer's signal-to-noise ratio.

Mapping proceeds in three stages. **Stage 1 — Sequence Segmentation:** the interaction stream is segmented into *action episodes*, contiguous runs of interactions bounded by attentional or contextual breaks. An action episode is closed when (a) the player transitions to a different scene or major UI surface, (b) an idle period exceeds the `episode_break_threshold` (configurable per product, default 30 seconds), or (c) a session-level event (login, disconnect, AFK) intervenes. Within an episode, interactions are ordered by `sequence_number` and grouped by `target_element` proximity.

**Stage 2 — Primitive Extraction:** within each action episode, the system extracts *behavioral primitives* — minimal meaningful units such as "examined item X," "attempted craft of Y," "initiated trade with Z," "explored region R." A primitive is defined by a *pattern rule*: a typed template over interaction sequences (e.g., `craft_primitive := navigate(workbench) → click(craft_button) → [optional] click(material_select)* → click(confirm) → outcome(evaluated)`). Pattern rules are versioned, registered in the Behavior Pattern Registry (see [Volume III, Section III.6.2]), and MAY be product-specific.

**Stage 3 — Episode Aggregation:** primitives are aggregated within and across episodes into the candidate behavioral patterns that the Behavior Layer classifies (see I.4.3.2). Aggregation produces counts, durations, success rates, and temporal distributions of each primitive type, which form the input feature vector for behavior classification.

The mapping rules must handle *noise and ambiguity* robustly. A `click` on a craft button that is immediately followed by a `cancel` is a *withdrawn* craft primitive, not a completed one; the rules must classify it accordingly and propagate the withdrawal as a behavioral signal (frequent withdrawal is itself a feature relevant to emotion inference — see I.4.4.2). A `navigate` that lands the player in a region they immediately leave is an *aborted exploration*, distinct from sustained exploration.

**REQ-BEH-021 (Rule Determinism):** Mapping rules MUST be deterministic functions of the interaction stream and context. Nondeterministic mapping is forbidden; where probabilistic classification is unavoidable, it MUST occur in the Behavior Layer (I.4.3) and MUST carry a confidence value. The Interaction Layer MUST NOT emit probabilistic outputs.

**REQ-BEH-022 (Rule Registration):** Every mapping rule MUST be registered with an ID, a version, the primitive type it produces, and a declarative specification of its interaction-sequence template. Unregistered rules MUST NOT be applied in production.

**REQ-BEH-023 (Withdrawal Propagation):** Withdrawn or aborted primitives MUST be preserved as first-class records, not discarded. The Emotion Layer depends on the rate and pattern of withdrawals; their silent loss would corrupt downstream emotion inference.

---

### I.4.3 Behavior Layer

#### I.4.3.1 Behavioral Pattern Definitions

The Behavior Layer promotes interaction sequences into *behavioral patterns* — recognized, named dispositions that signify intent or habitual response rather than momentary action. A behavioral pattern is the smallest unit of behavior about which it is meaningful to ask "why does the player do this?" — a question answered upstream by the Motivation Layer. Pattern definitions are therefore the bridge between the descriptive Interaction Layer and the inferential layers above.

A behavioral pattern is formally defined by six components: (1) a **canonical name** and stable identifier, drawn from the Behavioral Ontology (see [Volume III, Section III.2.1.4]); (2) a **definition** — a precise, operationalizable statement of what the pattern consists in; (3) a **constituent primitive set** — the behavioral primitives (from I.4.2.4) that, in characteristic arrangements, constitute the pattern; (4) a **temporal signature** — the windowing and recurrence properties that distinguish a sustained pattern from a one-off; (5) a **contextual precondition set** — the product-state contexts in which the pattern is meaningful (e.g., "endgame crafting mastery" is undefined in a tutorial region); and (6) a **negative definition** — patterns with which this one is mutually exclusive or in tension, used to enforce discriminant validity at the behavior level.

PsyForge ships a starter behavioral ontology including: *Systematic Exploration* (sustained, ordered traversal of unmapped regions with low backtracking); *Grinding* (repetitive engagement with high-frequency, low-novelty primitives for resource accumulation); *Speedrunning* (minimization of time-to-objective across episodes, with explicit optimization behaviors); *Socialization* (disproportionate time in social primitives relative to solo content); *Min-Maxing* (systematic search for optimal configurations, frequent respec/adjustment, study of external or in-game reference material); *Completionism* (engagement with optional content beyond marginal utility, collection-oriented primitives); *Habitual Return* (regular, low-variance session cadence to a stable set of activities); *Impulsive Spending* (high-velocity, low-deliberation purchase primitives); *Nesting/Personalization* (the housing example: sustained, decorative, low-competitive engagement with personal-space mechanics); *Skill Practice* (repeated engagement with difficulty-appropriate challenges with measurable performance improvement); *Avoidance* (systematic disengagement from specific content types or challenge tiers).

Patterns are not mutually exclusive; a player exhibits a *profile* — a weighted vector over the pattern space — rather than a single label. Single-label behavior classification is forbidden because it falsifies the empirically observed multi-dispositional nature of player behavior (see REQ-BEH-004 and the analogous prohibition at the motivation level).

**REQ-BEH-024 (Pattern Definition Completeness):** Every behavioral pattern registered in the ontology MUST specify all six components. Incomplete definitions MUST be rejected at registration and MUST NOT be usable by pattern detection.

**REQ-BEH-025 (No Single-Label Assignment):** Behavior classification MUST produce a weighted profile over patterns, not a single dominant label. Any downstream consumer that requires a single label (e.g., for a dashboard filter) MUST be served a derived view, and the underlying profile MUST be retained.

#### I.4.3.2 Pattern Detection Algorithms

Pattern detection is the algorithmic process by which the Behavior Layer consumes behavioral primitives (from I.4.2.4) and produces a weighted behavioral profile. PsyForge defines a hybrid detection architecture that combines rule-based, sequence-based, and learned detectors, with a reconciliation step that resolves disagreements.

**Rule-based detectors** implement the explicit definition of a pattern as a deterministic or thresholded function over primitive aggregates. For *Grinding*, the rule might be: "within a 1-hour window, ≥ 60% of primitives are of a single repetitive type with < 5% novel primitives and resource accumulation as the dominant outcome." Rule-based detectors are transparent, auditable, and cheap, and are preferred wherever a pattern admits a crisp operational definition. They are registered in the Pattern Detection Registry with their threshold parameters exposed for tuning.

**Sequence-based detectors** handle patterns whose essence is *temporal order* rather than aggregate counts. *Speedrunning*, for instance, is defined not by how many primitives occur but by their optimization-aligned ordering. Sequence detectors use sequential pattern mining (see [Volume III, Section III.6.1.1]) and sequence-embedding similarity (see [Volume III, Section III.4.2.2]) to match observed episode sequences against canonical pattern signatures. Sequence detectors emit a similarity score normalized to [0, 1].

**Learned detectors** handle patterns that resist explicit definition but are reliably recognizable from feature distributions. *Min-Maxing* and *Avoidance* often fall here. Learned detectors are supervised classifiers (gradient-boosted trees, small neural nets) trained on labeled exemplar populations, with the labels sourced from human annotators or from high-confidence rule-detected exemplars used as weak supervision. Learned detectors MUST publish a model card per [Volume III, Section III.5.5.3] documenting training data, biases, and known failure modes, and MUST be re-validated on a cadence per [Volume III, Section III.5.5.5].

**Reconciliation** merges the outputs of applicable detectors into a single weighted profile. Where detectors agree, confidence is boosted (the profile weight for a pattern is a function of the number and agreement of corroborating detectors). Where detectors disagree — a rule detector says *Exploration* while a sequence detector says *Speedrunning* — the reconciliation layer does not pick a winner; it carries both with reduced confidence and flags the disagreement as a candidate for human review or for additional primitive collection. Disagreement is information, not error.

**REQ-BEH-026 (Detector Transparency):** Every detector MUST be traceable: given a behavioral profile output, the system MUST be able to identify which detector produced which weight, on which primitives, with which parameters. Opaque detector outputs MUST NOT be admitted to the Knowledge Graph.

**REQ-BEH-027 (Learned-Detector Model Card):** Learned detectors MUST have a current, reviewed model card. Detectors whose model card is stale beyond the re-validation cadence MUST be disabled and their outputs quarantined.

**REQ-BEH-028 (Disagreement Preservation):** Reconciliation MUST NOT silently discard detector disagreement. Disagreements MUST be persisted as profile metadata and surfaced to downstream layers, which MUST account for the disagreement in their confidence computation.

#### I.4.3.3 Temporal Behavior Windows

Behavior is intrinsically temporal: the same primitives, arranged differently in time, signify different patterns. The Behavior Layer therefore operates over *temporal windows* — bounded slices of the interaction stream within which pattern detection is applied — and the choice of window fundamentally shapes what is detectable.

PsyForge defines three normative window classes. **Micro-windows** (seconds to a few minutes) capture within-episode behavior and are the primary input to emotion inference (I.4.4.2), where micro-features like inter-action latency, pause-before-confirm durations, and rapid retry cadence betray transient affect. **Meso-windows** (a single session, typically 20 minutes to several hours) capture within-session behavioral profiles and are the primary input to session-level behavior classification and to the behavioral state machine (I.4.3.4). **Macro-windows** (days to weeks, spanning many sessions) capture enduring dispositions and are the primary input to motivation inference (I.4.5) and motivational-shift detection (I.4.5.5).

Each window class has its own semantics for boundaries, overlap, and aggregation. Micro-windows are *sliding* windows with configurable step (default 1 second) and width (default 30 seconds), producing a continuous trace of micro-features suitable for state-machine transition detection. Meso-windows are *session-aligned* — they open at session start and close at session end, plus optional grace periods for late-arriving events — and produce one profile per session plus a session-sequence trace. Macro-windows are *tumbling* with configurable width (default 7 days) and produce rolling profiles that support drift and shift detection.

The choice of window parameters is not neutral. A micro-window too narrow will miss pauses that constitute the emotional signal; too wide will average it away. A macro-window too narrow will mistake session-to-session variance for motivational shift; too wide will miss genuine shifts. PsyForge therefore treats window parameters as *tunable, validated* configuration, not as fixed constants, and requires that any change to default window parameters be accompanied by a re-validation of the affected downstream inferences against ground truth (per the validity criteria of I.4.1.5).

Cross-window reasoning is necessary because patterns manifest at multiple scales. *Grinding* is detectable in a meso-window (a session dominated by repetitive primitives) but is only *diagnostic of a disposition* when it recurs across macro-windows. The Behavior Layer MUST propagate meso-window profiles into macro-window aggregation with explicit recurrence statistics — frequency, regularity, trend — so that the Motivation Layer can distinguish a habitual grinder from a player who ground once for a specific goal.

**REQ-BEH-029 (Window Parameter Validation):** Changes to default window parameters MUST trigger a re-validation of downstream emotion and motivation inferences. Validation results MUST be recorded; failures MUST block the parameter change.

**REQ-BEH-030 (Cross-Window Provenance):** A macro-window profile MUST be traceable to its constituent meso-window profiles, which MUST be traceable to their constituent micro-window features. The full provenance chain MUST be reconstructable for any behavior classification used in a recommendation.

#### I.4.3.4 Behavioral State Machine

Within and across sessions, a player's behavioral state is not static; it transitions. A player moves from *Exploration* to *Combat Engagement* to *Socialization* to *Disengagement*, and these transitions carry information that static profiles discard. The Behavioral State Machine (BSM) is the formal model that captures these transitions and exposes them to the Emotion and Motivation layers, which depend critically on transition patterns (a player who oscillates between Engagement and Disengagement is exhibiting a different affective state than one who monotonically disengages).

The BSM is a finite-state model whose states are *behavioral modes* — coarse-grained, mutually-exclusive categories suitable for transition reasoning, distinct from the multi-label pattern profiles of I.4.3.1. The normative state set is: `Engaged_Productive` (active progress toward an in-product goal), `Engaged_Exploratory` (active but non-goal-directed), `Social` (interaction-centric), `Transactional` (economy-centric), `Idle_In_Session` (session active, no input), `Disengaging` (declining input cadence, session-wind-down signals), `Re_Engaging` (recovering from a disengagement episode). States are not patterns; they are the modes between which patterns are exhibited.

Transitions are governed by transition probabilities estimated from the player's own history and from population priors, using the player model of [Volume III, Part III.5]. Transitions are *labeled* with their triggering primitive signatures: the `Engaged_Productive → Disengaging` transition is associated with repeated-failure primitives, escalating pause durations, and session-clock proximity to typical exit times. These transition labels are the primary input to emotion inference (a transition to `Disengaging` after repeated failures supports a *frustration* inference; the same transition after long successful play supports a *satisfaction-and-wind-down* inference).

The BSM operates at the meso-window granularity (within-session) and emits a state trace that the Emotion Layer consumes. Cross-session transitions (the `Disengaging → Re_Engaging` arc across days) feed the Motivation Layer's persistence and decay models (see I.4.4.4 and I.4.5.5).

**REQ-BEH-031 (State Exclusivity):** At any moment, a player MUST be in exactly one BSM state. The system MUST NOT report concurrent states. (Concurrent behavioral *patterns* are permitted and normal per I.4.3.1; concurrent *modes* are not.)

**REQ-BEH-032 (Transition Attribution):** Every state transition MUST carry a `trigger_signature` identifying the primitive pattern that most strongly contributed to the transition. Transitions without attribution MUST be marked `unattributed` and downgraded in confidence for downstream emotion inference.

**REQ-BEH-033 (BSM Validation):** The BSM's transition probabilities MUST be periodically re-estimated against realized transitions. Persistent divergence between estimated and realized transition rates is a model-drift condition per [Volume III, Section III.5.5.5] and MUST trigger retraining.

#### I.4.3.5 Behavior Classification Confidence Thresholds

Behavior classification produces probabilistic outputs, and probabilistic outputs demand explicit confidence thresholds that govern how the outputs are used downstream. This subsection defines the threshold structure and the operational rules that attach to it. The thresholds are not arbitrary tuning knobs; they are the contract by which the Behavior Layer promises a defined quality of evidence to the Emotion and Motivation layers, and ultimately to the Ethics and Recommendation gates.

PsyForge defines four confidence tiers. **High confidence** (≥ 0.85) indicates a behavior classification supported by multiple corroborating detectors, sufficient primitive volume, and a stable temporal signature. High-confidence classifications MAY be consumed directly by downstream layers and MAY contribute to recommendations without additional human review of the classification itself. **Moderate confidence** (0.65–0.85) indicates a classification supported by evidence but with meaningful uncertainty — typically a single dominant detector, marginal primitive volume, or borderline temporal stability. Moderate-confidence classifications MAY be consumed downstream but MUST propagate their uncertainty, and any recommendation resting substantially on a moderate-confidence classification MUST carry that uncertainty into its ethics score (see [Volume I, Section I.5.2]). **Low confidence** (0.40–0.65) indicates a tentative classification unsuitable for action. Low-confidence classifications MAY be used for exploratory analysis and MAY inform data-collection priorities (the system SHOULD collect more primitives to resolve the uncertainty) but MUST NOT anchor any recommendation. **Insufficient** (< 0.40) indicates that no classification is supportable; the system MUST emit an explicit `no_behavior_inferred` marker rather than a default classification.

Confidence is computed from four factors: detector agreement (the variance of detector outputs for the pattern), primitive volume (the count of supporting primitives relative to a pattern-specific minimum), temporal stability (the consistency of the pattern across the relevant window class), and population-base-rate alignment (whether the pattern's prevalence in this player is consistent with the player's archetype prior — see [Volume III, Section III.5.2]). The combination function is a weighted geometric mean whose weights are published and tunable, with the constraint that no factor may be driven to zero weight (each factor must retain at least 10% influence).

Thresholds are not universal; they are pattern-specific. *Impulsive Spending* at moderate confidence is more consequential than *Systematic Exploration* at moderate confidence, because the former can anchor a monetization recommendation with manipulation risk. Pattern-specific threshold overrides MUST be reviewed by the Ethics Officer Agent (see [Volume V, Section V.11]).

**REQ-BEH-034 (No-Default Classification):** When confidence falls below the low-confidence threshold, the system MUST emit `no_behavior_inferred` rather than defaulting to the most likely classification. Defaulting is forbidden because it manufactures false evidence.

**REQ-BEH-035 (Consequence-Tiered Thresholds):** Patterns that can anchor monetization, churn-intervention, or vulnerable-population recommendations MUST have their threshold overrides reviewed and approved by the Ethics Officer Agent. Unreviewed overrides for such patterns MUST NOT be active in production.

**REQ-BEH-036 (Confidence Propagation):** Downstream layers MUST treat upstream confidence as a multiplier on their own output confidence, not as a binary gate. An emotion inference resting on a moderate-confidence behavior classification MUST have its own confidence bounded above by the behavior classification's confidence.

---

### I.4.4 Emotion Layer

#### I.4.4.1 Emotion Taxonomy (Pleasure, Frustration, Flow, Boredom, Anxiety, etc.)

The Emotion Layer is the most epistemically delicate layer of the Behavioral Stack, because it makes claims about a category of state — subjective affect — that the system cannot observe directly. The taxonomy it uses therefore carries unusual weight: an over-broad taxonomy invites false precision, while an over-narrow one erases signal that the Behavior Layer genuinely supports. PsyForge's emotion taxonomy is deliberately *instrumented* rather than *comprehensive* — it includes only affective states for which defensible behavioral correlates exist and for which downstream reasoning (motivation, mechanic-emotion feedback) has a defined use.

The taxonomy is organized as a two-dimensional affective space augmented with discrete game-relevant states, drawing on the circumplex model and on flow theory (see [Volume III, Section III.2.2.2]). The two continuous axes are **Valence** (unpleasant ↔ pleasant, range [−1, +1]) and **Arousal** (deactivation ↔ activation, range [−1, +1]). Discrete states are regions or attractors within this space: *Pleasure* (high valence, moderate arousal), *Frustration* (low valence, high arousal), *Anxiety* (low valence, high arousal with avoidant rather than aggressive behavioral signature), *Flow* (moderate-high valence, moderate arousal, with the characteristic signature of sustained, low-variance, challenge-matched engagement), *Boredom* (low valence, low arousal, with exploration and disengagement signatures), *Relaxation* (moderate valence, low arousal, with social and routine signatures), *Excitement* (high valence, high arousal, with novelty and reward signatures), and *Disengagement-Satisfaction* (the wind-down state of a satisfying session, distinct from boredom by its post-success context).

Three states deserve particular care. **Flow** is included because it is the most-valued affective state for engagement design and because it has a well-validated behavioral signature (Csikszentmihalyi's challenge-skill balance; see [Volume III, Section III.2.2.2]). However, flow is *not* a single emotion; it is a structured condition of optimal engagement, and the system MUST represent it as a composite inference requiring both the challenge-skill balance signal and the behavioral steadiness signal. **Anxiety** is included but MUST be handled with the heightened ethical scrutiny specified in I.4.4.5: anxiety inference about a player is a sensitive claim with potential to drive harmful interventions, and PsyForge MUST NOT use anxiety inference to design anxiety-exploitative mechanics. **Frustration** is the most behaviorally legible negative affect and is the primary signal for difficulty-tuning recommendations; it is also the state most easily confused with *productive struggle*, and the taxonomy MUST distinguish them via the success-after-frustration signature (productive struggle resolves to mastery; unproductive frustration resolves to disengagement).

The taxonomy is *open under version control*: new states MAY be added via the corpus contribution process (see [Volume III, Section III.2.1.4]), but each addition MUST specify its valence-arousal coordinates, its behavioral correlates, its discriminant boundary against existing states, and an ethical-impact assessment. The system MUST NOT use unregistered emotion labels.

**REQ-BEH-037 (Composite Flow Representation):** Flow MUST be inferred as a composite requiring both challenge-skill balance and behavioral steadiness signals. A flow inference lacking either component MUST be downgraded to `flow_candidate` and MUST NOT be reported as confirmed flow.

**REQ-BEH-038 (Anxiety Ethical Gate):** Any recommendation derived from an anxiety inference MUST pass an explicit Ethics Officer review (see [Volume V, Section V.11.5]) and MUST be tested for anxiety-exploitation risk. Recommendations that would amplify player anxiety to drive engagement MUST be rejected.

**REQ-BEH-039 (Registered-Only Labels):** Emotion labels MUST come from the registered taxonomy. Unregistered labels MUST be rejected at the Emotion Layer boundary.

#### I.4.4.2 Emotion Inference from Behavioral Signals

Emotion inference is the process by which behavioral signals — micro-features of interaction timing, pacing, abandon patterns, and context — are mapped to the affective taxonomy. The fundamental epistemic commitment of this subsection, and of the Emotion Layer as a whole, is that **PsyForge infers emotions from behavior; it does not measure them.** This distinction is not hedging; it is the load-bearing epistemic fact that determines what the layer may and may not claim, and it is enforced architecturally (see I.4.4.5).

The inference consumes three signal classes. **Temporal micro-features** include inter-action latency distributions, pause-before-commit durations (a long pause before a confirm click is a deliberation signal, distinct from a long pause before a navigate which is an attention signal), rapid-retry cadence after failure (a frustration correlate), and session-clock-to-typical-exit proximity (a wind-down signal). **Outcome-sequence features** include the recent success/failure ratio, the variance of outcomes, the presence of escalating-difficulty encounters, and the player's response to failure (immediate retry vs. withdrawal vs. context-switch). **Contextual-affective features** include the challenge-skill balance (for flow inference), the novelty of recent content (for excitement/boredom), and the social-density of recent activity (for social-affect inference).

Inference is performed by a hybrid model: a rule-based component encodes the corpus-grounded behavioral correlates (e.g., "rapid retries with escalating failure and rising inter-action latency variance → frustration, valence ≈ −0.6, arousal ≈ +0.7"), and a learned component calibrates the rule outputs to the player's own baseline. The learned component is essential because the *same* behavioral signal means different things for different players: a 2-second pause before confirm is deliberation for a thoughtful player and hesitation for an impulsive one. The learned component therefore conditions inference on the player's behavioral baseline, drawn from the player model (see [Volume III, Part III.5]).

Outputs are *distributions* over the affective space, not point estimates. The Emotion Layer emits, for each inference window, a probability distribution over discrete states and a (valence, arousal) point with a confidence ellipse. The distributional form is mandatory: it preserves the genuine uncertainty of the inference and prevents the downstream illusion of certainty.

**REQ-BEH-040 (Inference-Not-Measurement Language):** All Emotion Layer outputs, reports, and API responses MUST use inferential language ("inferred frustration," "frustration probability") and MUST NOT use measurement language ("measured frustration," "frustration level"). This is an architectural constraint on the layer's output contract, enforced at the schema level.

**REQ-BEH-041 (Distributional Output):** Emotion inferences MUST be emitted as distributions, not point estimates. The valence-arousal point MUST be accompanied by a confidence ellipse. Point-only outputs MUST be rejected downstream.

**REQ-BEH-042 (Baseline Conditioning):** Emotion inference MUST condition on the player's behavioral baseline. Population-only inference (treating all players identically) is forbidden because it manufactures false confidence about individual affect.

#### I.4.4.3 Emotion-to-Motivation Mapping

The Emotion Layer does not exist in isolation; its purpose is to inform the Motivation Layer about the affective context in which motivations operate. The mapping from emotion to motivation is *probabilistic and many-to-many*: a single emotion can be consistent with multiple motivations, and a single motivation can produce multiple emotions depending on whether it is being satisfied or thwarted. The mapping therefore supplies *evidence* about motivation, not a determination of it.

The mapping is grounded in the corpus, primarily in Self-Determination Theory (SDT) and in flow theory (see [Volume III, Section III.2.4.1] and [Volume III, Section III.2.2.2]). The SDT framing is especially useful because it predicts affective consequences of need satisfaction and thwarting: *satisfied competence* produces flow and pleasure; *thwarted competence* produces frustration and, sustained, anxiety and boredom; *satisfied autonomy* produces pleasure and engagement; *thwarted autonomy* produces resentment (operationalized as low-valence, low-arousal disengagement); *satisfied relatedness* produces pleasure and relaxation; *thwarted relatedness* produces loneliness (operationalized as low-valence social withdrawal). These mappings are bidirectional: an observed pattern of competence-satisfaction emotion supports a competence-motivation inference, and a competence-motivation inference predicts competence-satisfaction emotion under conditions of successful challenge.

Flow occupies a special role: flow is the affective signature of *optimal motivation-mechanic fit*. When the Emotion Layer detects flow and the Motivation Layer detects a corresponding motivation (e.g., mastery), the conjunction is strong evidence that the engaged mechanic is well-serving that motivation — the gold-standard condition the Game Designer Agent seeks to replicate (see [Volume V, Section V.8]). Conversely, flow in the absence of a corresponding motivation is suspicious and may indicate a variable-reward compulsion pattern rather than genuine engagement; such a conjunction MUST be flagged for ethics review (see I.4.6.4 and [Volume I, Section I.5.1.3]).

The mapping is performed by a probabilistic graphical model whose structure is corpus-grounded and whose parameters are learned from labeled exemplars and from experimental data (where emotions and motivations are jointly validated via survey instruments; see I.4.4.5 and I.4.8.4). The model emits, for each motivation archetype, a posterior updated by the recent emotion trajectory. These posteriors are *soft* updates to the Motivation Layer's existing estimates; they do not override longer-horizon motivational inference.

**REQ-BEH-043 (Many-to-Many Mapping):** Emotion-to-motivation mapping MUST preserve the many-to-many structure. A single emotion MUST NOT be mapped to a single motivation deterministically. The mapping output MUST be a distribution over motivations.

**REQ-BEH-044 (Flow-Motivation Conjunction):** When flow is detected, the system MUST evaluate the conjunction against the Motivation Layer's current estimates. Flow without a corresponding motivation MUST be flagged for ethics review as a potential compulsion pattern.

#### I.4.4.4 Emotional State Persistence and Decay

Emotions are transient, but they are not memoryless. A frustrating failure at minute 10 of a session colors the player's affective state at minute 15 even if no further failure occurs, and a state of flow, once entered, has momentum that sustains engagement beyond the immediate challenge-skill balance. The Emotion Layer models this persistence and decay explicitly, because failing to do so would treat each inference window as independent and systematically underestimate affective momentum.

Persistence is modeled as a decaying influence of past emotion estimates on the current estimate, with two decay parameters: an *affective half-life* (the time over which a past emotion's direct influence halves, default 5 minutes, configurable per product and per emotion — frustration decays more slowly than excitement) and a *carryover ceiling* (the maximum fraction of the current estimate that can be attributable to carryover rather than current evidence, default 40%, to prevent carryover from overwhelming current signal). Decay is *event-gated*: a strong disconfirming event (a success after frustration, a social connection after loneliness) accelerates decay; a confirming event (another failure after frustration) resets and amplifies.

Cross-session persistence is handled separately and more cautiously. A player's emotional state at session end influences, weakly, the affective baseline of the next session — but this influence is *small* (default carryover ceiling 15% across sessions) and *decays geometrically* across consecutive sessions, so that a single frustrating session does not permanently color the player's inferred baseline. Sustained negative affect across many sessions is, however, a strong signal — not of transient emotion but of a motivational or mechanic problem, and the system MUST promote sustained-negative-affect patterns to the Motivation Layer as evidence of motivational thwarting rather than treating them as repeated independent emotional events.

The persistence model is also the mechanism by which the Emotion Layer detects *emotional arcs* within a session — the narrative-shaped trajectory of affect that the Narrative Designer Agent (see [Volume V, Section V.9]) reasons about for content-pacing recommendations. An arc from boredom through frustration to flow to satisfaction is a complete and satisfying arc; a flat negative affect across a session is an arc failure that the designer should investigate.

**REQ-BEH-045 (Decay Parameter Publication):** Affective half-lives and carryover ceilings MUST be published as configurable parameters with documented defaults and per-emotion overrides. Hidden decay parameters are forbidden because they make the model's behavior unaccountable.

**REQ-BEH-046 (Sustained-Negative-Affect Promotion):** Sustained negative affect across multiple sessions MUST be promoted to the Motivation Layer as evidence of motivational thwarting, not retained as repeated emotional events. The promotion MUST carry a `sustained_pattern` marker distinct from single-session emotion inferences.

**REQ-BEH-047 (Arc Detection):** The Emotion Layer MUST expose within-session affective trajectories as first-class outputs for arc reasoning. Flat trajectories of negative affect MUST be flagged as arc failures for the Narrative Designer Agent.

#### I.4.4.5 Emotion Measurement Validity and Ethical Limits

This subsection states the ethical and epistemic limits within which the Emotion Layer operates. These limits are not advisory; they are architectural constraints enforced by the Ethics Layer (see [Volume I, Section I.5.4]) and by the Ethics Officer Agent (see [Volume V, Section V.11]), and violations are gating failures, not soft warnings.

**The fundamental limit: PsyForge does not measure emotions.** The system observes behavior and infers affect. The bandwidth of behavior-to-affect mapping is inherently limited: multiple affective states produce behaviorally indistinguishable signals (a pause before confirm can be deliberation, hesitation, distraction, or motor delay), and the same affective state produces different behaviors in different players. Every emotion inference therefore carries irreducible uncertainty that no amount of behavioral data can eliminate. The system MUST represent this uncertainty honestly (per REQ-BEH-041) and MUST NOT present emotion inferences as facts about the player's internal state.

**The consent limit.** Inferring a person's emotional state from their behavior is a form of sensitive processing. PsyForge MUST obtain informed consent for emotion inference specifically — not buried in a general telemetry consent, but as a distinct, explainable consent item per [Volume VI, Section VI.3.2.5]. Players MUST be able to opt out of emotion inference while retaining the benefits of behavior-level and outcome-level analytics. The Emotion Layer MUST be architecturally isolable so that it can be disabled per-player without disabling the rest of the stack.

**The vulnerability limit.** Certain emotion inferences are categorically more sensitive: anxiety, depression-adjacent affect, addiction-adjacent compulsion patterns. These inferences MUST be subject to heightened protections: they MUST NOT be used to target the player with content designed to exploit the inferred vulnerability (the manipulation boundary, see [Volume I, Section I.5.1.3]); they MUST NOT be exposed to third parties without explicit, separate consent; and where the inferred state indicates potential harm (e.g., patterns consistent with problematic gaming), the system MUST surface this to designated human reviewers rather than to automated monetization paths.

**The actionability limit.** Emotion inferences are valuable insofar as they inform design improvements that benefit the player (better difficulty curves, better pacing, less frustrating onboarding). They are NOT valuable — and MUST NOT be used — as tools for individual psychological profiling exposed to non-design stakeholders, for personnel decisions, for insurance or credit assessment, or for any purpose unrelated to product improvement. The data-subject rights provisions of [Volume VI, Section VI.3.3] apply in full, including the right to access emotion inferences about oneself and the right to request their deletion and the deletion of derived models (per [Volume VI, Section VI.3.3.7]).

**The validation limit.** Emotion inferences are validated, where ethically possible, against self-report instruments (brief in-experience surveys, voluntary post-session affect checklists) and against the experimental methodology of I.4.8.4. Where validation data is unavailable, emotion inferences MUST be downgraded in confidence tier and MUST NOT anchor high-stakes recommendations.

**REQ-BEH-048 (Distinct Consent):** Emotion inference MUST operate under a consent item distinct from general telemetry consent. The Emotion Layer MUST be disableable per-player without disabling the rest of the stack.

**REQ-BEH-049 (Vulnerability Protections):** Inferences of anxiety, depression-adjacent, or compulsion-adjacent affect MUST be (a) excluded from monetization-relevant agent context, (b) excluded from third-party data sharing, and (c) surfaced to human review rather than automated action. Violation of this requirement is an ethics incident per [Volume I, Section I.5.3.5].

**REQ-BEH-050 (Purpose Limitation):** Emotion inferences MUST be used only for product-improvement purposes. Use for individual psychological profiling exposed to non-design stakeholders, personnel decisions, insurance, credit, or any non-product purpose is forbidden and MUST be blocked at the data-governance layer.

**REQ-BEH-051 (Validation-Gated Confidence):** Emotion inferences without validation evidence (self-report or experimental) MUST be capped at the `bronze` validity tier per REQ-BEH-011 and MUST NOT anchor high-stakes recommendations.

---

### I.4.5 Motivation Layer

#### I.4.5.1 Motivation Theory Frameworks (SDT, Fogg, BJ Fogg Behavior Model, etc.)

The Motivation Layer explains *why* behaviors recur by reference to the psychological drivers that the Behavior and Emotion layers surface as evidence. To do so rigorously, the layer is grounded in established motivation theory rather than in ad hoc motivational labels. PsyForge's corpus (see [Volume III, Part III.2]) supplies the formal frameworks; this subsection specifies how they are operationalized within the Behavioral Stack.

Four frameworks are normatively integrated. **Self-Determination Theory (SDT)** is the primary framework because its three needs — *autonomy*, *competence*, and *relatedness* — are well-validated, map cleanly to game and product behaviors, and have established affective consequences (see I.4.4.3). SDT supplies the layer's backbone: most motivation inferences are decomposed into autonomy, competence, and relatedness components, with the option to extend to the SDT motivation continuum (amotivation, extrinsic, intrinsic) for finer resolution. **The Fogg Behavior Model (B=MAP)** — Behavior equals Motivation times Ability times Prompt — is used diagnostically: when a behavior fails to occur despite an inferred motivation, the layer uses Fogg to distinguish motivational deficit from ability deficit from prompt absence. This distinction is critical for recommendations: a missing behavior due to ability deficit is solved by difficulty tuning, not by motivational design. **The Hook Model** (Trigger → Action → Variable Reward → Investment) is used to detect habit-formation patterns and, crucially, to detect their unhealthy escalation into compulsion (see the manipulation boundary, [Volume I, Section I.5.1.3]). **Goal-Setting Theory** and **Expectancy Theory** are used for long-horizon motivation: persistence toward distant goals and the calibration of effort to expected reward.

The frameworks are not used in isolation; they are *reconciled* into a unified motivation representation. SDT supplies the *what* (which needs are active); Fogg supplies the *why-now* (why a motivated behavior did or did not occur); the Hook Model supplies the *habituation* dimension; Goal-Setting and Expectancy supply the *temporal-horizon* dimension. The unified representation is a vector over the motivation archetypes of I.4.5.2, augmented with the diagnostic and temporal dimensions.

Corpus-grounded operation is mandatory: every motivational inference MUST cite the corpus framework(s) from which its reasoning is derived, and inferences that cannot be grounded in a registered framework MUST be rejected. This is the mechanism by which PsyForge avoids the ad hoc motivational storytelling that plagues flat analytics ("the player is motivated by social status" stated without theoretical grounding is unfalsifiable).

**REQ-BEH-052 (Framework Citation):** Every motivation inference MUST cite the corpus framework(s) supporting it. Uncited motivational claims MUST be rejected at the Motivation Layer boundary.

**REQ-BEH-053 (Diagnostic Decomposition):** When a motivated behavior fails to occur, the layer MUST perform a Fogg-style diagnostic decomposition (motivation vs. ability vs. prompt) before attributing the absence to motivational change. Misattribution of ability or prompt deficits to motivational decline is forbidden.

**REQ-BEH-054 (Hook-Model Escalation Detection):** Patterns matching the Hook Model's variable-reward-investment cycle MUST be monitored for unhealthy escalation. Escalation patterns meeting the corpus's problematic-engagement criteria (see [Volume III, Section III.2.2.5]) MUST be flagged to the Ethics Officer Agent and MUST NOT be used to optimize engagement.

#### I.4.5.2 Motivation Archetypes (Achievement, Social, Immersion, Mastery, etc.)

Motivation frameworks supply the theory; motivation archetypes supply the operational vocabulary by which PsyForge communicates motivational structure to agents and to humans. An archetype is a named, corpus-grounded cluster of motivational drivers that is recognizable across products and that has distinct implications for mechanic design and outcome prediction.

The normative archetype set, drawn from and reconciling Bartle's player types, Quantic Foundry's motivation taxonomy, and SDT (see [Volume III, Section III.2.2.1]), is: **Achievement** (progression, completion, mastery-of-systems, leaderboard rank); **Social** (relatedness, group belonging, reputation, cooperation); **Immersion** (narrative engagement, role-play, world-presence, aesthetic appreciation); **Mastery** (skill development for its own sake, distinct from Achievement by its intrinsic rather than extrinsic framing); **Autonomy** (self-direction, exploration, freedom from externally-imposed goals); **Collection** (completionism, acquisition, catalog completion); **Competition** (zero-sum social comparison, dominance); **Expression** (identity construction, personalization, the housing-example's identity motivation); **Stimulation** (novelty-seeking, arousal-seeking, sensation-seeking); and **Security** (stability, predictability, low-risk engagement, often a Veteran-population driver).

Archetypes are not player types — a player is not *an* Achievement player. A player has a *motivation profile*, a weighted vector over the archetype space, whose weights shift over time (see I.4.5.5). This is the same multi-label discipline enforced at the behavior level (REQ-BEH-025) and for the same reason: single-label motivation assignment falsifies the empirically observed multi-motivational structure of human engagement.

Archetypes carry *mechanic affinities* — declared, corpus-grounded mappings to mechanic categories (see I.4.6.1) — that the Mechanic Layer uses to evaluate mechanic-motivation fit. The *Expression* archetype has a strong affinity to housing/personalization mechanics; the *Achievement* archetype to progression and leaderboard mechanics; the *Mastery* archetype to skill-challenge and practice mechanics. These affinities are *probabilistic priors*, not deterministic rules: an Achievement-motivated player MAY engage deeply with housing if the housing offers achievement-style progression, and the layer MUST permit such cross-affinity engagement rather than forcing the player into the archetype's default affinities.

The archetype set is extensible via the corpus contribution process (see [Volume III, Section III.2.1.4]), with each addition requiring a corpus-grounded definition, a discriminant boundary against existing archetypes, a declared mechanic-affinity prior, and an ethical-impact assessment. Studio-defined custom archetypes are permitted (see [Volume III, Section III.5.2.4]) but MUST be mapped back to the standard set for cross-product reasoning.

**REQ-BEH-055 (Profile, Not Label):** Motivation inference MUST produce a weighted profile over archetypes. Single-archetype assignment is forbidden at the Motivation Layer.

**REQ-BEH-056 (Affinity as Prior):** Mechanic affinities MUST be treated as probabilistic priors. Cross-affinity engagement MUST be permitted and MUST NOT be treated as a classification error.

**REQ-BEH-057 (Custom-Archetype Mapping):** Studio-defined custom archetypes MUST declare a mapping to the standard set. Unmapped custom archetypes MUST NOT participate in cross-product or cross-tenant reasoning.

#### I.4.5.3 Motivation-to-Mechanic Linkage

The Motivation Layer's outputs are consumed by the Mechanic Layer to evaluate whether the product's mechanics are serving the motivations the player actually has. This linkage is the analytic core of design recommendation: a mechanic that serves a motivation the player lacks is wasted engineering; a motivation the player has that no mechanic serves is an unmet-need opportunity; a mechanic that inadvertently thwarts an active motivation is a design defect driving disengagement.

The linkage is modeled as a bipartite graph between motivation archetypes and mechanic categories (see I.4.6.1), with edge weights representing the *served-motivation strength* of each mechanic for each archetype. The graph is initialized from corpus priors (e.g., the corpus asserts that leaderboards strongly serve Achievement and Competition, weakly serve Social, and do not serve Autonomy) and refined per-tenant from observed mechanic-engagement data: a mechanic that engages Achievement-motivated players disproportionately, controlling for ability and prompt, is empirically raising its Achievement-served weight.

The linkage supports three reasonings that the Game Designer and Monetization Advisor agents consume (see [Volume V, Sections V.8 and V.10]). **Fit analysis** identifies mechanics whose served-motivation profile matches the player population's motivation profile — these are well-designed mechanics. **Gap analysis** identifies motivations present in the population that no mechanic serves — these are unmet-need opportunities and candidate recommendations. **Conflict analysis** identifies mechanics that serve one motivation while thwarting another active in the same player — for example, a monetization mechanic that serves Achievement via pay-to-progress but thwarts Autonomy by imposing externally-structured goals. Conflicts are not necessarily defects, but they MUST be surfaced for ethics review because mechanic-motivation conflicts are the primary substrate of player dissatisfaction and of manipulation risk.

The linkage is *causally evaluated*, not merely correlational. A high engagement of Achievement-motivated players with a mechanic is not, by itself, evidence that the mechanic *serves* Achievement — the players might be engaging for Stimulation reasons while incidentally being Achievement-motivated. The causal evaluation uses the cross-layer causal inference model (I.4.1.3): a mechanic-motivation link is upgraded from `correlational_only` to `causal` only when intervention analysis (changing the mechanic and observing motivation-aligned outcome changes) or mediation analysis supports the causal interpretation.

**REQ-BEH-058 (Gap and Conflict Surfacing):** The Motivation Layer MUST surface unmet-need gaps and mechanic-motivation conflicts to the relevant agents. Conflicts involving autonomy thwarting MUST be prioritized for ethics review.

**REQ-BEH-059 (Causal Upgrade Requirement):** Mechanic-motivation linkages MUST be typed `correlational_only` until causal evidence supports upgrade. `correlational_only` linkages MUST NOT anchor deployment recommendations without an accompanying sensitivity analysis per REQ-BEH-005.

#### I.4.5.4 Motivational Conflict and Competition

A player's motivations are not always harmonious. The Autonomy-driven desire for self-directed exploration conflicts with the Achievement-driven desire for externally-validated progression; the Social desire for belonging conflicts with the Competition desire for dominance; the Stimulation desire for novelty conflicts with the Security desire for stability. These intra-player motivational conflicts are not pathological — they are the normal structure of human motivation — but they have direct consequences for design: a product that serves one motivation while thwarting a co-active one generates predictable dissatisfaction, and a product that fails to recognize the conflict will misattribute the dissatisfaction to the served motivation rather than to the thwarted one.

PsyForge models motivational conflict at two levels. **Intra-profile conflict** is the tension between co-active motivations within a single player, measured by the product of their profile weights and their declared conflict strength (a corpus-grounded parameter: Autonomy-Achievement conflict is moderate, Social-Competition is high, Stimulation-Security is high). Intra-profile conflict is not a defect to be eliminated; it is a design constraint. A well-designed product *channels* conflict productively (e.g., leaderboards that offer both Competition and Social affiliation) rather than *resolving* it by suppressing one motivation.

**Inter-population motivational competition** is the tension between the motivational profiles of different player segments served by the same mechanic. A mechanic tuned to satisfy high-Achievement players may thwart the Autonomy of low-Achievement players; a social system tuned for high-Social players may alienate high-Security players who find forced socialization intrusive. Inter-population competition is the substrate of the "no design pleases everyone" problem and is the reason the simulation engine runs heterogeneous-population scenarios (see [Volume IV, Section IV.5.2.4]).

Conflict detection is performed by the Motivation Layer and surfaced to the Game Designer and Ethics Officer agents. Recommendations that resolve one conflict by creating another MUST have the trade-off made explicit in the recommendation (per the explainability requirements of [Volume III, Section III.7.3]); hidden trade-offs are forbidden because they prevent human reviewers from evaluating the recommendation honestly.

**REQ-BEH-060 (Conflict Channeling):** The Motivation Layer MUST distinguish conflict *resolution* (suppressing one motivation) from conflict *channeling* (serving both via integrated design). Recommendations MUST prefer channeling over resolution and MUST justify any resolution approach.

**REQ-BEH-061 (Explicit Trade-offs):** Recommendations that trade one motivational conflict for another MUST make the trade-off explicit in the recommendation's explanation. Hidden trade-offs MUST be flagged as explainability violations.

#### I.4.5.5 Motivational Shift Detection Over Time

Motivations are not fixed. A player who enters a product Mastery-motivated may, after achieving competence, shift toward Expression (personalization of the mastered skill) or toward Social (sharing mastery with a community); a player whose Autonomy is satisfied may eventually seek Achievement (externally-validated goals) to structure the now-unbounded freedom. These shifts are predictable, often healthy, and central to long-horizon design: a product that serves a player's motivations at week 1 but not at week 12 will lose a player who has *outgrown* the product's motivational offering — a churn cause that flat retention analytics cannot diagnose.

Motivational shift detection operates on macro-window motivation profiles (see I.4.3.3) and uses change-point detection over the profile-weight time series. A shift is declared when a profile weight crosses a per-archetype threshold sustainably across multiple macro-windows, with the change-point's confidence a function of the magnitude and consistency of the shift. Shifts are classified as *maturation* (a healthy progression along a corpus-grounded motivational trajectory, e.g., Mastery → Expression), *satiation* (a motivation satisfied to the point of declining weight, e.g., Achievement declining after a major milestone), *frustration* (a motivation thwarted to the point of declining weight, e.g., Autonomy declining under increasing external structuring), and *drift* (an unexplained shift not matching a corpus trajectory, requiring investigation).

Shifts feed three downstream consumers. The Game Designer Agent uses shifts to recommend content that anticipates the player's evolving motivations; the Monetization Advisor Agent uses maturation shifts to identify monetization opportunities aligned with the new motivation (and MUST NOT use frustration shifts to target vulnerable players — see REQ-BEH-049); the Outcome Layer uses sustained downward shifts in previously-strong motivations as a leading indicator of churn, ahead of the behavioral disengagement that traditional churn models detect.

**REQ-BEH-062 (Shift Classification):** Every detected motivational shift MUST be classified into one of maturation, satiation, frustration, or drift. Unclassified shifts MUST be flagged for human review rather than acted upon.

**REQ-BEH-063 (Frustration-Shift Protection):** Frustration-classified motivational shifts MUST be excluded from monetization-agent context. Using a frustration shift to target monetization is a manipulation-boundary violation per [Volume I, Section I.5.1.3] and MUST be blocked at the Ethics Layer.

**REQ-BEH-064 (Shift as Churn Signal):** Sustained downward motivational shifts MUST be surfaced to the Outcome Layer as leading churn indicators, distinct from behavioral disengagement signals.

---

### I.4.6 Mechanic Layer

#### I.4.6.1 Game Mechanic Taxonomy

The Mechanic Layer represents the product's design surface — the systems, rules, and affordances through which players act and through which motivations are served or thwarted. To reason about mechanics uniformly across products, PsyForge maintains a normative mechanic taxonomy that classifies any product's mechanics into a shared vocabulary, enabling cross-product learning and corpus-grounded mechanic-motivation reasoning.

The taxonomy organizes mechanics into eight categories. **Progression mechanics** govern advancement: leveling, skill trees, unlock ladders, prestige systems, battle passes. **Challenge mechanics** govern difficulty and skill expression: combat encounters, puzzles, time trials, boss fights, difficulty scaling. **Economy mechanics** govern value exchange: crafting, trading, auction houses, resource gathering, sinks and sources (these bridge to the Economy Layer of I.4.7). **Social mechanics** govern inter-player interaction: guilds, groups, leaderboards, chat, emotes, trading-between-players, PvP. **Expression mechanics** govern personalization and identity: housing, cosmetics, character customization, profile decoration, naming. **Exploration mechanics** govern spatial and content discovery: maps, waypoints, fast travel, hidden-content systems, procedural generation. **Narrative mechanics** govern story engagement: quests, cutscenes, dialogue systems, branching narratives, lore discovery. **Meta mechanics** govern the structure of engagement with other mechanics: daily quests, events, seasons, login rewards, retention loops.

Each mechanic in a product is registered with the taxonomy via the product's *mechanic manifest* (see [Volume II, Section II.2.1.1]), declaring its primary category, any secondary categories, its parameters (difficulty curves, reward schedules, costs), and its corpus-grounded served-motivation prior. The manifest is the Mechanic Layer's primary input; without it, the layer cannot perform fit, gap, or conflict analysis (see I.4.5.3).

The taxonomy is extensible: novel mechanic categories (e.g., a rhythm game's beat-matching mechanics, a VR product's spatial-manipulation mechanics) are registered via the corpus contribution process with the same requirements as emotion and motivation additions. Cross-category mechanics (a leaderboard that is both Social and Progression) are represented by primary and secondary category declarations rather than by a separate combined category, to preserve analytic separability.

**REQ-BEH-065 (Mechanic Manifest Required):** The Mechanic Layer MUST NOT operate without a registered mechanic manifest for the product. Absence of a manifest MUST degrade the layer to a no-operation state and MUST surface a configuration error.

**REQ-BEH-066 (Taxonomy Coverage):** Every mechanic in a product's manifest MUST map to at least one taxonomy category. Unmappable mechanics MUST be flagged for manifest revision.

#### I.4.6.2 Mechanic-to-Outcome Mapping

The Mechanic Layer's downstream purpose is to attribute outcomes to mechanics — to answer "which mechanics are responsible for the retention we observe, and which for the churn?" This attribution is the basis of design recommendation: a mechanic causally responsible for retention is a candidate for expansion; a mechanic causally responsible for churn is a candidate for revision.

Mechanic-to-outcome mapping is performed via the cross-layer causal inference model (I.4.1.3) and produces, for each mechanic-outcome pair, an *attributed effect* with a confidence interval and a causal-edge type. The mapping proceeds in three steps. **Engagement attribution** decomposes a player's total engagement into mechanic-level engagement, using the mechanic-engagement metrics of I.4.6.3. **Mediation analysis** tests whether the mechanic's engagement mediates the outcome: does the mechanic affect the outcome through the served-motivation channel (the canonical case), through the economy channel (e.g., a crafting mechanic driving retention through economic accumulation), or through direct affective channels (e.g., a narrative mechanic driving satisfaction directly)? **Interventional validation** upgrades correlational attributions to causal ones through simulation (see [Volume IV, Part IV.6]) and, where ethical, live experimentation.

The mapping must handle *interaction effects*: mechanics do not act in isolation. A social mechanic may drive retention only in the presence of a progression mechanic that gives the social interaction purpose; an exploration mechanic may drive satisfaction only when the economy does not penalize exploration time. Interaction effects are modeled as edges in the Knowledge Graph connecting co-engaged mechanics, and any outcome attribution to a single mechanic MUST be conditioned on its interaction context to avoid the *single-mechanic fallacy* of attributing a joint effect to one contributor.

The mapping must also handle *delayed effects*: a narrative mechanic's effect on retention may manifest sessions later, not within the session of engagement. Delayed attribution requires the long-horizon temporal windows of I.4.3.3 and is inherently lower-confidence; the layer MUST mark delayed attributions as such and MUST widen their confidence intervals to reflect the additional uncertainty.

**REQ-BEH-067 (Interaction Conditioning):** Mechanic-to-outcome attributions MUST be conditioned on interaction context. Single-mechanic attributions ignoring interaction effects MUST be marked `single_mechanic_fallacy_risk` and downgraded in confidence.

**REQ-BEH-068 (Delayed-Attribution Marking):** Attributions whose effect window exceeds the engagement session MUST be marked `delayed` with the estimated lag. Delayed attributions MUST carry widened confidence intervals.

#### I.4.6.3 Mechanic Engagement Metrics

To attribute outcomes, the Mechanic Layer needs precise, comparable measures of how much and how well each mechanic is engaged. This subsection defines the normative engagement metrics, which are the Mechanic Layer's primary descriptive output and the input to the attribution analyses of I.4.6.2.

The metric set comprises: **Engagement Time** — total time spent in mechanic-attributed primitives, excluding idle and AFK periods; **Engagement Breadth** — the count of distinct mechanic features (sub-mechanics, content pieces) the player engaged with, distinguishing depth-in-few from breadth-across-many; **Engagement Frequency** — session-level and macro-window recurrence rate of mechanic engagement; **Engagement Intensity** — a normalized measure of primitive density within engagement time, distinguishing focused from diffuse engagement; **Engagement Mastery** — for skill-expressive mechanics, the player's performance trajectory, normalized against the mechanic's difficulty curve; **Engagement Persistence** — the player's continuation of mechanic engagement across failure or frustration, a key input to the productive-struggle vs. unproductive-frustration distinction of I.4.4.1; and **Engagement Satiation** — the declining marginal engagement return on additional content, used to detect when a mechanic has been exhausted for a player.

Each metric is computed per-mechanic, per-player, per-window, and aggregated to population-level distributions for cross-player reasoning. Metrics are *normalized* against the mechanic's design parameters: Engagement Mastery is reported relative to the mechanic's intended difficulty curve, not as a raw score, so that mastery comparisons are meaningful across mechanics of different absolute difficulty.

Metrics are computed from the behavioral primitives of I.4.2.4, attributed to mechanics via the manifest's primitive-to-mechanic mappings. Attribution ambiguity — primitives that could belong to multiple mechanics — is resolved by a declared priority order in the manifest, and ambiguous attributions MUST be flagged with an `attribution_ambiguous` marker rather than silently assigned.

**REQ-BEH-069 (Metric Normalization):** Mechanic engagement metrics MUST be normalized against the mechanic's design parameters. Raw-score-only metrics MUST NOT be used for cross-mechanic comparison.

**REQ-BEH-070 (Ambiguity Flagging):** Primitives attributable to multiple mechanics MUST be flagged, not silently assigned. The flag MUST propagate to the affected metrics' confidence.

#### I.4.6.4 Mechanic-Emotion Feedback Loops

Mechanics and emotions are locked in a feedback relationship: mechanics evoke emotions, and emotions shape subsequent mechanic engagement. The Mechanic Layer models these loops explicitly because they are the substrate of both healthy engagement (a well-tuned challenge produces productive struggle that resolves to flow, reinforcing engagement) and unhealthy engagement (a variable-reward mechanic produces intermittent excitement that reinforces compulsive engagement). The same feedback architecture serves both, and the distinction between them is ethical, not mechanical — which is why this subsection's requirements are heavily ethics-gated.

The loop is modeled as a coupled system: mechanic parameters (e.g., a combat mechanic's difficulty curve and reward schedule) produce, via the Emotion Layer, an affective response; the affective response, via the Motivation Layer, shifts the player's motivational profile and engagement propensities; the shifted engagement modifies the mechanic's effective parameters (e.g., a frustrated player's reduced engagement lowers the mechanic's effective challenge-skill balance); the modified parameters produce a new affective response. The loop's stability is the analytic question: stable loops converge to sustained engagement (healthy); unstable positive-feedback loops escalate to compulsion or to disengagement (unhealthy).

The loop analysis consumes the mechanic's parameters, the player's emotion trajectory (from I.4.4.4), and the player's motivation profile, and produces a *loop classification*: `reinforcing_healthy` (engagement-affect spiral toward sustained flow), `reinforcing_compulsive` (engagement-affect spiral toward compulsive variable-reward patterns), `balancing` (self-correcting engagement that modulates toward healthy levels), or `destabilizing` (escalating toward disengagement). The `reinforcing_compulsive` classification is the primary trigger for ethics intervention: a mechanic whose loop is classified compulsive MUST be reviewed by the Ethics Officer Agent and MUST NOT be optimized for engagement.

This is the analytic home of the manipulation boundary (see [Volume I, Section I.5.1.3]).
