A mechanic that produces healthy flow through served motivation is legitimate design; a mechanic that produces compulsive engagement through variable-reward exploitation of inferred vulnerabilities is manipulation, regardless of its retention or revenue effects. The distinction is made here, at the loop level, by the conjunction of loop classification and served-motivation status (a compulsive loop without a served motivation is the manipulation signature).

**REQ-BEH-071 (Compulsive-Loop Ethics Gate):** Mechanics classified `reinforcing_compulsive` MUST be routed to the Ethics Officer Agent and MUST be excluded from engagement-optimization recommendations until the loop is reclassified or the mechanic is revised.

**REQ-BEH-072 (Manipulation Signature):** The conjunction of `reinforcing_compulsive` loop classification with absent served-motivation MUST be flagged as a manipulation signature. This is a blocking condition for the affected mechanic's recommendations and MUST trigger an ethics incident per [Volume I, Section I.5.3.5].

---

### I.4.7 Economy Layer

#### I.4.7.1 Virtual Economy Model

The Economy Layer models the value-exchange substrate through which mechanic engagement is denominated and constrained. In a game, this is the virtual economy — currencies, resources, prices, sinks, and sources. In a non-game product, the analogous structure (attention economy, token economy, points-and-rewards system) serves the same function. The layer's premise is that economic behavior is not merely transactional accounting but a behavioral signal of the highest density: how a player earns, spends, saves, and trades reveals motivation, discipline, and vulnerability more directly than almost any other behavior.

The virtual economy model represents the economy as a flow network of currencies and resources. Nodes are *sources* (where currency enters the player's possession: quest rewards, combat drops, daily login bonuses, real-money purchase) and *sinks* (where currency leaves: crafting costs, repair fees, cosmetic purchases, auction-house fees). Edges are flows with direction, magnitude, and velocity. The model is parameterized by the product's economy manifest — analogous to the mechanic manifest of I.4.6.1 — declaring currencies, exchange rates between them, source and sink definitions, and any caps or floors.

The model tracks, per player and aggregate, the *balance trajectory* of each currency, the *flow distribution* (which sources and sinks are active), the *velocity* (how quickly earned currency is spent), and the *hoarding ratio* (the fraction of earned currency retained). These are the inputs to the economic behavior patterns of I.4.7.2.

The model is *causally connected* to the Mechanic Layer: a mechanic's economic parameters (its costs, rewards, sink strength) are edges from the mechanic to the economy, and changes to a mechanic's economic parameters propagate through the economy model to predict balance and flow effects. This causal connection is what makes economy simulation (see [Volume IV, Part IV.3]) a grounded exercise rather than free speculation: the simulation instantiates the economy model with proposed mechanic changes and predicts the resulting economy states.

**REQ-BEH-073 (Economy Manifest Required):** The Economy Layer MUST NOT operate without a registered economy manifest. Absence MUST degrade the layer to a no-operation state.

**REQ-BEH-074 (Causal Mechanic-Economy Edges):** Edges from mechanics to economy flows MUST be typed and versioned in the Knowledge Graph. Unversioned or untyped economic-effect edges MUST NOT be used for economy simulation.

#### I.4.7.2 Economic Behavior Patterns

Economic behavior — how a player navigates the economy's sources and sinks — is itself a behavioral layer that classifies into recognizable patterns with motivational and outcome implications. The Economy Layer defines a normative pattern set: *Disciplined Saving* (high hoarding ratio, low velocity, deliberate sink selection); *Impulsive Spending* (low hoarding ratio, high velocity, low-deliberation sinks — see also the homonymous behavior pattern in I.4.3.1, here refined with economic specificity); *Strategic Investment* (currency deployed toward mechanics yielding long-term returns — crafting infrastructure, trade goods); *Status Spending* (disproportionate spend on visible/expression-class sinks — cosmetics, housing — the economic signature of the Expression motivation); *Frustration Spending* (spend velocity spikes following repeated failure, often on pay-to-progress mechanics — a vulnerability signal requiring ethical scrutiny); *Disengagement Spending* (terminal-session spending wind-down, predictive of churn); *Whale Behavior* (sustained high-magnitude real-money spend — requires the protections of I.4.7.4 and [Volume V, Section V.10.4]).

Economic behavior patterns are detected from the flow and balance data of I.4.7.1 using the same hybrid detection architecture as the Behavior Layer (rule-based, sequence-based, learned, reconciled — see I.4.3.2). Patterns are probabilistic and multi-label; a player exhibits an economic behavior *profile*, not a single economic type.

The most ethically consequential pattern is *Frustration Spending*: the conjunction of repeated failure, affective frustration inference (from I.4.4.2), and subsequent spend on pay-to-progress mechanics is the behavioral signature of vulnerability exploitation. The Economy Layer MUST surface this conjunction to the Ethics Officer Agent and MUST NOT permit monetization recommendations that would amplify it. This is the operational enforcement of the manipulation boundary at the economic level, complementing the mechanic-level enforcement of REQ-BEH-072.

**REQ-BEH-075 (Frustration-Spending Detection):** The Economy Layer MUST detect the frustration-spending conjunction (failure → frustration → pay-to-progress spend) and MUST route detections to the Ethics Officer Agent. Detections MUST be excluded from monetization-agent optimization context.

**REQ-BEH-076 (Whale Protections):** Players classified as whales MUST be subject to enhanced ethics review of any monetization recommendation affecting them. The Monetization Advisor Agent MUST NOT optimize whale spend without explicit Ethics Officer sign-off per [Volume V, Section V.11.5].

#### I.4.7.3 Economy-to-Outcome Linkage

The Economy Layer's downstream purpose is to link economic states and behaviors to outcomes — revenue (the direct linkage), retention (the indirect linkage via mechanic affordability and progression pacing), and satisfaction (the linkage via perceived fairness and value). This linkage is the analytic basis of monetization and economy-design recommendation.

The direct revenue linkage is, in one sense, trivially observable: revenue is a transaction event. But the *causal* linkage — which economic design choices produce sustainable revenue versus extractive revenue — is the substantive question. The layer applies the cross-layer causal inference model (I.4.1.3) to attribute revenue outcomes to economy design parameters: a sink's strength, a source's generosity, a currency's exchange rate. The distinction between *sustainable* revenue (revenue accompanied by maintained or improved satisfaction and retention) and *extractive* revenue (revenue accompanied by declining satisfaction and retention — the signature of monetization-driven churn) is the central ethical and business distinction, and it is made here by jointly analyzing revenue, satisfaction, and retention outcomes.

The retention linkage runs through mechanic affordability: an economy whose sinks are too demanding relative to its sources makes progression mechanics unaffordable, producing progression frustration and churn; an economy whose sources are too generous makes progression trivial, producing boredom churn. The Economy Layer computes an *affordability index* per mechanic — the ratio of typical player earning rate to the mechanic's cost — and flags mechanics whose affordability is out of the corpus-grounded healthy range (see [Volume III, Section III.2.6.5]).

The satisfaction linkage runs through perceived fairness: economies that are perceived as fair (transparent rules, consistent value, no pay-to-progress dominance) sustain satisfaction; economies perceived as unfair (opaque odds, arbitrary price changes, pay-to-progress dominance) erode satisfaction and trust. Perceived-fairness signals are inferred from behavioral proxies (spend-withdrawal rates, complaint-correlated spending declines) and from explicit survey data where available.

**REQ-BEH-077 (Sustainable vs. Extractive Classification):** Revenue outcomes MUST be classified as sustainable or extractive based on the joint trajectory of revenue, satisfaction, and retention. Extractive revenue patterns MUST be flagged and MUST NOT be optimized by the Monetization Advisor Agent.

**REQ-BEH-078 (Affordability Index):** The Economy Layer MUST compute and publish an affordability index per mechanic. Mechanics outside the corpus-grounded healthy range MUST be flagged for economy-design review.

#### I.4.7.4 Economic Health Indicators

Beyond per-player economic behavior, the Economy Layer monitors the health of the economy as a whole — the macroeconomic state whose stability determines whether the product's economic design is sustainable at population scale. These indicators are the inputs to the Economy Designer Agent (see [Volume V, Section V.7]) and to the economy-simulation forecasts of [Volume IV, Section IV.5.1.6].

The normative indicator set includes: **Currency Velocity** — the rate at which currency circulates, a primary inflation/deflation indicator (high velocity suggests inflationary pressure; collapsing velocity suggests hoarding and deflation); **Wealth Distribution** — the Gini coefficient of currency holdings across the player population, with high inequality flagging both fairness concerns and engagement-risk concentration (a population whose wealth is concentrated in a few whales is structurally fragile); **Price Stability** — the variance of key prices over time, with high variance flagging market instability that undermines perceived fairness; **Economic Participation Rate** — the fraction of the active player population engaging with the economy's non-trivial mechanics (crafting, trading), with declining participation flagging economy disengagement ahead of general churn; **Sink/Source Balance** — the ratio of total sink activity to total source activity, the primary long-term sustainability indicator (persistent imbalance produces either inflation or deflation); and **Real-Money-to-Virtual Flow Ratio** — the fraction of currency originating from real-money purchase, a key indicator of monetization pressure and of pay-to-progress risk.

Each indicator is computed continuously and published to the dashboard (see [Volume VII, Section VII.2.2.3]). Indicators crossing corpus-grounded thresholds trigger alerts to the Economy Designer Agent. Critically, the *interaction* between indicators carries more information than any single one: high wealth inequality combined with high real-money-to-virtual ratio is the signature of a pay-to-win economy; collapsing velocity combined with rising Gini is the signature of late-game hoarding by veterans and new-player exclusion.

Economic health indicators are also the primary calibration target for economy simulation (see [Volume IV, Part IV.3]): a simulated economy whose indicators diverge from observed indicators is miscalibrated, and the divergence MUST trigger simulation-fidelity investigation before simulated predictions are trusted.

**REQ-BEH-079 (Continuous Indicator Publication):** Economic health indicators MUST be computed continuously and published to the dashboard with their thresholds. Stale indicators MUST trigger a data-pipeline alert.

**REQ-BEH-080 (Simulation Calibration):** Economy simulations MUST be calibrated against observed health indicators. Simulations whose indicators diverge from observed indicators beyond tolerance MUST be flagged `miscalibrated` and MUST NOT be trusted for deployment recommendation.

---

### I.4.8 Outcome Layer

#### I.4.8.1 Outcome Taxonomy (Retention, Revenue, Satisfaction, Churn, etc.)

The Outcome Layer is the terminus of the Behavioral Stack and its justification: every prior layer exists to predict, explain, and ultimately improve outcomes. The outcome taxonomy defines the categories of result PsyForge reasons about, and the taxonomy's structure reflects a deliberate refusal to reduce all outcomes to a single metric — a refusal that is itself an ethical and methodological commitment.

The taxonomy comprises four primary outcome families. **Engagement outcomes** include retention (D1, D7, D30, and configurable long-horizon retention), session-frequency, session-duration, and engagement-breadth. These are the proximate measures of whether the product is holding the player's attention. **Commercial outcomes** include revenue (gross, per-player, per-cohort), ARPU and ARPPU, lifetime value (LTV), and conversion rate (free-to-paying). These are the proximate measures of commercial sustainability. **Experiential outcomes** include satisfaction (inferred from behavior and, where available, from surveys), perceived-fairness, and trust (a long-horizon outcome whose erosion is the leading indicator of multi-product churn for studios with portfolios). **Population outcomes** include churn (the negative of retention, decomposed into churn-cause categories), community health (see [Volume IV, Section IV.4]), and economy stability (the outcome-facing view of the indicators in I.4.7.4).

The taxonomy's ethical commitment is that no single outcome may be optimized in isolation. Retention without satisfaction is compulsion; revenue without retention is extraction; satisfaction without commercial sustainability is a product that cannot survive. Every outcome the layer predicts MUST be accompanied by the *co-active outcomes* it implies or threatens, and the Ethics Layer's seven scoring dimensions (see [Volume I, Section I.5.2.1]) operationalize this co-active discipline. A recommendation that improves retention by 4% while decreasing satisfaction by 2% is not, on its face, a good recommendation — it is a trade-off that must be evaluated as such.

The taxonomy is extensible for domain-specific outcomes (an education product's *learning outcome*, a health product's *behavior-change outcome*) via the corpus-swapping mechanism (see [Volume III, Section III.2.13]), with each addition requiring a definition, a measurement methodology, and a declaration of co-active outcomes.

**REQ-BEH-081 (Co-Active Outcome Reporting):** Any outcome prediction MUST be accompanied by its co-active outcomes. Single-outcome reporting is forbidden because it falsifies the trade-off structure that ethical evaluation requires.

**REQ-BEH-082 (No Single-Metric Optimization):** No recommendation MAY optimize a single outcome in isolation. The Ethics Layer MUST reject recommendations that lack co-active outcome analysis.

#### I.4.8.2 Outcome Prediction Models

Outcome prediction is the forward-looking function of the Outcome Layer: given the current state of the stack and a hypothetical intervention (a mechanic change, an economy rebalance, a content addition), predict the resulting outcome distribution. This is the function that makes the simulation engine (see [Volume IV]) valuable: predictions enable pre-deployment evaluation that flat analytics cannot provide.

Prediction models are stratified by horizon. **Short-horizon models** (next session, next 24 hours) predict session-level engagement and near-term commercial outcomes from current behavioral and emotional state; these models are high-frequency, run in near-real-time, and feed operational alerts (e.g., a churn-risk alert for a player showing disengagement-spending and sustained negative affect). **Medium-horizon models** (next 7–30 days) predict retention and revenue trajectories from motivational profile and recent behavioral trends; these models feed design and monetization recommendation. **Long-horizon models** (next 90+ days) predict satisfaction, trust, and LTV; these models are the highest-uncertainty and MUST carry the widest confidence intervals, calibrated per I.4.1.5's predictive-validity criterion.

Prediction models are *interventional*, not merely conditional: they predict outcomes *under a hypothetical change*, which requires the causal structure of the stack (I.4.1.3) rather than mere conditional-on-observation forecasting. A purely conditional model — "players who engage with housing retain at 80%" — cannot answer "if we add more housing, will retention rise?" because the engagement is not randomly assigned. The interventional model uses do-calculus to propagate the intervention through the stack and produces a counterfactual prediction with an explicit confidence interval.

All predictions are *distributional* (per REQ-BEH-081's co-active discipline and per the simulation output format of [Volume IV, Section IV.5.2]): a prediction is a joint distribution over co-active outcomes, not a set of independent point estimates, because the co-active outcomes are correlated and the correlation is decision-relevant (a mechanic change that independently raises retention 4% and revenue 3% is very different from one that raises retention 4% and revenue 3% but does so by trading satisfaction for engagement).

**REQ-BEH-083 (Interventional Prediction):** Outcome predictions MUST be interventional (do-calculus-based), not merely conditional. Conditional-only predictions MUST be marked `conditional_only` and MUST NOT be used as the basis for deployment recommendations without an explicit causal-modeling step.

**REQ-BEH-084 (Joint Distribution Output):** Outcome predictions MUST be joint distributions over co-active outcomes, not independent point estimates. Independent-point predictions MUST be rejected as falsifying the co-active structure.

**REQ-BEH-085 (Horizon-Calibrated Confidence):** Long-horizon predictions MUST carry confidence intervals calibrated against their historical accuracy per the predictive-validity criterion (I.4.1.5). Overconfident long-horizon predictions are a validity failure.

#### I.4.8.3 Outcome Attribution to Prior Layers

Prediction is forward-looking; attribution is backward-looking: given an observed outcome, which prior-layer states and changes are causally responsible? Attribution is the function that closes the behavioral-reasoning loop, turning observed outcomes into validated causal knowledge that improves future predictions and recommendations.

Attribution traverses the stack from Outcome backward, decomposing an observed outcome change into contributions from each layer. A retention increase is decomposed into contributions from the Mechanic Layer (which mechanics changed and how much each contributed), the Economy Layer (which economic changes contributed), the Motivation Layer (which motivational shifts contributed), the Emotion Layer (which affective trajectory shifts contributed), and the Behavior Layer (which behavioral pattern shifts contributed). The decomposition uses the causal edges in the Knowledge Graph and the mediation-analysis methods of [Volume III, Section III.7.2.6].

Attribution must handle *confounding and selection*: an observed retention increase after a mechanic change may be confounded by a concurrent content update, a seasonal effect, or a population-composition shift. The attribution MUST condition on registered confounders (per REQ-BEH-007) and MUST report the residual unattributed variance honestly — an outcome change that the stack cannot attribute is information (it signals an unmodeled cause) rather than a failure to be papered over.

Attribution must handle *distributed and delayed effects*: a mechanic change's effect on satisfaction may be mediated through economy changes that take days to manifest, and the attribution MUST trace the delayed mediation rather than attributing the satisfaction change to the most recent layer event. This is the backward-looking analog of the forward-looking delayed-attribution marking of REQ-BEH-068.

Attribution feeds the model-improvement loop: attributions that consistently fail to explain observed outcomes indicate Knowledge Graph gaps (missing edges, missing confounders, missing mechanics), which MUST be surfaced to the corpus and graph-maintenance processes (see [Volume III, Sections III.1.5 and III.2.1.4]).

**REQ-BEH-086 (Honest Residual Reporting):** Attribution MUST report unattributed variance. Attributions that claim 100% of an outcome change without residual MUST be flagged as over-attribution and downgraded.

**REQ-BEH-087 (Attribution-Driven Gap Surfacing):** Persistent attribution failures MUST be surfaced as Knowledge Graph gap reports. The system MUST NOT silently absorb unattributed outcomes into existing edges.

#### I.4.8.4 Outcome Validation Methodology

Outcome predictions and attributions are only as trustworthy as their validation. This subsection defines the methodology by which the Outcome Layer's claims are validated, closing the loop between prediction and observation that distinguishes a scientific model from a story.

Validation proceeds at three levels. **Internal validation** uses held-out historical data: predictions made on a training period are evaluated against realized outcomes in a held-out period, with calibration assessed per the predictive-validity criterion (I.4.1.5). Internal validation is continuous and automated; a model whose calibration degrades triggers retraining (see [Volume III, Section III.5.5.5]). **Simulation validation** uses the simulation-as-experiment pipeline (see [Volume IV, Section IV.6.4]): a prediction is tested in simulation before deployment, and the simulated outcome is compared to the predicted outcome, calibrating the simulation's fidelity. **Experimental validation** uses live A/B testing (see [Volume IV, Part IV.6]): a recommendation is deployed to a treatment group and withheld from a control, and the realized outcome difference is compared to the prediction. Experimental validation is the gold standard for causal claims and is the mechanism by which `correlational_only` edges are upgraded to `causal` (per REQ-BEH-005).

Validation is *ethically constrained*: not every prediction can or should be experimentally validated. Experiments that would expose players to harm (e.g., testing whether a frustration-spending mechanic increases revenue) are forbidden by the Ethics Layer and MUST be rejected at the experiment-design stage (see [Volume V, Section V.12] and [Volume IV, Section IV.9.3]). Where experimental validation is unethical, the layer MUST rely on simulation validation and natural-experiment analysis, and MUST widen confidence intervals to reflect the reduced validation strength.

Validation results are recorded as *evaluation cards* (see [Volume III, Section III.5.5.4]) linked to the prediction model and the recommendation that triggered the validation. The cards are the audit trail by which a future reviewer can reconstruct the evidence supporting (or undermining) a deployed recommendation. A recommendation deployed without a current, linked evaluation card MUST be flagged as `unvalidated_deployment` — a compliance condition that the deployment gate (see [Volume II, Section II.2.6]) MUST treat as requiring human override.

**REQ-BEH-088 (Three-Level Validation):** Every production prediction model MUST have current internal-validation results. Recommendation-triggered predictions MUST additionally have simulation-validation results before deployment. Causal claims MUST have experimental validation or documented ethical grounds for its absence.

**REQ-BEH-089 (Ethical Experiment Constraint):** Experiments exposing players to harm MUST be rejected at design. Validation relying on such experiments is forbidden; the layer MUST fall back to simulation and natural-experiment validation with widened confidence.

**REQ-BEH-090 (Evaluation Card Linkage):** Deployed recommendations MUST have a current, linked evaluation card. Recommendations lacking such linkage MUST be flagged `unvalidated_deployment` and MUST require human override at the deployment gate per [Volume II, Section II.2.6.3].

## Part I.5 — Ethics Framework

> **Status:** Normative. The requirements in this Part are binding on every PsyForge deployment, every corpus domain, and every recommendation that flows through the platform. This Part is written to be submittable to a regulatory body as evidence of responsible AI design.
>
> **Governing Law:** [WPAI Constitution](https://github.com/MrWizard94-Compile/Agents-Constitution), frozen per AGENTS.md. See [Volume I, Front Matter, FM.0.6] for conformance level definitions (MUST, SHOULD, MAY).
>
> **Cross-References:** [Volume II, Part II.2.6] (Recommendation Gate Architecture), [Volume IV, Part IV.9] (Simulation Ethics), [Volume V, Part V.11] (Ethics Officer Agent), [Volume IX, Part IX.5] (Compliance Framework), [Volume X, Section X.6.3] (Ethics Benchmarks), [Appendix B.9] (Ethics Score Card Schema), [Appendix E.2] (Ethics Decision Record Template).

---

### I.5.1 Ethical Foundations

PsyForge is a behavioral intelligence platform: it observes human behavior, infers emotional and motivational states, predicts outcomes, and recommends changes to the systems that shape that behavior. This capacity is ethically load-bearing in a way that ordinary analytics is not. A tool that merely reports "retention fell 3%" is descriptive; a tool that recommends "introduce a variable-reward schedule to recover the 3%" is prescriptive and operates directly on human psychology. The Ethics Framework exists because the difference between a behavioral intelligence platform that improves player welfare and one that degrades it is not a matter of intent — it is a matter of architecture. Intent erodes under commercial pressure; architecture does not. This Part therefore specifies ethics as a structural property of the system, not a review performed after the fact.

The foundational claim of this Part is the principle stated in [Volume I, Section I.2.1.8]: **Ethics by Architecture, not bolt-on**. A bolt-on ethics layer is one that scores recommendations after they are generated and can be overridden, deprioritized, or routed around when it becomes inconvenient. An architectural ethics layer is one that the system cannot produce a deployable recommendation without passing through, that holds veto authority that no other component can revoke, and whose absence is a hard failure rather than a warning. PsyForge implements the latter. The Ethics Agent specified in [Volume V, Part V.11] is the runtime embodiment of this Part; the scoring system in Section I.5.2 is its evaluation instrument; the governance in Section I.5.3 is its human accountability structure; the architecture in Section I.5.4 is its enforcement mechanism; and the legal mapping in Section I.5.5 is its external contract.

#### I.5.1.1 Ethics Charter

The PsyForge Ethics Charter is the single normative statement from which every other ethical obligation in the platform derives. It is short by design, because a charter that cannot be remembered cannot be enforced, and a charter that requires interpretation in the moment of pressure has already failed. The Charter is reproduced below in its binding form and MUST appear verbatim in every deployment's configuration, in the Ethics Agent's system prompt (see [Volume V, Section V.11.2.1]), and in the onboarding documentation supplied to every studio that licenses PsyForge.

> **PsyForge Ethics Charter**
>
> PsyForge exists to help studios build experiences that players choose to return to because those experiences serve the player's own goals — mastery, community, identity, story — not because those experiences exploit the player's psychological vulnerabilities. PsyForge will not optimize for engagement that comes at the expense of the player's welfare. PsyForge will not help any studio target a vulnerable person with a mechanism designed to convert that vulnerability into revenue. PsyForge treats every behavioral signal as something the player did not consent to be used against them. Where the platform's commercial interest and the player's welfare conflict, the player's welfare prevails. This is not a preference. It is a constraint encoded in the architecture.

The Charter is not aspirational language. Each clause maps to one or more enforceable requirements in this Part: the refusal to optimize exploitative engagement is operationalized in Section I.5.1.3 and scored by the Manipulation Risk dimension (I.5.2.1.e); the protection of vulnerable persons is operationalized in Section I.5.1.4; the consent obligation is operationalized in Section I.5.1.5 and in [Volume VI, Part VI.3.2]; the precedence of player welfare over commercial interest is operationalized by the Ethics Agent's veto authority in [Volume V, Section V.11.5] and the gating rules in Section I.5.2.3. A requirement that cannot be traced to the Charter is not a valid ethics requirement; a Charter clause that cannot be traced to an enforceable mechanism is a defect in this specification and MUST be reported under the principle violation process in [Volume I, Section I.2.2.4].

The Charter is owned by the Ethics Review Board (Section I.5.3.1) and may only be amended through the charter amendment process defined in [Volume I, Section I.2.4.2] with mandatory external review. No studio, no deployment, and no corpus domain may weaken or localize the Charter. A deployment that cannot accept the Charter MUST NOT be provisioned; this is enforced by the provisioning gate described in [Volume II, Section II.2.6.5]. The Charter's precedence over commercial terms is stated in the license agreement (see [Volume VIII, Section VIII.2.3.2]): a studio that violates the Charter risks suspension of license, and this consequence is implemented through the incident response process in Section I.5.3.5, not through discretionary legal action.

- **REQ-ETH-001 (MUST):** Every PsyForge deployment MUST embed the Ethics Charter verbatim in its configuration, in the Ethics Agent system prompt, and in studio onboarding materials.
- **REQ-ETH-002 (MUST):** No component, agent, or human role MUST be permitted to weaken, localize, or selectively apply the Charter.
- **REQ-ETH-003 (MUST):** Every normative ethics requirement in this Part MUST be traceable to a Charter clause; every Charter clause MUST be traceable to at least one enforceable mechanism.

#### I.5.1.2 Ethical Principles Derived from Core Philosophy

The eight core principles stated in [Volume I, Part I.2.1] are the platform's engineering philosophy; this section derives the ethical principles that the platform is obligated to uphold from that same philosophy. The derivation is explicit because ethical principles that are merely asserted can be re-interpreted under pressure, while ethical principles that are shown to follow from commitments the platform has already made are harder to abandon without abandoning the platform's identity.

The first derived principle is **Beneficence of Recommendation**: because PsyForge holds "Prediction over Reaction" ([Volume I, Section I.2.1.4]) and "Simulation before Deployment" ([Volume I, Section I.2.1.3]), it has the means to foresee the welfare consequences of a recommendation before those consequences reach a player. A platform with foresight has a welfare obligation that a purely reactive platform does not: it MUST NOT recommend a change it has simulated to harm a player segment, even if that change is commercially attractive. The failure mode this principle prevents is the "we didn't know" defense — PsyForge does know, or is required to know before it recommends, and the simulation ethics checks in [Volume IV, Section IV.9.3] enforce this.

The second derived principle is **Non-Maleficence toward Psychological Autonomy**: because PsyForge holds "Psychographics over Demographics" ([Volume I, Section I.2.1.2]) and builds causal models of motivation (see [Volume III, Part III.2.4]), it has the capacity to model what a player wants and therefore the capacity to act against what a player wants. The principle states that PsyForge MUST NOT use its motivational models to produce behavior in a player that the player's own stated or inferred goals would not endorse. This is the principle the Manipulation Boundary (Section I.5.1.3) operationalizes, and it is the principle that distinguishes persuasion — appealing to a player's existing goals — from manipulation — acting on a player's psychological levers to produce behavior that serves the operator, not the player.

The third derived principle is **Justice across Populations**: because PsyForge holds "Truth over Assumptions" ([Volume I, Section I.2.1.1]) and models behavior at the individual and segment level, it can detect when a recommendation benefits one segment at the disproportionate expense of another. The principle states that PsyForge MUST surface and, where the Fairness dimension (I.5.2.1.d) requires, refuse recommendations that produce disparate impact — even when aggregate metrics improve. The failure mode this prevents is the "on average it's fine" pattern in which a majority's gains hide a minority's losses.

The fourth derived principle is **Autonomy of the Human Reviewer**: because PsyForge holds "Human-in-the-Loop" ([Volume I, Section I.2.1.6]) as a structural principle, the human reviewer is not a courtesy — they are the final ethical authority for any recommendation that reaches deployment. The Ethics Agent holds veto authority (see [Volume V, Section V.11.5]), but the Ethics Agent does not approve; it gates. Approval is reserved for the human reviewer, and the system MUST NOT present a recommendation as approved on the basis of an ethics score alone. The failure mode this prevents is the automation bias pattern in which a green score is treated as a green light.

The fifth derived principle is **Transparency of Method**: because PsyForge holds "Evidence over Intuition" ([Volume I, Section I.2.1.7]), every ethics decision the platform makes — every score, every veto, every override — MUST be accompanied by a human-readable explanation citing the evidence and the rule applied. An ethics decision that cannot be explained cannot be audited, and an ethics system that cannot be audited cannot be trusted. This principle is operationalized by the audit trail in Section I.5.2.5 and the reporting in Section I.5.3.6.

- **REQ-ETH-004 (MUST):** PsyForge MUST NOT recommend a change it has simulated to produce net welfare harm to a player segment, regardless of aggregate commercial benefit.
- **REQ-ETH-005 (MUST):** PsyForge MUST NOT use motivational or emotional models to produce behavior in a player that the player's own stated or inferred goals would not endorse.
- **REQ-ETH-006 (MUST):** PsyForge MUST surface disparate impact across segments and MUST refuse recommendations that violate the Fairness gating threshold (I.5.2.3).
- **REQ-ETH-007 (MUST):** No recommendation MUST be presented as approved on the basis of an ethics score alone; human approval is REQUIRED for deployment.
- **REQ-ETH-008 (MUST):** Every ethics decision MUST be accompanied by a human-readable explanation citing evidence and the rule applied, persisted to the audit trail.

#### I.5.1.3 The Manipulation Boundary: What PsyForge Refuses to Optimize

The Manipulation Boundary is the single most important construct in this Part. It defines the class of optimizations PsyForge refuses to perform, regardless of commercial value, regardless of studio request, and regardless of whether the resulting behavior is legal in the operator's jurisdiction. The Boundary exists because the platform's core capacity — modeling human motivation and predicting how mechanics move it — is exactly the capacity that, pointed in the wrong direction, produces the most defensible-sounding forms of exploitation. A platform that can predict that a depressed player will respond to an "escape" mechanic with increased spending can either use that prediction to protect the player or to monetize the player. PsyForge is specified to do the former and is architecturally prevented from doing the latter.

The Boundary is defined by a test, not a list. A list of forbidden tactics becomes obsolete the moment a studio invents a new one; a test does not. The test is the **Autonomy Reversal Test**: an optimization is on the wrong side of the Boundary if, were the player fully informed of the mechanism and its purpose, the player would reasonably object to being subjected to it. An optimization that survives the player's informed, reflective endorsement is persuasion; one that requires the player's ignorance or compromised state to work is manipulation. The Ethics Agent applies this test in natural-language reasoning as part of the Manipulation Risk score (I.5.2.1.e), and the human reviewer applies it as the final check in the review process (Section I.5.3.2).

Concretely, PsyForge MUST NOT optimize for, recommend, or simulate as a positive outcome any of the following, which are named here as canonical violations of the Boundary so that the test has examples to calibrate against:

1. **Whale spending addiction.** PsyForge MUST NOT optimize monetization toward the small fraction of players whose spending is disproportionate and shows markers of compulsive behavior (see the spending-pattern markers in [Volume III, Section III.5.3.3]). The Monetization Advisor Agent ([Volume V, Part V.10]) is specified to flag, not to exploit, such players. The failure mode this prevents is the "whale harvesting" pattern documented in predatory monetization literature, in which a studio's revenue becomes dependent on the pathological spending of a few users.
2. **Targeting distressed users with escape mechanics.** PsyForge MUST NOT use inferred emotional distress (see the emotion inference in [Volume I, Section I.4.4.2]) as a targeting signal for mechanics whose engagement value depends on the player's desire to escape a negative state. A player who is inferred to be depressed or anxious MUST NOT be served a recommendation whose predicted effect depends on that depression or anxiety.
3. **Loss-aversion exploitation in spending.** PsyForge MUST NOT recommend the use of loss aversion (see [Volume III, Section III.2.3.1]) to convert a player's fear of losing progress into spending, where the loss is engineered by the system rather than organic to the game. The distinction is critical: a player losing a boss fight is organic; a player losing a daily streak because the system raised the streak threshold is engineered.
4. **Variable-reward schedules tuned to compulsion.** PsyForge MAY recommend variable-reward mechanics (see [Volume III, Section III.2.3.8]) for engagement, but MUST NOT tune those schedules to maximize the compulsive-repetition marker rather than the enjoyment marker. The Behavioral Feature Engine's "Habitual vs. Exploratory Ratio" ([Volume III, Section III.3.2.17]) is the signal the Ethics Agent uses to detect when a recommendation is pushing players past enjoyment into compulsion.
5. **Social-pressure monetization.** PsyForge MUST NOT recommend monetization mechanics whose predicted effect depends on social comparison or fear of exclusion (see [Volume III, Part III.2.7]), where the social pressure is manufactured by the system.

The Boundary is enforced at three layers: the Ethics Agent refuses to assign a passing Manipulation Risk score to a recommendation that fails the Autonomy Reversal Test (Section I.5.2.3); the simulation engine flags outcomes that show the markers above as ethics violations ([Volume IV, Section IV.9.3.3]); and the human reviewer may not deploy a recommendation the Ethics Agent has vetoed without going through the override process in Section I.5.2.4, which requires documented justification and Board notification. A studio that requests an optimization that crosses the Boundary is not served the optimization; the request itself is logged to the ethics incident queue (Section I.5.3.5) so that patterns of attempted manipulation can be detected across studios.

- **REQ-ETH-009 (MUST):** PsyForge MUST NOT optimize for, recommend, or score as positive any outcome that fails the Autonomy Reversal Test.
- **REQ-ETH-010 (MUST):** The Ethics Agent MUST refuse a passing Manipulation Risk score to any recommendation that targets a player's psychological vulnerability as the mechanism of effect.
- **REQ-ETH-011 (MUST):** Any studio request for an optimization that crosses the Manipulation Boundary MUST be logged to the ethics incident queue, regardless of whether the request is granted (it MUST NOT be).
- **REQ-ETH-012 (SHOULD):** The Ethics Agent SHOULD apply the named canonical violations above as calibration examples when reasoning about novel tactics.

#### I.5.1.4 Vulnerable Population Protections (Minors, Addictive Tendencies, etc.)

A vulnerable population, for the purposes of this Part, is any group of players for whom the ordinary assumption that "the player can protect their own interests" does not hold, or holds only weakly. PsyForge does not treat vulnerability as a demographic category; it treats it as a state that the platform can and must detect, because a system that models behavior but claims it cannot detect vulnerability is a system that has chosen not to detect it. The Behavioral Feature Engine ([Volume III, Part III.3]) produces the signals; this section specifies what the platform does with them.

The first protected class is **minors**. Players identified or reasonably inferred to be under the age of digital majority in their jurisdiction (see the age verification approach in [Volume VI, Section VI.3.5.1]) are subject to the restrictions in [Volume VI, Part VI.3.5]: no behavioral modeling for monetization purposes, no targeted recommendations that use the minor's inferred motivations, and no inclusion in simulations whose purpose is to optimize monetization mechanics. The Ethics Agent MUST refuse a passing score to any recommendation that would, if deployed, target a minor with a monetization mechanic. The failure mode this prevents is the well-documented pattern of minors being exposed to gambling-like mechanics; PsyForge's position is that if it cannot verify a player is not a minor, it must treat the player as a minor for the purposes of monetization modeling. This is a conservative default and is intentional.

The second protected class is **players showing markers of addictive tendencies**. The Behavioral Feature Engine produces a "Habitual vs. Exploratory Ratio" ([Volume III, Section III.3.2.17]) and a "Session Fatigue" signal ([Volume III, Section III.3.2.9]); the Player Model produces spending-prediction confidence intervals ([Volume III, Section III.5.3.3]). When these signals cross thresholds defined in the Ethics Agent's configuration (calibrated against the corpus domain's addiction-research literature; see [Volume III, Section III.2.2.5]), the player is flagged as a member of this protected class for the duration of the flag. For flagged players, PsyForge MUST NOT recommend mechanics whose predicted effect depends on the player's compulsive engagement, MUST NOT include the player in monetization-optimization simulations as a representative of a desirable outcome, and SHOULD recommend protective interventions (spending caps, session reminders, friction additions) to the studio. The flag is not shown to the player by default — surfacing it is a studio decision governed by the consent framework in Section I.5.1.5 — but the flag does affect what PsyForge will and will not recommend.

The third protected class is **players showing signs of acute distress**. The emotion inference layer ([Volume I, Section I.4.4.2]) can infer states such as frustration, anxiety, and what the corpus terms "negative-valence high-arousal" states. When a player's inferred emotional state crosses a distress threshold, the Ethics Agent MUST NOT recommend any monetization mechanic whose predicted effect depends on that distress, and SHOULD recommend a reduction in pressure mechanics for that player segment. This is the operationalization of the second canonical violation in Section I.5.1.3: a distressed player is not a monetization opportunity.

The fourth protected class is **players in a position of asymmetric disadvantage**, which includes new players still in onboarding (see [Volume III, Section III.2.6.4]) and players whose behavioral model confidence is low (see [Volume III, Section III.5.1.4]), because the system's predictions about them are least reliable and the players are least equipped to recognize engineered pressure. For these players, the Ethics Agent applies a tighter Manipulation Risk threshold (Section I.5.2.3) and SHOULD recommend conservative mechanics over aggressive ones.

Vulnerability flags are themselves sensitive data. They are subject to the privacy controls in [Volume VI, Part VI.3], are never used as targeting signals for the very exploitation they exist to prevent, and are auditable under the trail in Section I.5.2.5. A vulnerability flag that is found to have been used as a targeting signal is a Severity-1 ethics incident (Section I.5.3.5).

- **REQ-ETH-013 (MUST):** Players identified or inferred to be minors MUST NOT be targeted by monetization recommendations or included in monetization-optimization simulations.
- **REQ-ETH-014 (MUST):** Where the platform cannot verify a player is not a minor, the player MUST be treated as a minor for the purposes of monetization modeling.
- **REQ-ETH-015 (MUST):** Players flagged for addictive tendencies MUST NOT be the target of recommendations whose predicted effect depends on compulsive engagement.
- **REQ-ETH-016 (MUST):** Players showing acute distress MUST NOT be the target of monetization recommendations whose predicted effect depends on that distress.
- **REQ-ETH-017 (MUST):** Vulnerability flags MUST be treated as sensitive data and MUST NOT be used as targeting signals for exploitation.
- **REQ-ETH-018 (SHOULD):** For players in asymmetric-disadvantage positions, the Ethics Agent SHOULD apply a tighter Manipulation Risk threshold and SHOULD favor conservative mechanics.

#### I.5.1.5 Informed Consent for Behavioral Modeling

PsyForge builds models of human behavior from observed interaction data. In most jurisdictions, this processing requires a lawful basis and, for sensitive inferences, explicit consent (see the regulatory mapping in Section I.5.5.1 and the consent management architecture in [Volume VI, Part VI.3.2]). This section specifies the ethical standard for consent, which exceeds the legal minimum in several respects, because the ethical problem with behavioral modeling is not only that the player was not asked — it is that the player was not asked in a way that let them understand what they were being asked for.

The first requirement is **specificity of consent**. A blanket consent to "data collection" or "analytics" is not consent to behavioral modeling, and it is not consent to the inference of emotional or motivational states. PsyForge MUST distinguish, in its consent capture (see [Volume VI, Section VI.3.2.5]), at minimum the following granular scopes: (a) collection of interaction telemetry, (b) inference of behavioral patterns, (c) inference of emotional states, (d) inference of motivational states, (e) use of inferences in recommendations that affect the player's own experience, and (f) use of inferences in aggregate models that do not affect the player individually. A player MAY consent to (a) and (b) without consenting to (c) through (f), and the system MUST enforce that distinction in the data flow (see [Volume VI, Section VI.3.2.3]). The failure mode this prevents is consent bundling, in which a player who wants their telemetry collected to fix bugs is silently consenting to have their emotions modeled.

The second requirement is **comprehension of consent**. Consent is not informed if the player does not understand what they have consented to. PsyForge's consent surfaces MUST use plain language, MUST avoid the euphemisms that the industry has used to obscure behavioral modeling ("personalization," "improved experience"), and MUST state, in a sentence a non-technical reader can parse, what the platform infers and to what end. The Ethics Agent SHOULD review consent language as part of corpus-domain onboarding and MAY refuse to provision a deployment whose consent language fails a comprehension check. This is a strong position; it is the position the Charter requires.

The third requirement is **reversibility of consent**. Consent that cannot be withdrawn is not consent. PsyForge MUST support withdrawal of any consent scope at any time, MUST propagate that withdrawal through the data pipeline (see [Volume VI, Section VI.3.3.7] for deletion propagation across stores, models, embeddings, and graph), and MUST NOT degrade the player's core experience as a consequence of withdrawal — a player who withdraws consent for emotional inference must still be able to play the game. The failure mode this prevents is the consent trap, in which withdrawal is technically possible but practically punishing.

The fourth requirement is **consent for minors and protected classes**. For minors, the consent framework in [Volume VI, Section VI.3.5.2] applies: enhanced consent, parental consent where required by jurisdiction, and the right to withdraw at the minor's or guardian's request. For players who, at the time of consent, are in a state of acute distress or other compromised decision-making capacity, the platform SHOULD defer non-essential consent requests until the state passes, on the principle that consent given under duress is not the consent the Charter requires.

The fifth requirement is **no consent for manipulation**. Consent does not authorize manipulation. A player who consents to behavioral modeling has not consented to be manipulated, because the Manipulation Boundary (Section I.5.1.3) is a constraint on the platform, not a right the player can waive. This is the ethical floor below which no contract, consent, or studio request can take the platform. It is the reason the Boundary is enforced by architecture and not by policy: consent cannot authorize what the architecture will not perform.

- **REQ-ETH-019 (MUST):** Consent MUST be captured at granular scope and the data flow MUST enforce the distinction between scopes.
- **REQ-ETH-020 (MUST):** Consent language MUST use plain language, avoid euphemism, and state what is inferred and to what end; the Ethics Agent MAY refuse to provision a deployment whose consent language fails comprehension review.
- **REQ-ETH-021 (MUST):** Consent withdrawal MUST be supported at any time, propagated through the pipeline, and MUST NOT degrade the player's core experience as a consequence.
- **REQ-ETH-022 (MUST):** Consent MUST NOT authorize any optimization that crosses the Manipulation Boundary; the Boundary is a constraint on the platform, not a waivable right.
