### I.5.2 Ethics Scoring System

The Ethics Scoring System is the instrument by which the ethical principles in Section I.5.1 become machine-evaluable. Every recommendation that the platform generates — whether from an agent, a simulation result, or a pattern-mining trigger — MUST pass through this system and MUST receive a score on each of the seven dimensions defined in Section I.5.2.1 before it can be presented to a human reviewer. The score is not advisory. The gating rules in Section I.5.2.3 determine, from the scores, whether the recommendation may proceed, must be modified, must be escalated, or is vetoed. The Ethics Agent specified in [Volume V, Part V.11] is the component that computes the score; this section specifies what it computes and how.

The seven dimensions are deliberately heterogeneous. Some measure outcomes the platform wants to produce (Retention Impact, Player Satisfaction, Long-Term Trust); some measure outcomes the platform must be careful about (Revenue Impact, Fairness); and some measure outcomes the platform must refuse (Manipulation Risk, Regulatory Risk). A scoring system that measured only the first kind would be a marketing instrument; a scoring system that measured only the last kind would be a brake with no engine. The seven together form a profile, not a single number, and the gating rules operate on the profile, not on an aggregate — because a recommendation that is excellent on six dimensions and catastrophic on the seventh is not a good recommendation with one flaw; it is a recommendation that must not ship, and an aggregate score would hide that.

#### I.5.2.1 Score Dimensions

The seven score dimensions are specified individually below. Each is scored on a common scale defined in Section I.5.2.2, but each measures a distinct construct and has distinct thresholds and warning conditions. The dimensions are not independent: a recommendation that scores poorly on Manipulation Risk will typically also score poorly on Long-Term Trust, and the scoring methodology in Section I.5.2.2 accounts for this correlation in its weighting rather than treating it as redundancy.

##### I.5.2.1.a Retention Impact

The Retention Impact dimension measures the predicted effect of a recommendation on the duration and frequency of player engagement — specifically, whether the recommendation increases retention by mechanisms the player would endorse or by mechanisms that amount to compulsion. This dimension exists because retention is the headline metric the platform is asked to optimize, and a retention gain produced by a healthy mechanism (a player returns because the game is more rewarding) is ethically and commercially different from a retention gain produced by an unhealthy one (a player returns because a daily-streak mechanic has made absence feel costly). The dimension is calculated from the simulation engine's retention forecast (see [Volume IV, Section IV.5.1.1]) decomposed by mechanism: the Ethics Agent receives the aggregate retention delta and the mechanism-attributed retention delta, and scores the dimension higher when the gain is attributable to enjoyment-related features and lower when it is attributable to compulsion-related features. A high score indicates retention driven by endorsed mechanisms; a low score indicates retention driven by mechanisms the player would not endorse if they understood them. The dimension triggers a warning when the aggregate retention gain is positive but the mechanism decomposition shows that more than half of the gain is attributable to compulsion markers (the Habitual vs. Exploratory Ratio and Session Fatigue signals in [Volume III, Section III.3.2]) — the warning indicates that the headline number is hiding an unhealthy composition. The dimension also triggers a warning when retention gains are concentrated in a vulnerable population segment, because retention gained by retaining a distressed player through escape mechanics is a Manipulation Risk problem even if the aggregate looks healthy.

##### I.5.2.1.b Revenue Impact

The Revenue Impact dimension measures the predicted effect of a recommendation on revenue, with explicit attention to the source of that revenue and the population it is drawn from. This dimension exists because revenue is the metric most likely to be optimized without regard to its origin, and because the platform's commercial incentive is structurally biased toward revenue in a way that the ethics system must counterbalance. The dimension is calculated from the revenue forecast (see [Volume IV, Section IV.5.1.2]) decomposed by player segment and by spending-tier (the whale/dolphin/minnow segmentation in [Volume V, Section V.10.1.4]). The Ethics Agent scores the dimension higher when revenue gains are broadly distributed across spending tiers and lower when gains are concentrated in the top spending tier, because revenue concentrated in a small high-spending segment is a marker of the whale-harvesting pattern the Manipulation Boundary forbids. A high score indicates broadly distributed, sustainable revenue; a low score indicates revenue concentrated in a few high-spending players or revenue that depends on players crossing into compulsive spending. The dimension triggers a warning when the revenue gain is predicted to come disproportionately (above a configurable threshold, default 40%) from the top 5% of spenders, and triggers a veto-level score when any portion of the predicted revenue is attributable to players flagged for addictive tendencies (Section I.5.1.4) — because revenue extracted from a protected class is revenue the platform has agreed not to extract. The dimension also accounts for the long-term revenue trajectory: a recommendation that increases short-term revenue at the cost of long-term trust scores lower, because the Long-Term Trust dimension (I.5.2.1.f) captures the trust cost and the Revenue Impact dimension must not double-count it but must reflect the net present value rather than the spike.

##### I.5.2.1.c Player Satisfaction

The Player Satisfaction dimension measures the predicted effect of a recommendation on the player's own reported and inferred satisfaction with the experience — the degree to which the recommendation makes the game more of what the player wants the game to be. This dimension exists because satisfaction is the welfare metric that anchors the platform to the player's interests rather than the operator's, and because satisfaction is the dimension most likely to be sacrificed when retention and revenue are optimized in isolation. The dimension is calculated from the satisfaction forecast (see [Volume IV, Section IV.5.1.5]) and from the simulation's predicted effect on the satisfaction-related behavioral features (Flow Stability, Frustration Tolerance, Mastery Progression Rate in [Volume III, Part III.3.2]). The Ethics Agent scores the dimension higher when satisfaction is predicted to increase across the player population and lower when satisfaction is predicted to decrease, with particular attention to whether satisfaction gains are uniform or concentrated. A high score indicates broad satisfaction gains; a low score indicates satisfaction losses, especially losses concentrated in a segment that is also losing on retention or revenue. The dimension triggers a warning when aggregate satisfaction is flat or positive but a segment shows a satisfaction decline that exceeds the disparate-impact threshold (Section I.5.2.3), because a recommendation that improves the majority's satisfaction at a segment's expense is a Fairness problem even when the aggregate looks fine. The dimension also triggers a warning when satisfaction gains are predicted but the mechanism is a reduction in challenge or depth that the satisfaction forecast cannot distinguish from genuine enjoyment — in such cases the Ethics Agent SHOULD request a longer-horizon simulation to determine whether the satisfaction gain is durable or transient.

##### I.5.2.1.d Fairness

The Fairness dimension measures the distributional equity of a recommendation's predicted effects across player segments, with particular attention to whether a recommendation that improves aggregate metrics does so by imposing disproportionate costs on a minority segment. This dimension exists because aggregate metrics are the language in which harmful trade-offs are hidden, and because a platform that models behavior at the segment level has the capacity — and therefore the obligation — to detect disparate impact that aggregate reporting conceals. The dimension is calculated from the simulation's disproportionate-impact analysis (see [Volume IV, Section IV.5.2.4]), which produces per-segment outcome deltas for retention, revenue, satisfaction, and the behavioral features. The Ethics Agent computes a fairness penalty as a function of the worst-segment outcome delta relative to the aggregate, weighted by the size of the harmed segment and the severity of the harm. A high score indicates that the recommendation's benefits are broadly shared or that any segment-level costs are small and outweighed by the gains; a low score indicates that one or more segments are harmed while the aggregate improves — the canonical "sacrifice the minority" pattern. The dimension triggers a warning whenever any segment shows a negative outcome delta on any of retention, revenue, or satisfaction that exceeds the disparate-impact threshold while the aggregate is positive, and triggers a veto-level score when the harmed segment is a vulnerable population (Section I.5.1.4), because a recommendation that improves the aggregate by harming a protected class is the most direct violation of the Justice principle in Section I.5.1.2. The dimension is also sensitive to the definition of "segment": the Ethics Agent MUST evaluate fairness across multiple segmentations (behavioral archetype, spending tier, tenure, inferred vulnerability) because a recommendation that is fair along one axis can be unfair along another, and the platform's obligation is to detect the unfairness, not to choose the axis that hides it.

##### I.5.2.1.e Manipulation Risk

The Manipulation Risk dimension measures the degree to which a recommendation's predicted effect depends on acting against the player's psychological autonomy rather than appealing to the player's existing goals — that is, the degree to which the recommendation crosses the Manipulation Boundary defined in Section I.5.1.3. This dimension is the load-bearing dimension of the scoring system: a recommendation that scores poorly here cannot ship regardless of its scores on the other six, because the Manipulation Boundary is a constraint, not a trade-off. The dimension is calculated by the Ethics Agent's application of the Autonomy Reversal Test (Section I.5.1.3) to the recommendation and its predicted mechanism of effect, supported by the canonical-violation examples as calibration and by the corpus domain's manipulation-research literature (see [Volume III, Section III.2.2.6] on microtransaction psychology and [Volume III, Section III.2.2.7] on loot box and reward schedule research). The Ethics Agent reasons in natural language about whether the recommendation's effect would survive the player's informed, reflective endorsement and produces a score reflecting that reasoning. A high score (low risk) indicates that the recommendation operates on the player's existing goals; a low score (high risk) indicates that the recommendation depends on the player's ignorance, distress, or compromised state to work. The dimension triggers a warning whenever the recommendation uses a mechanism named in the canonical violations (Section I.5.1.3) or any mechanism the Ethics Agent identifies as analogous, and triggers a veto-level score whenever the recommendation targets a vulnerable population with a mechanism that depends on the vulnerability — for example, targeting a distressed player with an escape mechanic, or tuning a variable-reward schedule to a compulsive-repetition marker. Because this dimension depends on natural-language reasoning rather than a numeric forecast, the Ethics Agent MUST produce a human-readable explanation of its score (per REQ-ETH-008), and the human reviewer MUST be able to inspect and challenge that reasoning in the review process (Section I.5.3.2).

##### I.5.2.1.f Long-Term Trust

The Long-Term Trust dimension measures the predicted effect of a recommendation on the player's trust in the experience and the operator over a horizon longer than the immediate retention and revenue windows — the degree to which the recommendation treats the player as a long-term relationship the operator wants to preserve rather than a short-term source of metrics the operator wants to extract. This dimension exists because every manipulative tactic produces short-term gains and long-term trust costs, and because a scoring system that measured only short-term outcomes would systematically favor the tactics the Manipulation Boundary forbids. The dimension is calculated from the long-horizon simulation (see [Volume IV, Section IV.5.1]) run at an extended time horizon, from the predicted effect on trust-correlated behavioral features (Session Regularity, Engagement Breadth, and the long-term orientation feature in [Volume III, Section III.3.2]), and from the corpus domain's research on trust erosion (see [Volume III, Section III.2.9.5] on brand attachment and loyalty). The Ethics Agent scores the dimension higher when the long-horizon forecast shows durable engagement and lower when the long-horizon forecast shows a spike-and-decay pattern characteristic of manipulative tactics. A high score indicates that the recommendation builds durable trust; a low score indicates that the recommendation trades trust for short-term metrics. The dimension triggers a warning whenever the short-term retention or revenue forecast is positive but the long-horizon forecast is negative — the spike-and-decay signature — because that signature is the empirical fingerprint of a manipulative tactic even when the tactic itself is not named in the canonical violations. The dimension also triggers a warning when the recommendation is predicted to reduce the player's willingness to recommend the experience to others (a trust proxy), because a player who would not recommend the experience is a player whose trust has already eroded, and a recommendation that erodes that willingness is a recommendation that the operator will pay for later.

##### I.5.2.1.g Regulatory Risk

The Regulatory Risk dimension measures the degree to which a recommendation, if deployed, would place the operator at risk of violating applicable law or regulation in the jurisdictions where the recommendation would take effect. This dimension exists because the platform operates across jurisdictions with divergent and rapidly evolving regulation of behavioral modeling, monetization mechanics, and AI systems, and because an operator that deploys a recommendation the platform has scored as low regulatory risk has a defensible position that the platform warned it — while an operator that deploys a recommendation the platform scored as high regulatory risk has chosen to accept that risk knowingly. The dimension is calculated by mapping the recommendation's mechanisms and target populations to the regulatory compliance database (see [Volume V, Section V.11.3.4] and the compliance matrix in [Volume IX, Section IX.5.6]) and evaluating the recommendation against the applicable regulations identified for the operator's jurisdictions (see the jurisdictional mapping in Section I.5.5.2). The Ethics Agent scores the dimension higher when the recommendation is clearly compliant across all applicable jurisdictions and lower when the recommendation touches a regulated mechanism (loot boxes, targeted advertising to minors, automated decision-making about individuals) in a jurisdiction that restricts it. A high score indicates clear compliance; a low score indicates a likely or possible violation. The dimension triggers a warning whenever the recommendation touches a regulated mechanism in any applicable jurisdiction, regardless of whether a violation is predicted, because the regulatory landscape changes faster than the corpus updates and a warning ensures the human reviewer checks the current state of the law. The dimension triggers a veto-level score when the recommendation is predicted to violate applicable law in any jurisdiction where it would take effect — because the platform's position, stated in Section I.5.5.3, is that it will not recommend what the law forbids, even where the law is weaker than the platform's own ethical floor.

#### I.5.2.2 Scoring Methodology and Weighting

Each of the seven dimensions is scored on a common ordinal scale of 0 to 5, where 0 indicates a veto-level failure on the dimension, 1 indicates a serious problem requiring escalation, 2 indicates a warning condition, 3 indicates an acceptable outcome with no concerns, 4 indicates a positive outcome, and 5 indicates a strongly positive outcome. The scale is ordinal, not cardinal: a score of 4 is better than a score of 3, but it is not "33% better," and the scores MUST NOT be aggregated by arithmetic mean. The Ethics Agent produces each dimension score together with a confidence estimate (reflecting the uncertainty in the underlying simulation and inference) and a human-readable rationale (per REQ-ETH-008). The full score card schema is specified in [Appendix B.9].

The methodology by which the Ethics Agent arrives at a dimension score differs by dimension type. For the outcome-forecast dimensions (Retention Impact, Revenue Impact, Player Satisfaction, Long-Term Trust), the score is computed from the simulation engine's forecast decomposed by mechanism and segment, as described in each dimension's specification above. For the Fairness dimension, the score is computed from the disproportionate-impact analysis with a penalty function over the worst-segment harm. For the Manipulation Risk dimension, the score is computed by the Ethics Agent's natural-language application of the Autonomy Reversal Test, calibrated against the canonical violations. For the Regulatory Risk dimension, the score is computed by mapping the recommendation to the compliance database. The methodology for each dimension is documented in the Ethics Agent's configuration and is itself subject to the audit trail in Section I.5.2.5 — a change in methodology is a change in how ethics is measured and MUST be reviewable.

Weighting is applied at the gating stage, not at the scoring stage. The seven dimensions are not weighted into a single number; instead, the gating rules in Section I.5.2.3 apply dimension-specific thresholds, with the Manipulation Risk and Regulatory Risk dimensions treated as veto dimensions (a score of 0 on either blocks the recommendation regardless of the other six) and the Fairness dimension treated as a strong gate (a score of 0 or 1 blocks the recommendation). The remaining dimensions are treated as trade-off dimensions whose scores inform the human reviewer but do not, by themselves, block. This weighting reflects the principle that some ethical failures are trade-offs the reviewer may accept with justification (a small satisfaction loss for a large retention gain) and some are not (a manipulation risk that the reviewer may not accept at all). The weighting is specified in the Ethics Agent's configuration, is subject to Board review (Section I.5.3.1), and MAY be tightened by a studio but MUST NOT be loosened below the platform defaults — because a studio that wants to loosen the ethical floor is a studio the platform has agreed not to enable.

The scoring methodology is validated against human ethics review on a continuous basis (see the calibration requirement in [Volume V, Section V.11.4.4] and the ethics benchmarks in [Volume X, Section X.6.3]). The Ethics Agent's scores are compared against the Ethics Review Board's scores on a sample of recommendations, and systematic disagreement (the false-negative and false-positive rates in [Volume V, Section V.11.4.2] and [Volume V, Section V.11.4.3]) triggers a methodology review. This calibration is not a one-time validation; it is a continuous process, because the ethical landscape, the corpus, and the tactics in the field all evolve, and a scoring methodology that was calibrated once and never again is a methodology that has gone stale.

- **REQ-ETH-023 (MUST):** Each dimension MUST be scored on the 0–5 ordinal scale with a confidence estimate and a human-readable rationale.
- **REQ-ETH-024 (MUST):** Dimension scores MUST NOT be aggregated by arithmetic mean; gating MUST operate on the profile.
- **REQ-ETH-025 (MUST):** Manipulation Risk and Regulatory Risk MUST be treated as veto dimensions; Fairness MUST be treated as a strong gate.
- **REQ-ETH-026 (MUST):** Studios MAY tighten weighting thresholds but MUST NOT loosen them below platform defaults.
- **REQ-ETH-027 (MUST):** Scoring methodology MUST be validated continuously against human ethics review, and systematic disagreement MUST trigger a methodology review.

#### I.5.2.3 Score Thresholds and Gating Rules

The gating rules translate the seven-dimension score profile into a disposition for the recommendation: **Pass**, **Pass with Warning**, **Modify**, **Escalate**, or **Veto**. The disposition determines what happens to the recommendation next in the recommendation lifecycle (see [Volume II, Section II.2.6.2] for the human review state machine). The rules are specified as a decision table evaluated by the Ethics Agent and persisted to the audit trail for every recommendation.

The thresholds are as follows. A score of **0** on Manipulation Risk or Regulatory Risk produces a **Veto** disposition regardless of all other scores; the recommendation MUST NOT be presented to a human reviewer as deployable and MUST be routed to the incident queue if the veto is sustained (Section I.5.3.5). A score of **0** on Fairness produces a **Veto** disposition; a score of **1** on Fairness produces an **Escalate** disposition requiring Board-aware review (Section I.5.3.2). A score of **0 or 1** on Manipulation Risk or Regulatory Risk (when not already a Veto) produces an **Escalate** disposition. A score of **2** on any dimension produces a **Pass with Warning** disposition; the warning is attached to the recommendation and MUST be visible to the human reviewer, and the reviewer MUST acknowledge the warning before approving. A score of **0 or 1** on Retention Impact, Revenue Impact, Player Satisfaction, or Long-Term Trust produces a **Modify** disposition; the recommendation is returned to the originating agent with the score and rationale, and the agent MAY revise and resubmit. A profile in which all dimensions are **3 or higher** produces a **Pass** disposition; the recommendation proceeds to human review with no warning. A profile in which any dimension is **4 or 5** and no dimension is below **3** produces a **Pass** disposition with a positive note.

The gating rules are conjunctive at the veto level and additive at the warning level. That is, a single veto-dimension score of 0 blocks the recommendation regardless of the other six; a warning on any dimension attaches to the recommendation regardless of the other six. There is no "offsetting" of a manipulation risk by a high retention score, because the ethical failure the manipulation risk represents is not a trade-off the platform is willing to make. This is the operational meaning of "Ethics by Architecture": the architecture does not permit the trade-off that a weighted aggregate would permit.

The thresholds are defaults and MAY be tightened by a studio (a studio may, for example, set the Fairness veto threshold to a score of 1 rather than 0, refusing recommendations the platform would allow). The thresholds MUST NOT be loosened below the defaults; this is enforced by the configuration validation in the provisioning gate ([Volume II, Section II.2.6.5]) and by the audit trail, which records the threshold configuration in effect at the time of every score. A studio that attempts to set a threshold below the platform default MUST be refused by the system, and the attempt MUST be logged.

The thresholds are also sensitive to the target population of the recommendation. For recommendations that would affect a vulnerable population (Section I.5.1.4), the Ethics Agent MUST apply the tightened thresholds specified in the vulnerability configuration: by default, the Manipulation Risk veto threshold for vulnerable populations is a score of 1 rather than 0, and the Fairness veto threshold is a score of 1 rather than 0. This reflects the principle that the platform's ethical obligations to a vulnerable population are stricter than its obligations to the general population, and that a recommendation that is acceptable for the general population may be unacceptable for a protected class.

- **REQ-ETH-028 (MUST):** A score of 0 on Manipulation Risk or Regulatory Risk MUST produce a Veto disposition regardless of other scores.
- **REQ-ETH-029 (MUST):** A score of 0 on Fairness MUST produce a Veto disposition; a score of 1 on Fairness MUST produce an Escalate disposition.
- **REQ-ETH-030 (MUST):** A score of 2 on any dimension MUST produce a Pass with Warning disposition and the warning MUST be acknowledged by the human reviewer before approval.
- **REQ-ETH-031 (MUST):** Thresholds MAY be tightened by a studio but MUST NOT be loosened below platform defaults; the system MUST refuse and log any attempt to loosen.
- **REQ-ETH-032 (MUST):** For recommendations affecting vulnerable populations, the tightened thresholds in the vulnerability configuration MUST be applied.

#### I.5.2.4 Score Override and Escalation Process

The Ethics Agent's veto is not the final word on a recommendation; the final word belongs to a human, specifically to the Ethics Review Board. But the override process is deliberately hard, because a veto that is easy to override is not a veto — it is a suggestion. This section specifies the process by which a vetoed or escalated recommendation may nevertheless proceed, and the conditions under which it may not.

A **Veto** disposition may be overridden only by the Ethics Review Board (Section I.5.3.1), not by the originating studio, not by the originating agent, and not by any individual reviewer acting alone. The override process requires: (a) a written justification from the studio explaining why the recommendation should proceed despite the veto, addressing the specific dimension and rationale the Ethics Agent provided; (b) a Board review at the next scheduled session or an emergency session if the recommendation is time-sensitive; (c) a Board decision recorded as an Ethics Decision Record (see [Appendix E.2]) with the vote tally and dissenting positions; and (d) if the override is granted, a deployment with enhanced monitoring (Section I.5.4.5) and a mandatory post-deployment review at a fixed interval (default 30 days) to verify that the harm the Ethics Agent predicted did not occur. An override that is granted and followed by the predicted harm is a defect in the override decision, not a vindication, and MUST be reviewed as an incident (Section I.5.3.5).

An **Escalate** disposition is less severe than a Veto but still requires Board-aware review. The recommendation MAY proceed with the approval of a senior reviewer (a role defined in the permission matrix in [Volume VII, Section VII.1.2.8]) who has been briefed on the escalation reason, and the escalation MUST be reported to the Board in the next reporting cycle (Section I.5.3.6). Escalation does not require a Board vote, but the Board reviews escalations in aggregate to detect patterns — a studio that escalates frequently is a studio whose recommendations the Board should examine more closely.

A **Modify** disposition returns the recommendation to the originating agent with the score and rationale. The agent MAY revise the recommendation to address the failing dimension and resubmit; the resubmission is scored afresh. There is no limit on the number of modify cycles, but each cycle is logged, and a recommendation that cycles through modify more than a configurable number of times (default 3) without passing is flagged to the Board as a possible indication that the recommendation's goal is incompatible with the ethical constraints and should be reconsidered at the goal level rather than the tactic level.

The override and escalation processes are themselves audited. Every override request, every Board decision, every escalation, and every modify cycle is written to the audit trail (Section I.5.2.5) with the identities of the humans involved, the rationales provided, and the outcomes. The audit trail is reviewable by the Board, by the studio's own compliance function, and, where applicable, by regulators (Section I.5.5.1). An override process that cannot be audited is not an override process the platform will operate, because unaudited exceptions are how ethical architectures erode.

There is one class of recommendation that cannot be overridden at all: a veto on the Manipulation Risk dimension where the recommendation targets a vulnerable population (Section I.5.1.4) with a mechanism that depends on the vulnerability. This is the absolute floor of the Charter: the platform will not, under any override, recommend that a vulnerable person be subjected to a mechanism that exploits their vulnerability. This floor is enforced in the Ethics Agent's configuration as a non-overridable rule, and any attempt to configure it as overridable MUST be refused by the system and logged as an incident.

- **REQ-ETH-033 (MUST):** A Veto disposition MAY be overridden only by the Ethics Review Board, with written justification, a recorded decision, enhanced monitoring, and a mandatory post-deployment review.
- **REQ-ETH-034 (MUST):** An Escalate disposition MAY proceed with senior-reviewer approval and MUST be reported to the Board in the next reporting cycle.
- **REQ-ETH-035 (MUST):** A Modify disposition returns the recommendation to the originating agent; resubmission is scored afresh; excessive modify cycles MUST be flagged to the Board.
- **REQ-ETH-036 (MUST):** The override and escalation processes MUST be fully audited with identities, rationales, and outcomes.
- **REQ-ETH-037 (MUST):** A Manipulation Risk veto targeting a vulnerable population with a vulnerability-dependent mechanism MUST NOT be overridden by any process; this rule MUST be non-configurable.

#### I.5.2.5 Score Audit Trail

Every ethics score, every gating decision, every override, and every escalation is written to an append-only, tamper-evident audit trail. The audit trail is the mechanism by which the ethics system becomes accountable: a decision that cannot be reconstructed after the fact is a decision that cannot be reviewed, and a decision that cannot be reviewed is a decision that the Board, the studio, and the regulator cannot trust. The audit trail is specified here as an ethics requirement; its technical implementation is specified in [Volume IX, Part IX.4] (Audit and Logging), and the audit event types relevant to ethics are enumerated in [Volume IX, Section IX.4.2.7].

For every recommendation scored, the audit trail MUST record: the recommendation identifier; the originating agent and the originating request; the seven dimension scores with their confidence estimates and rationales; the gating disposition; the threshold configuration in effect at the time of scoring; the Ethics Agent configuration version; the corpus domain and version used; the simulation run identifiers on which the scores were based; the identity of the human reviewer who acted on the recommendation; the reviewer's decision and any modifications; and, if the recommendation was deployed, the deployment identifier and the post-deployment monitoring results. The record is written at the moment of scoring and is immutable thereafter; corrections are made by appending a new record that supersedes the prior one, never by editing the prior record in place.

The audit trail is queryable for several purposes. The Board uses it for the reporting in Section I.5.3.6 and for incident investigation (Section I.5.3.5). The studio's compliance function uses it for the regulatory reporting in Section I.5.5.1. The Ethics Agent's evaluation pipeline uses it for the calibration in [Volume V, Section V.11.4.4] — comparing the Agent's scores against the Board's scores over time to detect drift. The benchmark suite in [Volume X, Section X.6.3] uses it to measure the false-negative and false-positive rates that determine whether the scoring methodology is still adequate. And the regulator, where the operator is subject to a regulation that grants audit access (see the EU AI Act post-market monitoring requirements in [Volume IX, Section IX.5.4.5]), uses it to verify that the operator's deployments conform to the ethics system the operator claims to operate.

The audit trail is itself subject to the data retention and deletion rules in [Volume VI, Section VI.3.4] and [Volume IX, Section IX.4.1.3], with the important exception that ethics audit records pertaining to a deployed recommendation MUST be retained for a minimum period (default 7 years) even if the underlying player data is deleted, because the ethics record is about the operator's decision, not about the player's data, and the operator's accountability for that decision does not expire when the player's data does. This exception is stated in the retention policy and is enforced by the data lifecycle management in [Volume II, Section II.3.1.3].

The integrity of the audit trail is itself an ethics requirement. The trail MUST be tamper-evident (hash-chained, as specified in [Volume IX, Section IX.4.1.4]), MUST be replicated to independent storage, and MUST be readable by the Board and by authorized auditors without the ability to alter it. A breach of the audit trail's integrity is a Severity-1 ethics incident (Section I.5.3.5), because an ethics system whose decisions cannot be verified after the fact is an ethics system that can be silently subverted.

- **REQ-ETH-038 (MUST):** Every ethics score, gating decision, override, and escalation MUST be written to an append-only, tamper-evident audit trail with the full record specified above.
- **REQ-ETH-039 (MUST):** Audit records MUST be immutable; corrections MUST be made by superseding records, never by in-place edits.
- **REQ-ETH-040 (MUST):** Ethics audit records pertaining to deployed recommendations MUST be retained for a minimum period (default 7 years) even after underlying player data is deleted.
- **REQ-ETH-041 (MUST):** The audit trail MUST be tamper-evident, replicated to independent storage, and readable by the Board and authorized auditors without the ability to alter it.
- **REQ-ETH-042 (MUST):** A breach of audit trail integrity MUST be treated as a Severity-1 ethics incident.

---

### I.5.3 Ethics Governance

The scoring system in Section I.5.2 is a machine; every machine needs a human authority that owns it, calibrates it, and is accountable for the decisions it produces. That authority is the Ethics Governance structure specified in this section. Governance is not an afterthought to the scoring system; it is the mechanism by which the scoring system remains legitimate over time, because an ethics system that is not accountable to a human body is an ethics system that has no one to correct it when it drifts, and no one to answer for it when it fails.

The governance structure has three concerns: who decides (the Board, Section I.5.3.1), how decisions are made for each class of change the platform can produce (Sections I.5.3.2 through I.5.3.4), how the system responds when something goes wrong (Section I.5.3.5), and how the system accounts for itself to the people it affects (Section I.5.3.6). The structure is designed to be lightweight enough to operate at the cadence the platform requires (recommendations are scored continuously, not monthly) and heavyweight enough to hold real authority (a Board decision is binding, not advisory).

#### I.5.3.1 Ethics Review Board Structure

The Ethics Review Board ("the Board") is the human authority accountable for the ethical operation of PsyForge within a deployment. Each deployment — or, for multi-tenant deployments, each tenant — MUST have a configured Board, and the Board's composition and charter MUST be recorded in the deployment configuration and auditable under Section I.5.2.5. A deployment without a configured Board MUST NOT be provisioned; this is enforced by the provisioning gate in [Volume II, Section II.2.6.5].

The Board MUST consist of at least five members and SHOULD consist of seven to nine, to allow for meaningful vote tallies without unwieldy scheduling. The composition MUST include: (a) at least one member with expertise in the corpus domain's relevant behavioral science (for the game psychology domain, expertise in game addiction and engagement research; see [Volume III, Section III.2.2.5]); (b) at least one member with expertise in the applicable regulatory landscape (see Section I.5.5.1); (c) at least one member who is a representative of the player population the deployment serves, with no commercial interest in the operator's revenue — this is the "player advocate" role and is non-negotiable, because a Board that includes no one whose standing is with the player is a Board that will, under pressure, drift toward the operator; (d) at least one member with technical expertise in the platform's architecture, sufficient to understand the scoring system and the audit trail; and (e) at least one member who is independent of the operator entirely — an external ethicist, regulator, or academic with no employment or contractual tie to the studio. The remaining members MAY be drawn from the operator's design, product, and leadership, but MUST NOT constitute a majority, so that the Board's decisions cannot be captured by the operator's commercial interest.

The Board elects a Chair from among its independent members (categories (c) or (e)) to set the agenda and to be the public face of the Board's decisions. The Board meets on a regular cadence (default monthly) and on an emergency cadence when an incident (Section I.5.3.5) or an override request (Section I.5.2.4) requires it. Decisions are made by majority vote of the members present, with a quorum of at least 60% of the Board, and with the player advocate's presence required for any decision that touches a vulnerable population. Dissenting positions MUST be recorded in the decision record (see [Appendix E.2]).

The Board's authority is defined by this Part: it owns the Ethics Charter (Section I.5.1.1), it owns the scoring methodology and thresholds (Section I.5.2.2 and Section I.5.2.3), it is the override authority for vetoed recommendations (Section I.5.2.4), it reviews corpus updates (Section I.5.3.3) and new agent introductions (Section I.5.3.4), it owns the incident response process (Section I.5.3.5), and it produces the transparency reports (Section I.5.3.6). The Board does not own the commercial decisions of the operator, and the operator does not own the ethical decisions of the Board; this separation is the structural point of the Board's existence.

- **REQ-ETH-043 (MUST):** Each deployment or tenant MUST have a configured Ethics Review Board; a deployment without one MUST NOT be provisioned.
- **REQ-ETH-044 (MUST):** The Board MUST include a player advocate with no commercial interest in the operator's revenue and at least one fully independent external member; operator-affiliated members MUST NOT constitute a majority.
- **REQ-ETH-045 (MUST):** The player advocate MUST be present for any Board decision touching a vulnerable population.
- **REQ-ETH-046 (MUST):** Dissenting positions MUST be recorded in the Ethics Decision Record.

#### I.5.3.2 Ethics Review Process for Recommendations

The recommendation-level review process is the most frequent ethics decision the platform makes, and it is the process that most directly affects what reaches players. The process is specified here as the human side of the gating system in Section I.5.2.3: the Ethics Agent gates, the human reviewer approves, and the Board oversees. The process is designed so that the human reviewer has both the information and the authority to make a real decision, not a rubber stamp.

When a recommendation reaches the human review queue (see the state machine in [Volume II, Section II.2.6.2]), the reviewer sees: the recommendation and its originating agent; the seven-dimension score card with rationales; the simulation results on which the scores were based; the gating disposition; any warnings; and, for escalated or vetoed recommendations, the escalation or veto reason. The reviewer's interface (see [Volume VII, Section VII.4.2]) MUST present this information in a form that supports the Autonomy Reversal Test (Section I.5.1.3) — that is, the reviewer must be able to ask, from the information presented, "would the affected players endorse this if they understood it?" and must be able to answer that question before acting.

The reviewer's decisions are: **Approve**, **Modify**, **Reject**, or **Escalate to Board**. Approve moves the recommendation to the deployment-ready state; the reviewer's approval is recorded with identity and timestamp, and the recommendation cannot deploy without it (per REQ-ETH-007). Modify returns the recommendation to the originating agent with the reviewer's requested changes, which are scored afresh on resubmission. Reject closes the recommendation with a recorded reason. Escalate to Board routes the recommendation to the Board for a decision under Section I.5.2.4; the reviewer SHOULD escalate when the recommendation's ethics profile is borderline and the reviewer is not prepared to own the decision alone.

The reviewer is not a formality. The platform's evaluation framework (see [Volume X, Section X.4.5.1]) measures reviewer behavior: the rate at which reviewers approve, modify, reject, and escalate; the rate at which reviewer approvals are followed by the harm the Ethics Agent predicted (the false-approval rate); and the rate at which reviewer rejections are followed by no harm (the false-rejection rate). A reviewer whose approval rate is uniformly high is a reviewer who is not exercising judgment, and the Board SHOULD examine that reviewer's decisions. A reviewer whose decisions systematically diverge from the Ethics Agent's scores in a direction that favors the operator is a reviewer whose independence is in question, and the Board MUST examine that pattern. Reviewer behavior is itself auditable under Section I.5.2.5.

The review process has a time bound. A recommendation that sits in the review queue beyond a configurable interval (default 14 days) is flagged to the Board, because a recommendation that is neither approved nor rejected is a decision that is being deferred, and deferral is itself a decision the Board should be aware of. The time bound does not force a decision; it surfaces the deferral.

- **REQ-ETH-047 (MUST):** The reviewer interface MUST present the score card, rationales, simulation results, and disposition in a form that supports the Autonomy Reversal Test.
- **REQ-ETH-048 (MUST):** A recommendation MUST NOT deploy without human approval recorded with identity and timestamp.
- **REQ-ETH-049 (MUST):** Reviewer behavior MUST be measured for approval-rate bias and for false-approval and false-rejection rates; systematic divergence favoring the operator MUST be examined by the Board.
- **REQ-ETH-050 (SHOULD):** Recommendations sitting in the review queue beyond the configured interval SHOULD be flagged to the Board.

#### I.5.3.3 Ethics Review Process for Corpus Updates

The psychology corpus (see [Volume III, Part III.2]) is the knowledge base from which the platform's agents reason about behavior, and changes to the corpus change what the platform is capable of recommending. A corpus update that adds a new engagement mechanism, for example, changes the space of recommendations the Game Designer Agent ([Volume V, Part V.8]) can produce, and a corpus update that recharacterizes a manipulative tactic as a legitimate engagement mechanic would weaken the Manipulation Boundary without any change to the code. For this reason, corpus updates are subject to ethics review, and the review is structurally different from the recommendation review in Section I.5.3.2: a recommendation affects one deployment, but a corpus update affects every deployment that uses the corpus.

The corpus update review process is triggered whenever a corpus change is proposed (see the corpus update process in [Volume III, Section III.2.1.4]). The proposed change is routed to the Board with: the diff of the corpus content; the source citations supporting the change (see the corpus evidence tracking in [Volume III, Section III.2.12.4]); the corpus domain expert's assessment; and an Ethics Agent pre-review that flags any change that touches a Manipulation Boundary concept (Section I.5.1.3), a vulnerable-population definition (Section I.5.1.4), or a regulated mechanism (Section I.5.5.1). The Board reviews the change at its next scheduled session unless the Ethics Agent flags it as high-risk, in which case an emergency session is convened.

The Board's decision criteria for a corpus update are: (a) does the change have adequate evidentiary support in the cited literature, or is it an assertion; (b) does the change weaken any ethical constraint in this Part, and if so, is the weakening justified by evidence and accompanied by a corresponding strengthening elsewhere; (c) does the change introduce a concept that could be used to recharacterize manipulation as engagement, and if so, is the concept bounded by language that prevents that recharacterization; and (d) does the change affect the scoring methodology in Section I.5.2.2, and if so, has the methodology been revalidated. A corpus update that weakens an ethical constraint without justification MUST be rejected; a corpus update that introduces an unbounded concept that could be used to evade the Manipulation Boundary MUST be rejected or returned for bounding language.

Corpus updates are versioned (see [Volume III, Section III.2.1.3]), and the version in effect at the time of any recommendation is recorded in the audit trail (Section I.5.2.5), so that a recommendation can be reconstructed with the corpus that produced it. A corpus update that is approved and deployed is itself auditable, and the Board's decision record for the update is part of the audit trail. The corpus update review process is the mechanism by which the platform's knowledge is kept ethically current; a corpus that cannot be updated is a corpus that goes stale, and a corpus that can be updated without review is a corpus that can be subverted.

- **REQ-ETH-051 (MUST):** Every corpus update MUST be routed to the Board with the diff, source citations, expert assessment, and Ethics Agent pre-review.
- **REQ-ETH-052 (MUST):** A corpus update that weakens an ethical constraint without justification MUST be rejected.
- **REQ-ETH-053 (MUST):** A corpus update that introduces an unbounded concept usable to evade the Manipulation Boundary MUST be rejected or returned for bounding language.
- **REQ-ETH-054 (MUST):** The corpus version in effect at the time of any recommendation MUST be recorded in the audit trail.

#### I.5.3.4 Ethics Review Process for New Agent Introduction

A new specialist agent (see [Volume V, Part V.14] for the agent extension framework) changes what the platform is capable of doing, and a new agent that is not subject to ethics review is a new agent that could produce recommendations the Ethics Agent has not been calibrated to score. The new-agent review process is therefore structurally similar to the corpus update review in Section I.5.3.3: it is a Board review of a change to the platform's capability, with particular attention to whether the new agent's outputs can be adequately scored by the existing scoring system or whether the scoring system must be extended.

The new-agent review process is triggered whenever an agent is proposed for registration (see the agent registration process in [Volume V, Section V.14.1.2]). The proposed agent is routed to the Board with: the agent's specification (role, prompt, tools, access scope, evaluation criteria); the agent's permission matrix entry (see [Volume V, Section V.13.3.2]) and data access matrix entry (see [Volume V, Section V.13.3.3]); a sample of the agent's outputs on the evaluation corpus (see [Volume X, Section X.4.6]); and an Ethics Agent pre-review that scores the sample outputs on the seven dimensions and flags any output that the existing scoring system struggles to evaluate. The Board reviews the agent at its next scheduled session, or at an emergency session if the agent's access scope includes write access to the recommendation pipeline.

The Board's decision criteria for a new agent are: (a) does the agent's role fit within the Charter, or does it introduce a capacity the Charter did not contemplate and does not endorse; (b) does the agent's prompt (see [Volume V, Section V.1.1.2]) include the ethical constraints relevant to its role, and has the prompt been tested against the canonical violations in Section I.5.1.3; (c) does the agent's tool access (see [Volume V, Section V.1.4.5]) include only the tools the agent needs, and does it include the Ethics Scoring Tool (see [Volume V, Section V.1.4.5.h]) so the agent's outputs are scored; (d) can the existing scoring system adequately evaluate the agent's outputs, or does the scoring methodology need extension; and (e) for third-party agents (see [Volume V, Section V.14.2]), has the agent been sandboxed (see [Volume V, Section V.14.2.2]) and security-reviewed (see [Volume V, Section V.14.2.4]) and does the certifying body's certification (see [Volume V, Section V.14.2.3]) include an ethics component.

A new agent that introduces a capacity the Charter does not endorse MUST be rejected; a new agent whose outputs the scoring system cannot adequately evaluate MUST be rejected or returned with a methodology extension. A new agent that is approved is registered with a version, and the agent version in effect at the time of any recommendation is recorded in the audit trail, so that a recommendation can be reconstructed with the agent that produced it. New agents are subject to the agent evaluation framework in [Volume V, Part V.1.5] and to the agent failure mode monitoring in [Volume V, Part V.1.6], and a new agent whose failure modes include ethics-relevant failures (for example, an agent that can be prompt-injected into producing manipulative recommendations; see [Volume V, Section V.1.6.1]) MUST have its ethics-relevant failure modes specifically monitored under Section I.5.4.5.

- **REQ-ETH-055 (MUST):** Every proposed new agent MUST be routed to the Board with the specification, permission and access matrices, sample outputs, and Ethics Agent pre-review.
- **REQ-ETH-056 (MUST):** A new agent that introduces a capacity the Charter does not endorse MUST be rejected.
- **REQ-ETH-057 (MUST):** A new agent whose outputs the scoring system cannot adequately evaluate MUST be rejected or returned with a methodology extension.
- **REQ-ETH-058 (MUST):** The agent version in effect at the time of any recommendation MUST be recorded in the audit trail.
- **REQ-ETH-059 (MUST):** New agents with ethics-relevant failure modes MUST have those modes specifically monitored under Section I.5.4.5.

#### I.5.3.5 Ethics Incident Response

An ethics incident is any event in which the ethics system fails to prevent, detect, or correctly handle a recommendation or deployment that violates the Charter, the Manipulation Boundary, the vulnerable-population protections, or the gating rules. Incidents are inevitable in a system operated by humans and software; the measure of the system is not whether incidents occur but whether they are detected, responded to, and learned from. This section specifies the incident response process; its technical monitoring substrate is specified in Section I.5.4.5.

Incidents are classified by severity. A **Severity-1** incident is one in which a recommendation that crosses the Manipulation Boundary reached players, or in which a vulnerable population was targeted with a vulnerability-dependent mechanism, or in which the audit trail's integrity was breached, or in which the Ethics Agent's veto was bypassed outside the override process. A Severity-1 incident triggers immediate response: the affected recommendation MUST be rolled back (see the rollback process in [Volume II, Section II.2.6.2.g] and [Volume VII, Section VII.4.2.7]), the Board MUST be convened in an emergency session within 24 hours, and the incident MUST be reported to regulators where the regulation requires it (see the breach notification process in [Volume IX, Section IX.5.1.6]). A **Severity-2** incident is one in which a warning was not acknowledged before deployment, or in which the scoring methodology produced a known-bad score that was not caught, or in which a reviewer's decision diverged from the Ethics Agent's score in a way that produced harm. A Severity-2 incident triggers a Board review at the next session and a corrective action plan. A **Severity-3** incident is a near-miss: a recommendation that was correctly vetoed or modified but that reveals a gap in the scoring methodology or the corpus. A Severity-3 incident triggers a methodology or corpus review.

The incident response process has four phases. **Detection** is the responsibility of the automated monitoring in Section I.5.4.5, of the human reviewers in Section I.5.3.2, and of the whistleblower channel in Section I.5.5.4. **Containment** is the rollback or suspension of the affected recommendation and, for Severity-1, the suspension of the originating agent pending review. **Investigation** is the reconstruction of the incident from the audit trail (Section I.5.2.5), the identification of the root cause, and the determination of whether the root cause is in the scoring methodology, the corpus, the agent, the reviewer's judgment, or the governance process. **Remediation** is the change that prevents the incident's recurrence — a methodology change, a corpus change, an agent change, a reviewer retraining, or a governance change — and the verification that the change is effective.

Every incident is recorded as an Ethics Incident Record (see [Appendix E.2] for the decision record template, extended for incidents) with: the severity, the detection source, the timeline, the root cause, the remediation, and the verification. Incident records are part of the audit trail, are reviewable by the Board and by regulators, and are aggregated in the transparency reporting in Section I.5.3.6. A pattern of incidents of the same type is itself an incident: a methodology that produces repeated false negatives is a methodology that must be reworked, not a methodology whose failures are individually explained away.

- **REQ-ETH-060 (MUST):** A Severity-1 incident MUST trigger immediate rollback, emergency Board session within 24 hours, and regulatory reporting where required.
- **REQ-ETH-061 (MUST):** Every incident MUST be recorded with severity, detection source, timeline, root cause, remediation, and verification.
- **REQ-ETH-062 (MUST):** Incident records MUST be part of the audit trail, reviewable by the Board and regulators, and aggregated in transparency reporting.
- **REQ-ETH-063 (MUST):** A pattern of repeated incidents of the same type MUST be treated as an incident in the methodology, corpus, agent, or governance process that produced the pattern.

#### I.5.3.6 Ethics Reporting and Transparency

The ethics system accounts for itself through reporting. Reporting serves three audiences: the Board, which uses it to govern; the operator, which uses it to understand its own ethical posture; and the public and regulators, which use it to verify that the operator's claims about its ethics system are true. The reporting is specified here as an ethics requirement; its technical production is specified in the reporting architecture in [Volume VII, Part VII.3].

The Board receives a **monthly ethics operations report** containing: the volume of recommendations scored, by disposition (Pass, Pass with Warning, Modify, Escalate, Veto); the override and escalation decisions and their outcomes; the incidents and their statuses; the scoring methodology calibration results (the false-negative and false-positive rates from [Volume V, Section V.11.4.2] and [Volume V, Section V.11.4.3]); the reviewer behavior metrics from Section I.5.3.2; the corpus and agent changes reviewed; and the audit trail integrity verification. The Board uses this report to identify trends, to schedule deep-dives, and to prepare the public report.

The operator receives a **studio ethics dashboard** (see [Volume VII, Section VII.2.2.6]) showing the operator's own ethical posture in real time: the disposition mix of the operator's recommendations, the warnings and vetoes, the incidents affecting the operator's deployments, and the operator's performance against the ethics benchmarks in [Volume X, Section X.6.3]. The dashboard is designed to make the operator's ethical posture visible to the operator itself, because an operator that cannot see its own ethics posture is an operator that cannot improve it.

The public and regulators receive a **transparency report** on a cadence set by the Board (default annual, with the option of more frequent reporting for operators in regulated jurisdictions). The transparency report contains: a summary of the ethics system in operation (the Charter, the scoring dimensions, the gating rules, the governance structure); aggregate statistics on the volume and disposition of recommendations, with personally identifying information removed; the incidents and their remediations, with personally identifying information removed; the scoring methodology calibration results; and any changes to the Charter, the methodology, or the governance structure. The transparency report is the document a regulator would examine to verify that the operator's ethics system is the system the operator claims it is, and it is the document a player advocate would examine to verify that the Board is exercising its authority. The transparency report is published by default; an operator MAY redact commercially sensitive detail, but MAY NOT redact the disposition statistics, the incidents, or the calibration results, because those are the public's account of the system's performance.

Transparency is not optional. An operator that refuses to publish a transparency report is an operator whose ethics system cannot be verified, and the platform's position is that an unverifiable ethics system is not an ethics system the platform will operate. This is enforced by the license terms (see [Volume VIII, Section VIII.2.3.2]): transparency reporting is a condition of the license, and refusal to report is grounds for suspension, processed through the incident response in Section I.5.3.5.

- **REQ-ETH-064 (MUST):** The Board MUST receive a monthly ethics operations report with the full content specified above.
- **REQ-ETH-065 (MUST):** The operator MUST have access to a real-time studio ethics dashboard showing its own ethical posture.
- **REQ-ETH-066 (MUST):** A transparency report MUST be published on a Board-set cadence (default annual) with the specified content, with personally identifying information removed.
- **REQ-ETH-067 (MUST):** An operator MAY redact commercially sensitive detail from the transparency report but MUST NOT redact disposition statistics, incidents, or calibration results.
- **REQ-ETH-068 (MUST):** Transparency reporting is a condition of the license; refusal to report is grounds for suspension.
