### I.5.4 Ethics in the Architecture

The governance and scoring in Sections I.5.2 and I.5.3 are specified as requirements; this section specifies how those requirements are enforced by the architecture of the platform. The distinction between "Ethics by Architecture" and "ethics as bolt-on" is entirely a distinction about where the enforcement lives. A bolt-on ethics layer is a service that other services call if they choose to; an architectural ethics layer is a service that the data pipeline cannot bypass, that holds a position in the pipeline that no other component can route around, and whose absence is a build failure rather than a runtime warning. This section specifies the architectural position, the component, the simulation integration, the deployment gate, and the monitoring that together make the ethics system architectural.

#### I.5.4.1 Ethics Layer in the Data Pipeline

The Ethics Layer occupies a fixed position in the end-to-end data pipeline specified in [Volume II, Section II.2.1]: it sits between agent reasoning (step II.2.1.8) and human review (step II.2.1.10), and no recommendation object may traverse from the agent layer to the human review layer without passing through it. This position is enforced at the pipeline level by the data flow architecture in [Volume II, Part II.2], which does not expose a path from agent output to the review queue that bypasses the Ethics Layer. The position is not configurable; a deployment that attempts to configure a bypass MUST fail to build, and the attempt is logged.

The Ethics Layer's input is a recommendation object produced by an agent (or by the orchestrator's synthesis of multiple agents; see [Volume V, Part V.2]). The recommendation object includes the proposed change, the originating agent(s), the simulation runs on which the recommendation is based, the target population, and the predicted outcomes. The Ethics Layer's output is the recommendation object annotated with the seven-dimension score card (Section I.5.2.1), the gating disposition (Section I.5.2.3), any warnings, and the audit trail record identifier. The annotated object is what the human reviewer sees; the unannotated object is not available to the reviewer, because a reviewer who could see the unscored recommendation might be influenced by it before seeing the score.

The Ethics Layer is synchronous with respect to the recommendation pipeline: a recommendation does not proceed to the review queue until the Ethics Layer has produced its score. This is a deliberate choice. An asynchronous ethics layer would allow recommendations to queue for review before being scored, which would create pressure to score quickly or to score after the fact, both of which erode the integrity of the gate. The synchronous position means the Ethics Layer must be performant enough to score at the rate the pipeline produces recommendations, and the performance target for the Ethics Agent is specified in [Volume V, Section V.11.4] and the architecture quality attributes in [Volume II, Section II.1.4.1]. Where the Ethics Layer cannot keep up, the pipeline backpressures rather than bypassing, because a pipeline that bypasses the ethics layer under load is a pipeline that has chosen throughput over ethics.

The Ethics Layer is also the point at which the vulnerable-population flags (Section I.5.1.4) are applied to the scoring. The flags are computed upstream in the Player Model (see [Volume III, Part III.5]) and are attached to the recommendation's target population; the Ethics Layer reads the flags and applies the tightened thresholds (Section I.5.2.3) accordingly. The flags do not pass to the human reviewer in a form that identifies individual players; the reviewer sees the aggregate flag distribution (for example, "12% of the affected population is flagged for addictive tendencies") and the threshold configuration that resulted, not the identities.

- **REQ-ETH-069 (MUST):** The Ethics Layer MUST occupy a fixed position between agent reasoning and human review; no recommendation MUST traverse that boundary without passing through it.
- **REQ-ETH-070 (MUST):** A deployment that attempts to configure a bypass MUST fail to build and the attempt MUST be logged.
- **REQ-ETH-071 (MUST):** The Ethics Layer MUST be synchronous with the recommendation pipeline; under load, the pipeline MUST backpressure rather than bypass.
- **REQ-ETH-072 (MUST):** Vulnerable-population flags MUST be applied at the Ethics Layer; individual player identities MUST NOT pass to the human reviewer.

#### I.5.4.2 Ethics Agent Specification (Cross-Reference Volume V)

The Ethics Agent is the component that implements the Ethics Layer at runtime. Its full specification is in [Volume V, Part V.11]; this section cross-references that specification and states the architectural constraints that bind it. The cross-reference is necessary because the Ethics Agent is both an ethics component (specified by this Part) and an agent (specified by the agent framework in Volume V), and the constraints that make it an ethical authority are different from the constraints that make it a functional agent.

The Ethics Agent is specified in [Volume V, Section V.11.1] as having five responsibilities: ethics scoring of all recommendations, manipulation risk assessment, vulnerable population protection, compliance verification, and ethical veto authority. The first four are the scoring and gating functions specified in Section I.5.2; the fifth is the veto mechanism specified in [Volume V, Section V.11.5]. The veto authority is the architectural keystone: the Ethics Agent's veto is not a recommendation that other components may overrule; it is a gate that only the Board may override (Section I.5.2.4), and the override is itself audited and limited.

The Ethics Agent's access scope is specified in [Volume V, Section V.11.3] as full read access to the Knowledge Graph, access to the Ethics Corpus, and access to the Regulatory Compliance Database. The read access is necessary because the Agent must be able to inspect the causal chains (see [Volume III, Part III.1]) and the corpus concepts (see [Volume III, Part III.2]) that underlie a recommendation to score it; an Ethics Agent that could not read the graph would be scoring recommendations it could not understand. The Ethics Agent has no write access to the recommendation pipeline other than the annotation it produces; it cannot modify a recommendation, only score it. This separation is deliberate: an Ethics Agent that could modify recommendations would be an Ethics Agent that could weaken them, and the architecture's position is that the Ethics Agent's power is the power to refuse, not the power to rewrite.

The Ethics Agent's evaluation criteria are specified in [Volume V, Section V.11.4] and include the calibration against human ethics review that is the basis for the methodology validation in Section I.5.2.2. The Ethics Agent is itself subject to the agent failure mode monitoring in [Volume V, Part V.1.6], and the failure modes that are ethics-relevant — hallucination that produces an unjustified score, circular reasoning that produces a consistent but wrong score, and prompt injection that attempts to make the Agent score a manipulative recommendation as safe — are specifically monitored under Section I.5.4.5, because an Ethics Agent that can be subverted is an ethics system that can be subverted.

The Ethics Agent's prompt (see [Volume V, Section V.11.2.1]) includes the Ethics Charter (per REQ-ETH-001), the Manipulation Boundary and the canonical violations (Section I.5.1.3), the vulnerable-population definitions (Section I.5.1.4), the scoring methodology (Section I.5.2.2), and the gating rules (Section I.5.2.3). The prompt is versioned, and the version in effect at the time of any score is recorded in the audit trail, so that a score can be reconstructed with the prompt that produced it. Changes to the prompt are subject to the new-agent-review-equivalent process in Section I.5.3.4, because a change to the Ethics Agent's prompt is a change to how ethics is measured.

- **REQ-ETH-073 (MUST):** The Ethics Agent's veto MUST be a gate that only the Board may override, with the override itself audited and limited per Section I.5.2.4.
- **REQ-ETH-074 (MUST):** The Ethics Agent MUST have read access to the Knowledge Graph, Ethics Corpus, and Regulatory Compliance Database, and MUST NOT have write access to the recommendation pipeline other than its annotation.
- **REQ-ETH-075 (MUST):** The Ethics Agent's prompt MUST include the Charter, the Manipulation Boundary, the vulnerable-population definitions, the scoring methodology, and the gating rules.
- **REQ-ETH-076 (MUST):** The Ethics Agent's prompt version in effect at the time of any score MUST be recorded in the audit trail; prompt changes MUST be reviewed under the Section I.5.3.4 process.

#### I.5.4.3 Ethics Checks in Simulation (Cross-Reference Volume IV)

The simulation engine (see [Volume IV, Part IV.1]) is where the platform predicts the consequences of a recommendation before it reaches players, and the simulation is therefore where many ethical problems can be detected before they become recommendations. The simulation ethics checks are specified in [Volume IV, Part IV.9]; this section cross-references that specification and states the architectural constraints that bind the simulation to the ethics system.

The simulation ethics checks specified in [Volume IV, Section IV.9.3] are: ethics checks during simulation (IV.9.3.1), which evaluate the simulation's intermediate states for ethics-relevant conditions (for example, a persona crossing into compulsive spending during the simulation); simulation outcome ethics scoring (IV.9.3.2), which produces the forecasts the Ethics Agent uses to score the outcome dimensions; rejecting simulations that violate ethics constraints (IV.9.3.3), which terminates a simulation run whose outcomes cross the Manipulation Boundary or produce a vulnerable-population harm; and simulation transparency and auditability (IV.9.3.4), which ensures the simulation's ethics-relevant state is auditable.

The architectural constraint is that the simulation's outcome forecasts are the primary input to the Ethics Agent's scoring of the outcome dimensions (Retention Impact, Revenue Impact, Player Satisfaction, Long-Term Trust) and of the Fairness dimension (via the disproportionate-impact analysis in [Volume IV, Section IV.5.2.4]). A recommendation that has not been simulated MUST NOT be scored, because a score without a simulation is a score based on the Ethics Agent's prior beliefs rather than on the platform's predictive capacity, and the platform's position (see [Volume I, Section I.2.1.3]) is that simulation precedes deployment — and, by extension, precedes ethical judgment of deployment. There is a narrow exception for recommendations that are too small to simulate (for example, a copy change in a tooltip), for which the Ethics Agent MAY score on the basis of the corpus and the recommendation's content without a dedicated simulation, but the exception is narrow and the Agent MUST record that no simulation was run.

The simulation's rejection of ethics-violating outcomes (IV.9.3.3) is the earliest point at which the Manipulation Boundary is enforced. A simulation that shows a recommendation producing a whale-harvesting pattern, or targeting a distressed persona with an escape mechanic, or producing a spike-and-decay trust pattern, is terminated and the termination is reported to the Ethics Agent, which scores the recommendation accordingly. This means that many manipulative recommendations are caught at the simulation stage, before the Ethics Agent's scoring is even invoked, because the simulation itself refuses to produce the outcome the manipulative recommendation would have produced. This is "Ethics by Architecture" at its most concrete: the system's predictive model will not generate the evidence that would support the manipulative recommendation, because the system is specified to treat that evidence as a signal to refuse, not as a signal to optimize.

- **REQ-ETH-077 (MUST):** A recommendation MUST NOT be scored without a simulation, except for the narrow class of recommendations too small to simulate, for which the Ethics Agent MUST record that no simulation was run.
- **REQ-ETH-078 (MUST):** The simulation MUST terminate runs whose outcomes cross the Manipulation Boundary or produce a vulnerable-population harm, and MUST report the termination to the Ethics Agent.
- **REQ-ETH-079 (MUST):** The simulation's ethics-relevant state MUST be auditable per [Volume IV, Section IV.9.3.4].

#### I.5.4.4 Ethics Gates in Recommendation Deployment

The deployment gate is the final architectural enforcement point: it is the mechanism that ensures no recommendation reaches production without having passed the ethics system and having been approved by a human. The deployment gate is specified in the Recommendation Gate Architecture in [Volume II, Part II.2.6]; this section states the ethics-specific constraints that bind it.

The No-Direct-Production-Write Doctrine (see [Volume II, Section II.2.6.1]) is the architectural foundation: no system component writes directly to production; everything flows through recommendation. The ethics-specific extension of this doctrine is that no recommendation flows to deployment without an ethics score and a human approval, and the deployment gate (see [Volume II, Section II.2.6.3]) enforces both. The gate's state transition rules require that a recommendation in the Approved state (II.2.6.2.e) has both a passing ethics disposition (or an override recorded per Section I.5.2.4) and a recorded human approval (per REQ-ETH-007 and REQ-ETH-048); a recommendation that lacks either MUST NOT transition to Deployed (II.2.6.2.f). This is enforced at the code level by the gate's transition guards, which are specified in [Volume II, Section II.2.6.3] and which check the audit trail for the score and the approval before permitting the transition.

The gate bypass prevention controls in [Volume II, Section II.2.6.5] are ethics-relevant: a bypass of the gate is a bypass of the ethics system, and the controls that prevent bypass (code-level write prohibition, audit trail checks, separation of duties) are the controls that make the ethics system architectural rather than advisory. The emergency override process in [Volume II, Section II.2.6.6] is the one permitted exception, and it is specified as human-only, time-limited, and fully audited — and, for ethics purposes, an emergency override that bypasses the ethics gate MUST be reported to the Board as a Severity-1 incident (Section I.5.3.5), because an emergency override that bypasses ethics is an override that has chosen to accept ethics risk under time pressure, and that choice must be reviewed after the fact even if it was justified in the moment.

The deployment gate also enforces the post-deployment monitoring that the override process (Section I.5.2.4) requires. A recommendation deployed under an override MUST be tagged for enhanced monitoring (Section I.5.4.5), and the deployment gate records the tag so that the monitoring system can apply the enhanced cadence. A recommendation deployed normally is subject to the standard monitoring, which still tracks whether the predicted outcomes occurred and whether any ethics-relevant conditions emerged in production.

- **REQ-ETH-080 (MUST):** The deployment gate MUST NOT transition a recommendation to Deployed without a passing ethics disposition (or recorded override) and a recorded human approval.
- **REQ-ETH-081 (MUST):** The gate's transition guards MUST check the audit trail for the score and approval before permitting the transition to Deployed.
- **REQ-ETH-082 (MUST):** An emergency override that bypasses the ethics gate MUST be reported to the Board as a Severity-1 incident.
- **REQ-ETH-083 (MUST):** A recommendation deployed under an override MUST be tagged for enhanced monitoring, and the deployment gate MUST record the tag.

#### I.5.4.5 Automated Ethics Monitoring and Alerting

The ethics system is monitored in production by automated monitoring that detects conditions the scoring system did not predict, drift in the scoring system itself, and attempts to subvert the ethics system. The monitoring is the runtime substrate of the incident response process in Section I.5.3.5; without it, incidents would be detected only by human reviewers, and human reviewers see only what reaches the review queue. The monitoring is specified here as an ethics requirement; its technical implementation uses the observability architecture in [Volume II, Part II.7] and the alerting in [Volume II, Section II.7.4].

The monitoring has four concerns. The first is **outcome verification**: for each deployed recommendation, the monitoring tracks whether the predicted outcomes (retention, revenue, satisfaction, the behavioral features) occurred, and whether any ethics-relevant conditions emerged that the simulation did not predict. A divergence between the predicted and the actual outcomes is not itself an incident, but a pattern of divergence in a direction that would have changed the score is a methodology defect and triggers a methodology review (Section I.5.2.2). A divergence in a direction that crosses the Manipulation Boundary in production — for example, a recommendation that the simulation predicted would not produce a whale-harvesting pattern but that did produce one — is a Severity-1 incident (Section I.5.3.5) and triggers immediate rollback.

The second concern is **scoring system drift**: the monitoring tracks the Ethics Agent's score distribution over time, the calibration against human review (per [Volume V, Section V.11.4.4]), and the false-negative and false-positive rates. A drift in the score distribution (for example, a gradual increase in Pass dispositions without a corresponding change in the recommendation population) is a signal that the scoring methodology is going stale or that the corpus is drifting, and triggers a methodology or corpus review. The drift monitoring is the mechanism by which the continuous validation in REQ-ETH-027 is implemented.

The third concern is **subversion detection**: the monitoring tracks attempts to bypass the ethics system, including attempts to configure a threshold below the platform default (per REQ-ETH-031), attempts to configure the non-overridable rule in REQ-ETH-037 as overridable, attempts to route a recommendation around the Ethics Layer (per REQ-ETH-070), and patterns of override requests that suggest an attempt to normalize vetoes as overridable. Each is logged and, depending on severity, escalated to the Board. Subversion detection is the monitoring that protects the ethics system itself, because an ethics system that can be quietly subverted is an ethics system in name only.

The fourth concern is **vulnerable-population monitoring**: the monitoring tracks the size and composition of the vulnerable-population flags (Section I.5.1.4), the rate at which recommendations affect flagged populations, and the rate at which the tightened thresholds (Section I.5.2.3) are applied. A sudden increase in the flagged population (for example, a sudden increase in players flagged for addictive tendencies) is itself an ethics-relevant signal — it may indicate a change in the game's effect on its players — and triggers a Board notification even if no individual recommendation is implicated.

The alerting routes ethics-relevant alerts to the Board, to the operator's compliance function, and, for Severity-1 conditions, to the on-call response defined in [Volume II, Section II.7.4.4]. The alerting is designed to avoid alert fatigue (see [Volume II, Section II.7.4.5]) by aggregating related signals and by suppressing alerts that the monitoring can determine are benign, but the suppression itself is auditable, so that a suppressed alert that turned out to be significant is reconstructable.

- **REQ-ETH-084 (MUST):** The monitoring MUST verify predicted against actual outcomes for each deployed recommendation; a divergence that crosses the Manipulation Boundary in production MUST trigger a Severity-1 incident and immediate rollback.
- **REQ-ETH-085 (MUST):** The monitoring MUST track scoring system drift and trigger methodology or corpus review on drift.
- **REQ-ETH-086 (MUST):** The monitoring MUST detect and log attempts to bypass the ethics system, and MUST escalate based on severity.
- **REQ-ETH-087 (MUST):** The monitoring MUST track vulnerable-population flag size and composition and MUST notify the Board on sudden increases.
- **REQ-ETH-088 (MUST):** Alert suppression MUST be auditable so that suppressed alerts are reconstructable.

### I.5.5 Ethics and Law

The relationship between ethics and law is one of the most important and most misunderstood in this Part. The platform's position, stated in the Charter and operationalized in this section, is that the law is a floor, not a ceiling: the platform complies with the law in every jurisdiction where it operates, but the platform's ethical obligations do not reduce to the law's requirements, and where the law is weaker than the Charter, the Charter prevails. This is not a claim that the platform is above the law; it is a claim that the platform's ethical commitments exceed the law's minimums, and that an operator who follows the platform's ethics system is, by construction, in compliance with the law — but an operator who follows only the law is not, by construction, in compliance with the platform's ethics.

This section maps the ethics system to the major regulatory regimes, addresses the variation across jurisdictions, states the principle of ethics beyond legal minimums, and specifies the whistleblower channel that allows the platform's own people to report when the system is failing.

#### I.5.5.1 Regulatory Compliance Mapping (GDPR, CCPA, COPPA, EU AI Act, etc.)

PsyForge operates across jurisdictions with overlapping and sometimes contradictory regulation of behavioral data, automated decision-making, and AI systems. The compliance framework in [Volume IX, Part IX.5] specifies the platform's compliance with each regime in detail; this section maps the ethics system in this Part to those regimes, so that an operator can see how the ethics system contributes to regulatory compliance and where the ethics system exceeds the regulatory minimum.

**GDPR (General Data Protection Regulation).** The ethics system's consent framework (Section I.5.1.5) implements GDPR's requirements for lawful basis (see [Volume IX, Section IX.5.1.1]) and for granular consent, and exceeds them by requiring comprehension and reversibility beyond what GDPR strictly mandates. The vulnerable-population protections (Section I.5.1.4) implement GDPR's special-category protections where behavioral inferences rise to the level of special-category data (see the inferential privacy discussion in [Volume III, Section III.5.4.4]). The audit trail (Section I.5.2.5) implements GDPR's accountability principle and supports the Data Protection Impact Assessment (see [Volume IX, Section IX.5.1.2]). The right to explanation that GDPR provides for automated decision-making (Article 22) is implemented by the explainability requirement in REQ-ETH-008 and by the Ethics Agent's rationale output.

**CCPA / CPRA (California Consumer Privacy Act / California Privacy Rights Act).** The ethics system's consent and withdrawal framework (Section I.5.1.5) supports the consumer rights implemented in [Volume IX, Section IX.5.2], including the right to opt out of processing (see [Volume IX, Section IX.5.2.2]). The transparency reporting (Section I.5.3.6) supports the consumer's right to know. The ethics system exceeds CCPA's minimums by treating behavioral modeling as requiring specific consent even where CCPA would permit it under "business purpose."

**COPPA (Children's Online Privacy Protection Act).** The minor protections in Section I.5.1.4 and in [Volume VI, Part VI.3.5] implement COPPA's requirements (see [Volume IX, Section IX.5.3]) for age gating, parental consent, and data collection restrictions for under-13s. The ethics system exceeds COPPA by extending the protections to all minors (not just under-13s) for monetization modeling, and by the conservative default in REQ-ETH-014 that treats unverifiable players as minors for monetization purposes.

**EU AI Act.** The EU AI Act (see [Volume IX, Section IX.5.4]) classifies AI systems by risk and imposes obligations on high-risk systems. PsyForge's classification is addressed in [Volume IX, Section IX.5.4.1]; the ethics system in this Part implements the Act's transparency requirements (IX.5.4.2) via the explainability in REQ-ETH-008, the human oversight requirements (IX.5.4.3) via the human review in Section I.5.3.2 and the Board in Section I.5.3.1, and the post-market monitoring (IX.5.4.5) via the monitoring in Section I.5.4.5. The ethics system's documentation — the Charter, the scoring methodology, the governance structure, the audit trail, the transparency reports — is designed to serve as the conformity assessment evidence (IX.5.4.4) the Act requires, and this Part is written to be submittable as part of that evidence.

**Sector-specific and regional regulations.** The compliance framework in [Volume IX, Section IX.5.5] addresses HIPAA (for the healthcare corpus variant), SOC 2, ISO 27001, PCI DSS, and regional regulations (LGPD, PIPL, and others). The ethics system's audit trail (Section I.5.2.5) and transparency reporting (Section I.5.3.6) support the audit and reporting requirements of these regimes, and the compliance matrix in [Volume IX, Section IX.5.6] maps each regulation to the platform features that implement it, with the ethics system as a cross-cutting contributor.

- **REQ-ETH-089 (MUST):** The ethics system MUST implement the consent, accountability, and explanation requirements of GDPR, and MUST exceed them on comprehension and reversibility.
- **REQ-ETH-090 (MUST):** The minor protections MUST implement COPPA and MUST extend to all minors for monetization modeling, with the conservative default in REQ-ETH-014.
- **REQ-ETH-091 (MUST):** The ethics system's documentation MUST be sufficient to serve as EU AI Act conformity assessment evidence, including transparency, human oversight, and post-market monitoring.
- **REQ-ETH-092 (MUST):** The compliance matrix in [Volume IX, Section IX.5.6] MUST map each applicable regulation to the ethics system features that implement it.

#### I.5.5.2 Jurisdictional Variations

The ethics system is specified as a single normative framework, but it operates across jurisdictions whose laws vary in ways that affect how the framework is configured and, in some cases, how it is explained to players. This section specifies how the platform handles jurisdictional variation without permitting the variation to weaken the ethical floor.

The first principle is that the ethical floor is global. The Charter (Section I.5.1.1), the Manipulation Boundary (Section I.5.1.3), the vulnerable-population protections (Section I.5.1.4), the veto dimensions (Section I.5.2.3), and the non-overridable rule (REQ-ETH-037) are the same in every jurisdiction. A jurisdiction whose law permits what the Charter forbids does not cause the Charter to be relaxed in that jurisdiction; the platform's position is that it will not perform what the Charter forbids anywhere, and an operator who wishes to perform what the Charter forbids in a permissive jurisdiction is an operator the platform will not enable. This is the meaning of "ethics beyond legal minimums" stated in Section I.5.5.3.

The second principle is that where the law is stricter than the Charter, the law prevails. A jurisdiction whose law forbids what the Charter permits (for example, a jurisdiction that bans loot boxes entirely, where the Charter permits them subject to the Manipulation Boundary) causes the platform to operate at the stricter standard in that jurisdiction. This is implemented by the Regulatory Risk dimension (I.5.2.1.g) and the jurisdictional mapping in the compliance database (see [Volume V, Section V.11.3.4]): the Ethics Agent scores a recommendation against the laws of the jurisdictions where the recommendation would take effect, and a recommendation that is legal in the operator's home jurisdiction but illegal in a player's jurisdiction is scored as a Regulatory Risk veto for that player's population.

The third principle is that jurisdictional configuration is per-deployment, not per-recommendation. A deployment is configured with the set of jurisdictions it serves (see the deployment configuration in [Volume II, Part II.5]), and the Ethics Agent's regulatory evaluation uses that set. A recommendation that would be legal in some configured jurisdictions and illegal in others is either scoped to the legal jurisdictions (if the platform supports per-jurisdiction targeting) or vetoed (if it does not). The platform MUST NOT deploy a recommendation to a jurisdiction where it is illegal on the strength of its legality elsewhere.

The fourth principle is that jurisdictional variation in consent and transparency is handled by the consent framework (Section I.5.1.5) and the transparency reporting (Section I.5.3.6), not by varying the ethical floor. A jurisdiction that requires additional consent scopes (for example, a jurisdiction that requires explicit consent for behavioral advertising) gets those scopes added to the consent capture for players in that jurisdiction; a jurisdiction that requires additional transparency (for example, a jurisdiction that requires publication of the logic of automated decisions) gets that transparency added to the reporting for that jurisdiction. The ethical floor — what the platform will and will not do — does not vary; what varies is the legal surface through which the player is informed and from which they consent.

- **REQ-ETH-093 (MUST):** The ethical floor (Charter, Manipulation Boundary, vulnerable-population protections, veto dimensions, non-overridable rule) MUST be global and MUST NOT be relaxed in any jurisdiction.
- **REQ-ETH-094 (MUST):** Where the law is stricter than the Charter, the law MUST prevail in that jurisdiction, enforced by the Regulatory Risk dimension.
- **REQ-ETH-095 (MUST):** A recommendation MUST NOT be deployed to a jurisdiction where it is illegal on the strength of its legality elsewhere.
- **REQ-ETH-096 (MUST):** Jurisdictional variation in consent and transparency MUST be handled by varying the legal surface, not by varying the ethical floor.

#### I.5.5.3 Ethics Beyond Legal Minimums

This section states, as a normative principle, that the platform's ethical obligations exceed the law's minimums, and specifies the areas in which the platform deliberately exceeds them. The principle is not a boast; it is a commitment with consequences, because the areas in which the platform exceeds the law are areas in which the platform will refuse to do what the law permits, and an operator who expected the platform to do what the law permits in those areas will be disappointed.

The first area is **behavioral modeling of vulnerable populations**. The law in most jurisdictions permits behavioral modeling of adults, including adults with addictive tendencies, subject to consent. The platform's ethics system (Section I.5.1.4) refuses to use such modeling for monetization optimization, even where the law permits it, because the Charter's commitment to player welfare exceeds the law's commitment to operator latitude. An operator who wishes to monetize players flagged for addictive tendencies will find that the platform will not produce the recommendations that would enable it, regardless of the law.

The second area is **manipulation that the law does not forbid**. The law in most jurisdictions does not forbid the specific tactics named in the canonical violations (Section I.5.1.3), because the law tends to address specific harms after they are documented rather than to prohibit the patterns that produce them. The platform's Manipulation Boundary forbids the patterns regardless of whether the law has caught up to them, because the Autonomy Reversal Test is an ethical test, not a legal one. An operator who wishes to use a tactic the law permits but the Boundary forbids will find that the platform will not score the recommendation as passing, regardless of the law.

The third area is **transparency about the system itself**. The law in most jurisdictions does not require an operator to publish the logic of its automated decision-making, the calibration of its ethics scoring, or the incidents its ethics system has handled. The platform's transparency reporting (Section I.5.3.6) publishes these by default, because the platform's position is that an ethics system that cannot be examined is an ethics system that cannot be trusted, and the law's silence on the matter does not change that.

The fourth area is **the conservative default for unverifiable populations**. The law in most jurisdictions permits an operator to assume a player is an adult unless the operator has reason to know otherwise. The platform's conservative default (REQ-ETH-014) treats an unverifiable player as a minor for monetization modeling, because the cost of error — monetizing a minor — is asymmetric and severe, and the law's tolerance of assumption does not make the assumption ethical.

The fifth area is **the duty to refuse**. The law in most jurisdictions does not require a platform to refuse a customer's request to perform a legal but unethical optimization. The platform's position, stated in REQ-ETH-011 and operationalized in the incident queue, is that the platform will refuse such requests and will log them, because a platform that silently accedes to unethical requests is a platform that has chosen to be the instrument of those requests, and the law's permission to accede does not make the acceding ethical.

This principle has a commercial consequence: the platform will refuse some business that the law permits. This is a deliberate choice and is stated in the license terms (see [Volume VIII, Section VIII.2.3.2]). The platform's commercial model is built on the premise that studios that want ethical behavioral intelligence are the market the platform serves, and that studios that want unethical behavioral intelligence are not. The principle of ethics beyond legal minimums is the principle that defines the platform's market.

- **REQ-ETH-097 (MUST):** The platform MUST refuse to use behavioral modeling of vulnerable populations for monetization optimization even where the law permits it.
- **REQ-ETH-098 (MUST):** The platform MUST apply the Manipulation Boundary to tactics the law permits but the Boundary forbids.
- **REQ-ETH-099 (MUST):** The platform MUST publish transparency reporting that the law does not require, including scoring logic calibration and incidents.
- **REQ-ETH-100 (MUST):** The platform MUST apply the conservative default for unverifiable populations even where the law permits assumption.
- **REQ-ETH-101 (MUST):** The platform MUST refuse and log requests for legal but unethical optimizations; the law's permission does not make the optimization ethical.

#### I.5.5.4 Whistleblower and Internal Reporting

The ethics system in this Part is specified, scored, governed, and monitored, but every one of those mechanisms can fail, and the people most likely to detect the failure first are the people inside the operator or the platform who see what the mechanisms do not. The whistleblower and internal reporting channel is the mechanism by which those people can report a failure without risking the retaliation that would otherwise silence them. This section specifies the channel; its technical implementation uses the access control and audit architecture in [Volume IX, Parts IX.3 and IX.4].

The channel MUST be available to every person with access to the platform: the operator's employees, the platform's employees, the Board members, the human reviewers, and the third-party agent and corpus contributors (see [Volume V, Part V.14]). The channel MUST accept reports of: suspected Manipulation Boundary violations (Section I.5.1.3); suspected vulnerable-population targeting (Section I.5.1.4); suspected ethics system subversion (Section I.5.4.5); suspected scoring methodology errors; suspected reviewer bias (Section I.5.3.2); and any other conduct that the reporter believes violates the Charter. The channel MUST also accept reports of retaliation against a person who has made a report, because a channel that does not protect its reporters is a channel that will be silenced.

The channel MUST protect the reporter's identity. Reports MAY be made anonymously, and where the reporter identifies themselves, the identity MUST be protected from the operator and from any party other than the Board's investigation function. The channel's technical implementation MUST use the access controls in [Volume IX, Part IX.3] to restrict access to reports to the Board and to designated investigators, and the audit trail MUST record access to reports so that unauthorized access is detectable. Retaliation against a reporter is a Severity-1 incident (Section I.5.3.5) and is grounds for license suspension (see [Volume VIII, Section VIII.2.3.2]).

The channel MUST route reports to the Board, not to the operator's management, because a channel that routes to the operator's management is a channel that the operator's management can suppress. The Board MUST acknowledge receipt of every report within a configurable interval (default 5 business days), MUST investigate every report, and MUST report the outcome to the reporter where the reporter is identifiable. The investigation uses the audit trail (Section I.5.2.5) and the incident response process (Section I.5.3.5), and the outcome is recorded as an incident record if the report is substantiated.

The channel is itself subject to transparency reporting (Section I.5.3.6): the number of reports received, the number substantiated, and the actions taken are reported in aggregate, with personally identifying information removed. The reporting demonstrates to the platform's people that the channel is operative and to the public that the platform is accountable to its own people. A channel that receives no reports over a long period is not necessarily a sign that there is nothing to report; it may be a sign that the channel is not trusted, and the Board SHOULD examine a long silence as a possible indicator of a reporting failure rather than a clean record.

The channel is the final safety net of the ethics system, because the ethics system's integrity depends on the people inside it being able to say when it is wrong. A system that cannot be corrected by its own people is a system that will eventually be wrong without correction, and the platform's position is that an ethics system that cannot be reported to from inside is not an ethics system that can be trusted from outside.

- **REQ-ETH-102 (MUST):** A whistleblower channel MUST be available to every person with access to the platform, accepting anonymous reports and protecting reporter identity.
- **REQ-ETH-103 (MUST):** The channel MUST route reports to the Board, not to operator management; retaliation against a reporter MUST be a Severity-1 incident and grounds for license suspension.
- **REQ-ETH-104 (MUST):** The Board MUST acknowledge, investigate, and report the outcome of every report; substantiated reports MUST be recorded as incidents.
- **REQ-ETH-105 (MUST):** Aggregate report statistics MUST be included in transparency reporting; a long silence in reports SHOULD be examined as a possible indicator of reporting failure.
